## Day 2: The DNCT Rule

<!-- Source: Day 2 - The DNCT Rule.pdf; page 1 -->

Copywriting is not about writing. At least, not about the usual definition of writing.

At school you’ve learned that writing ‘well’ means using complex sentences and fancy words that impress your reader.

With Copywriting, it’s quite the opposite. To us Copywriters, writing well means writing clearly, and conversationally, using simple words and simple syntax...

SO your reader understands you.

So it’s not about being grammatically correct. Or using fancy words. Rather, it’s about being as close as possible to reality.

Calling a cat a cat, and not a specimen of the feline species.

This is the first rule of Copywriting.

The one you shall never forget. I call it the DNCT rule. DNCT means “Do Not Confuse Them.” And you need to keep it ona sticky note somewhere on your desk, because this is the first lesson, and where most beginner Copywriters fail.

So starting now, when you write something read it again and ask yourself:

- Does my reader understand every word I’m writing?
- Do they have to think to get what I’m saying, or are they getting it instantly?
- Is my writing concrete and easy to read?
- Is my writing emotionally compelling?
- Are my transitions logical?

If you’re not certain, here are two ways to make your writing clearer:

1. Read it out loud. When you lose track of what you’re reading.. or when you fumble a word.. this is the signal that you need to rewrite the sentence.
2. Ask someone to read your Copy. And then to tell you what they’ve understood: Who’s talking, who’s the target audience, what’s the problem, what’s the solution, what’s the big promise, etc.


<!-- Source: Day 2 - The DNCT Rule.pdf; page 2 -->

Follow a cadence

To keep the reader reading, your Copy must ‘sing’.

This means following a rhythm. And rhythm comes from tension and release.

Using a combination of short sentences and longer ones, you will create a cadence. A groove.

80% of your sentences can be medium or short. 20% long. Spread them evenly so your writing doesn’t feel repetitive.

Also, keep two similar arguments in the same paragraph. When you’ve built tension, release it with a punchline.

Just like this.

Sometimes you also have sentences that are hard to say in one breath. When you identify one, don’t use a comma. Instead, split them into 2 separate sentences using a period, or an ellipsis: “...”

When writing Copy you’re allowed to skip using commas altogether, except when you’re enumerating.

The idea is to preserve the flow of reading. And not be disturbed by unnecessary punctuation.

Delete, delete, delete...

To keep your writing clear...

Go back to every sentence and delete as many words as possible without the sentence losing its meaning.

This means deleting filler words. But also switching to the active voice whenever possible.

So a sentence like:

“I’m going to tell you about the time when I lost my car in the parking lot.”

Becomes:

“Once I lost my car ina parking lot.”

Save your reader time. Get to the point.


<!-- Source: Day 2 - The DNCT Rule.pdf; page 3 -->

For instance, my mentors always told me to delete the 6 first lines of my sales letters.

Because rookie Copywriters never start in medias res (in the middle of the action.) They explain everything first. Which isn’t necessary.

When writing your first pieces of Copy, follow this rule: Find the number of words you’ve written, and write the same letter with twice as less words. Without weakening the argument.

You will see that you always end up with more powerful Copy.

Keep it simple, stupid!

At the end of the day, keep things simple.

Effective Copy is not this mysterious thing that takes years to master. At the end of the day, it’s a simple idea brought forward through simple words.

Nothing less, nothing more.

Just remember one thing: It’s about them, not you.


<!-- Source: Day 2 - The DNCT Rule.pdf; page 4 -->

### Summary

Copywriting is about being clear, not clever.

Write conversationally, using simple words and sentences. DO NOT CONFUSE YOUR READER:

- Ask yourself: “would my reader understand it instantly? is it concrete and visual? does it flow well and are my transitions logical?”
- Read it aloud and watch out for when you ‘zone out’
- Ask someone to read it and ask basic questions: Who, What, Why, How...

Make your writing ‘flow’:

- 80% short / medium sentences, 20% long sentences
- Group similar arguments together
- Don’t abuse commas, keep one idea per sentence

Get to the point:

- Delete filler words, “kill your darlings”
- Start in the heart of the action
- Try deleting the first 6 sentences

Keep It Simple, Stupid!

And remember...

Your writing is about your reader.

Not you.


<!-- Source: Day 2 - The DNCT Rule.pdf; page 5 -->

## Day 3: Mastering Emotions

<!-- Source: Day 3 - Mastering Emotions.pdf; page 1 -->

“People buy with emotions and justify it with logic.”

You’ve probably heard this sentence before because it’s true. The brain has two hemispheres: the left ‘logical’ brain. And the right ‘emotional’ brain.

The left hemisphere controls the right side of the body and the logical processes such as speech. When you talk, it’s the left brain talking.

The right brain does not possess the ability to talk. It controls your creative thinking, your emotions, and your instincts.

Hence why it’s called the ‘reptilian’ brain. It’s the oldest part of our consciousness, and your unconscious’ headquarters.

And if you want to become a master Copywriter, able to understand people at a deep level, the first step is to understand how this dichotomy works.

To do that I’m going to tell you a quick story.

In 1848 when a man named Phineas P. Gage had a life-changing accident.

m~< aml i
- 4 ¢ iyo? | aN f , n ff of wt 4 Ta 3

This railroad foreman was packing explosive powder into a hole with an iron rod when the powder detonated.

The 3.5 feet (1.1m) iron rod passed right through his skull, destroying a chunk of his left frontal lobe in the process.

Gage survived this injury. But he did not fully recover. Because the left frontal lobe is linked with logic, speech, and reasoning... Gage lost the ability to talk and to think rationally.


<!-- Source: Day 3 - Mastering Emotions.pdf; page 2 -->

After the incident Gage became emotional, rowdy, and impulsive. And guess what? He couldn’t resist his buying impulses.

Everything he saw that tickled his fancy; he’d buy.

This led him to become an addict, to lose his money and job, and struggle for the rest of his life.

Gage’s story baffled neurologists. They found out that buying is at its core an emotional response.

Meaning that even if you THINK you buy things for certain logical reasons... You don’t.

It’s the right (reptilian) hemisphere of your brain that dictates what you want and what you don’t.

And it has even more power on you than you might expect.

There might be a second ‘You’ hiding inside your brain...

In fact, there might just be a ‘second you’ living inside of your own brain.

Let me explain.

In 1977, neuroscientists Michael Gazzaniga and Joseph LeDoux of New York University made a stunning discovery about our left and right brains.

They studied a patient named PS, who suffered an accident which left him ‘split brained.’

This means that the physical link between the 2 hemispheres (the ‘corpus callosum’) of his brain had been severed.

This left both of his hemispheres separated, without a way to communicate between them...

Now free of developing their own personal thought patterns and desires.

So of course, Gazzaniga and LeDoux tried to reach PS’ reptilian brain to ask it questions.


<!-- Source: Day 3 - Mastering Emotions.pdf; page 3 -->

At first, they couldn’t. Because remember...? Only the left hemisphere controls speech.

For instance, when asked the question of what PS wanted to do after he graduated, he said- or rather his left brain said ‘surveyor.’

Quite a boring job!

So LeDoux and Gazzaniga came up with a complex system to isolate the left brain, leaving only the right brain in command.

Since it was unable to verbally communicate, they gave it Scrabble letters it could assemble to create words.

They asked the same questions. And while the right brain shared some likes and dislikes.. It also had areas of complete disagreement.

Now hear this: when LeDoux and Gazzaniga asked the right brain what it wanted to do after graduating, it spelt out the words: A-U-T-O-M-O-B-I-L-E R-A-C-E-R.

PS’ true and hidden dream.

The thing he desired most, without even being conscious about it, cooped up in the right, emotional, brain.

The 2 neurologists concluded that:

“Each hemisphere in PS has a sense of self and each possesses its own system for subjectively evaluating current events, planning for further events, setting response priorities, and generating personal responses.”

And that’s the secret.

When you write Copy, you don’t talk to someone.

You talk to their right brain. Quite literally because they’re a person of their own.

Meaning that you mustn’t care about making things too logical as long as they’re emotionally compelling.

Your prospect has dreams, desires and aspirations buried deep in their subconscious, most of them unachieved.


<!-- Source: Day 3 - Mastering Emotions.pdf; page 4 -->

How to infuse emotions in your writing Once you know that.. How do you talk to people’s right brain?

Well this is the whole reason why Copywriting is so hard to master. Because it forces you to stop THINKING and start FEELING.

In our daily lives feeling isn’t well seen. We tell our children not to cry. We hide our tears. We don’t want to admit when we’re unwell.

We build layers of protection around our unconscious mind to protect us from shame, self-pity, desperation, and even existential dread.

Copywriting is about peeling these layers. And Quiet Copy is about doing that with subtlety. Because our emotional brain is the most vulnerable, and valuable, part of ourselves.

It is our true identity. And that’s the secret.

Identity stems from the Latin idem, meaning ‘same.’

Meaning that we can only define ourselves in comparison with others.

To know yourself, you must know who you’re not. This is why babies always put things in their mouth: to understand what they’re not.

We define ourselves in relation to others.

To talk to the right brain, you must talk about relationships.

Relationship = how they compare to others.

- How they’re going to be seen.
- What status they will get.
- How others will consider them.
- Etc.

In every good story, it’s never about what happens... But about how the characters react.

Take any good story: it will revolve around a character and their relationship with the world and others.


<!-- Source: Day 3 - Mastering Emotions.pdf; page 5 -->

Your product is the catalyst triggering a change of identity in your reader.

When you know that, it’s never about the money, the calls booked, the clients signed, the relieved pain, etc. It’s about how they see themselves. And how other people see them.

Hence why John Caples’ “They Laughed When I Sat Down At the Piano... But When I Started to Play!~” is one of the best headlines ever written.

Forget about the obvious benefits of a product. And start looking at what moves people without them even knowing.


<!-- Source: Day 3 - Mastering Emotions.pdf; page 6 -->

### Summary

Buying is an emotional response...

~So focus on making an emotionally compelling argument with your Copy.

And since we only define ourselves in relation to others, you must talk about relationships:

- How your reader is going to be seen.
- The status he will achieve.
- How your reader is going to see himself.

Don’t say how great it feels to drive a luxury car...

Say how great it feels to have other people lust over it and respect you for your success.

Your product is the catalyst triggering a change of identity in your reader.

And his identity is always tied to how he and others see him.


<!-- Source: Day 3 - Mastering Emotions.pdf; page 7 -->

## Day 4: Awareness Levels

<!-- Source: Day 4 - Awareness Levels.pdf; page 1 -->

You do not talk to different people the same way. And this is true at 2 levels of the Copywriting process:
1. When creating and marketing your product.
2. When writing your Copy.

And to help us do that, we use the ‘5 levels of awareness’ that advertising legend Eugene Schwartz came up with in the 60s.

His premise was simple: Having a great product is not enough. You must meet your Prospects where they’re at in their journey.

The awareness scale lets you assess how familiar your audience is with their problem and your solution. It helps tailor your Copy to match your audience's current state of mind. Here are the five levels and what they mean:

UANAWARE PROBLEM SOLUTION PRODUCT MOST AWARE AWARE AWARE AWARE Don’t know Know they Know Know about Know about they have a |have a there’s a your your problem (or | problem to solution to solution, product and are not solve, but their but don’t what it honest don’t know problem, know does. about it). there’s a but don’t everything solution to | know which it does or it. exactly. how well it does it.

1. An ‘unaware’ Prospect has never heard about the problem, the solution, and your product and brand. They’re the most detached from you. And the chance of them becoming a buyer is slim.

EX: A 60 year-old cattle raiser <> online copywriting business. They don’t know anything about it.


<!-- Source: Day 4 - Awareness Levels.pdf; page 2 -->

2. A ‘problem aware’ Prospect knows that there’s a challenge, opportunity, or problem, but they don’t know what the solution is, nor that your product and brand exist.

EX: A woman who just had her first child <> a weight loss supplement. For the first time in her life, she’s taken on excess weight, and she doesn’t know what to do about it.

3.A ‘solution aware’ Prospect faces a problem and has tried different solutions in the past, to no avail. They don’t know yours yet.

EX: A watch enthusiast <> your new brand of Swiss luxury watches. Here I’m taking the example of someone who’s not facing a problem per se (but the rule still applies).

4.A ‘product aware’ prospect knows about you and your product, but they’ve never purchased it.

EX: A business owner <> a tax software. At first they filed their taxes by hand, but now that their business has grown, they’re considering their options.
5. ‘Most aware’ customers are good buyers of yours. They know and appreciate you well.

EX: Many online marketers buy every new book or course from their favorite teacher because they know it’s going to give them an edge.

Each awareness level requires a different approach.

At the level of the products

A product never exists on its own, separate from a context.

You create products in relation to each other. And each one has its own place on the awareness scale.

The idea is to get people from 1 to 5: From unaware to most aware.

You structure your products according to this scale.


<!-- Source: Day 4 - Awareness Levels.pdf; page 3 -->

This is why you have ‘front-end’ products and ‘back-end’ products.

Also known as Top of Funnel (TOF) / Middle of Funnel (MOF) / Bottom of Funnel (BOF) products.

Front-end/TOF products ‘convert:’ They turn non-buyers into buyers.

They are the first point of entry for your prospects to become customers.

Back-end/MOF-BOF products ‘monetize:’ They incite buyers to spend more with you.

They build loyalty with your brand.

Now ask yourself: What are Front-end products selling? What’s the goal?

It’s NOT revenue... But a first step into your world.

Which is why you want quality volume on the front-end... And revenue on the back-end.

This changes the way you sell, whether it’s a front-end product or a back-end product.

Front-end: have a strong promise that’s ‘too good to refuse,’ the key word being ‘Abundance.’

Back-end: offer credibility and results, the key word being ‘Certainty.’

Here’s an example:

- Free book on the front-end with a $2.99 shipping fee.
- $298 upsell on the back-end.
- $12k/year coaching program at BOF.

You wouldn’t write the same copy for each.

Quick note: you want your front-end product to feel like a bargain, but you still want your prospect to spend a little amount with you.

As surprising as it may seem, there’s a world of difference in commitment between grabbing something for free and spending as little as $2.99 on something.


<!-- Source: Day 4 - Awareness Levels.pdf; page 4 -->

The first scenario builds an audience of ‘triers’ (aka leeches) while the second one builds a list of buyers.

Now here’s how to use awareness levels in your Copy.

At the level of the Copy

First, look at what type of product you’re selling, whether it’s a front-end or back-end product.

Then you must decide which awareness level you want to target.

If you’re on the front-end, with paid traffic (like ads) for example, you can address every awareness level. Inside this pool you will have people who’ve never heard about you or what you sell.. And people who know you.

You have to choose the bigger market, the one with the largest volume and hence, better potential.

If you’re on the back-end, you will only talk to clients, or qualified audiences.

So usually, no need to go the unaware route, unless you’re selling something entirely new to them (they don’t know they need it.)

Now, here are the examples from earlier and how you could go about it.

Remember: You want to meet people at their current understanding and guide them to deeper awareness and / or engagement with the subject.

1. For the unaware, introduce the issue and focus on building general awareness. Use broad, relatable contexts and stay away from technical details or specific solutions.

EX: Say you want to pitch your Copy skills to the 60 years old cattle raiser...

You could tell him how sharing his daily life on the farm and how he looks after his animals on the Internet could help his business.

It would show his customers how high-quality his meat and dairy products are and allow him to charge higher prices.


<!-- Source: Day 4 - Awareness Levels.pdf; page 5 -->

Or he could go for a direct-to-consumer business model and increase his margins.

To catch his attention, you could show him how other farmers are doing it. In other words, tell hima story.
2. For the problem aware, acknowledge the problem directly. And hit them with a ‘proclamation’ like a shocking fact to get their attention.

EX: Back to the woman who just had her first child... Here, you could tell her how Japanese women always return to their pre-pregnancy weight... Thanks to copious amounts of green tea.

You happen to sell a weight-loss supplement that combines green tea extracts with caffeine but you don’t mention that yet.

3. For the solution aware, you can tease a ‘secret.’ It’ll allow you to compare and contrast different solutions.

EX: Let’s say you’ve launched your own microbrand of luxury Swiss watches and want to market it to watch collectors... You could tease an insider secret about how most ‘luxury’ watch brands purchase cheap movements from China, barely modify them and yet claim their movements are built ‘in-house...’

~And position your watches as the more honest and sensible choice.

Or you could rave about the huge markup jewelers are making on watches. And how your direct-to-consumer model allows you to offer the same quality a lot cheaper.
4. For the product aware, a simple ‘problem-solution’ cascade will work. Identify your Prospect’s most pressing issue and follow it up with an easy, effective solution (your product.) EX: You’ re selling a tax SaaS to small business owners. Tell them how filing taxes takes too long. How complicated it is. How a minor reporting mistake can cost you $$$. And how big of a pain in the ass it is.


<!-- Source: Day 4 - Awareness Levels.pdf; page 6 -->

And offer to try your tax software for free for the next month.

5. For the most aware, a strong ‘promise’ can be enough to get them interested.

EX: You sell a Copywriting course to online marketers.

Promise them the insider knowledge you’ll reveal inside is the key to unlocking multi 6-figures launches. Back it up with proof and you’re set.

6. For your customers, a straightforward ‘offer’ will do.

EX: Your customer sees his yearly membership in your online community coming to an end.

Offer a special discount for renewal or a gift (one of your courses, a free call, etc.)

As you can see from these 6 examples, the more aware your prospect is, the more direct you should be.

Here are some more general guidelines:

- Assess your prospect’s current level of awareness before engaging with him: research and talk directly with your audience
- Make sure your first sentences confirm you’re addressing the right level of awareness
- Focus on your audience’s needs and perspective
- Use appropriate language and depth to match each level of awareness: don’t mention ‘AIDA’ to people who don’t know the difference between Copywriting and ‘copyrighting’
- Make sure the path to the next level of awareness is clearly laid out and makes sense
- Increase complexity and specificity gradually

An Intro To ‘Leads’

The goal of this course is to gradually introduce you to key concepts of effective Copywriting...

.Which is why we’re currently focusing on the ‘backbone’ of Copy instead of the ‘writing.’

So without getting into too much detail, I’d like you to understand the importance of a Copywriting ‘lead.’


<!-- Source: Day 4 - Awareness Levels.pdf; page 7 -->

A Copywriting ‘lead’ is the opening section of your ad or sales letter. Your first 200-1000 words.

Its sole purpose is to capture your reader's attention and entice them to continue reading.

An effective lead piques curiosity, addresses a pain point, or offers a compelling benefit that resonates with your target audience. It sets the tone for the rest of the copy and creates a desire to learn more.

Leads are crucial because they determine whether a reader will engage with your content or move on.

There are 6 types of leads and the book ‘Great Leads’ from Michael Masterson (Mark Ford) & John Forde will tell you everything you need to know about them.

Most Aware = ProductAware =* SolutionAware = ProblemAware = Unaware eS DIRECT INDIRECT Offer Story Leads Leads Promise Proclamation Leads Leads Problem-Solution Secret Leads Leads

And if you pay attention, you’ll notice I’ve described all 6 of them above without mentioning them while using relatable, real-life examples.

Because my goal is to give you a gradual understanding of the Copywriting process without drowning you in writing templates.

We’ll be covering a lot of ground during this 31-day training, hence why I stand by my recommendation of not reading books for now.

But once you and I are finished, I highly recommend you give ‘Great Leads’ a thorough read.

For now, simply remember that your Copy needs to address your prospects where they currently are.


<!-- Source: Day 4 - Awareness Levels.pdf; page 8 -->

### Summary

The 5 levels of awareness help you assess how familiar your Prospects are with your product.

- Unaware: They don’t know they have a problem.
- Problem aware: They know they have a problem but don't know there's a solution.
- Solution aware: They know a solution exists but don't know about your specific product.
- Product aware: They know about your product but not what it does exactly.
- Most Aware: They are your customers or know about your product and are ready to buy; they just need to know the deal.

The businesses that make the most money understand this scale and apply it to their range of products.

They structure it so their customers can move higher up the awareness scale. They solve more of their own problems and spend more money as a result.

Front-end products are Supposed to sell at high volume. Back-end products are Supposed to be highly profitable. Front-end products should feel like a bargain, but never free. You can choose which awareness level you want to target. Go for the one with the best potential for revenue.

The way you start your sales message is key: it determines whether your reader will engage with your Copy or not. Your first sentences should match where your Prospect is in his journey.

The more aware your prospect is, the more direct you should be.


<!-- Source: Day 4 - Awareness Levels.pdf; page 9 -->

## Day 5: The Cascading Effect

<!-- Source: Day 5 - The Cascading Effect.pdf; page 1 -->

Or How to Turn Ideas Into Cash

Now that you know about the 2 most important principles of Copy:

1. Writing clearly...
2. Talking to the “unconscious,” emotional brain...

It’s time to zoom out and talk about the process of turning pretty much anything into cold hard cash.

Ready? Let’s go.

First, look at this Rubik’s Cube:

This is Reality (the whole of what exists.)

And it’s a solid MESS of information with no identifiable pattern.

Everything seems random.

And this leads to unhappiness and lost dreams:

- Most people live unfulfilling lives.
- They never amount to much.
- They’ll never be remembered.

That’s where you intervene.

Your role is to solve this Rubik’s cube FOR your prospect. And they pay you to do it.

To do that you can’t create new colors, or new sides. You can’t just hit the Rubik’s cube until it breaks either.

You’ve got to play the game and turn each side until you harmonize the colors.

Each ‘turn’ of the hand being a step towards order.

That’s Copy.


<!-- Source: Day 5 - The Cascading Effect.pdf; page 2 -->

And we can in fact break down the process into 5 important ‘turns’:

1. You start with a rough idea worth sharing. 2.To refine this idea, you find its Unique Selling Proposition.
3. Once you’ve got it, you want to find its Mechanism.
4. You then find the best way to show it: a Big Idea.
5. Finally, you make it readily accessible through an Offer.

Each ‘turn’ refines the idea.

Refining means: “Making finer,” i.e. separating the wheat from the chaff.

You will hear me compare Copywriting with sculpting and mining a lot, because they follow the same path from CHAOS to ORDER.

From the ore to the jewel.

And if you go back to the 5 turns I’ve described, you’ll note the language I’m using.

I’m not saying that you should CREATE, or even GENERATE each element.

I’m saying ‘find’ them. Meaning that they already exist somewhere. And don’t worry: In the coming days we will talk about each one.

The lesson today is about the Cascading Effect.

Or how to go from this...

a | : — ..to this... a

This is the basis of Copywriting. And it works by putting your thinking in motion.

That’s the ‘cascade.’ It falls. It flows.

It requires looking at things not as a set reality, but as a process.


<!-- Source: Day 5 - The Cascading Effect.pdf; page 3 -->

This is your WHY as a Copywriter.

Your true purpose, whether you know it or not: You’re entangling reality for the rest of us, and showing us the way.

Which is why you carry a responsibility...

Because there are two forms of Copywriting: whitehat and blackhat.

Whitehat improves the world and the lives of your readers. It makes them like you, and come back for more.

Blackhat manipulates, deceives, and betrays for your own gain.

You will be tempted to go blackhat. You will be tempted to choose the left-hand path of personal accomplishment. Maybe you even have to go this way to realize it’s not the right way.

Remember that you are the sum of your actions. And that the Universe seldom forgives... and never forgets.

Persuasion and Copywriting are not neutral tools. Every sales letter you put out will have real life consequences on real people.

Choose your path carefully.


<!-- Source: Day 5 - The Cascading Effect.pdf; page 4 -->

### Summary

We as Copywriters entangle reality for our readers and show them a clear, straightforward path towards their dreams.

We turn chaos into order through a 5-step process:

1. We start with a rough idea worth sharing. 2.To refine this idea, we find its Unique Selling Proposition.
3. Once we get it, we find its Mechanism. 4.We then find the best way to show it: a Big Idea.
5. Finally, we make it readily accessible through an Offer.

Each step refines the idea and makes it more valuable and desirable.

Everything we put out can either improve our readers’ lives or deceive.

As such, we carry a responsibility and I urge you to choose your path carefully.


<!-- Source: Day 5 - The Cascading Effect.pdf; page 5 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

We’ll address the favorite buzzword of the marketing world...

‘Positioning.’

And you’ll understand why some brands make a killing under the spotlight of success while the rest scrap for breadcrumbs in the dark.

Because here’s the thing...

The most successful companies know that it’s all about how their customers perceive them...

And they know how to manufacture this perception.

For instance, they stay away from the ‘better’ trap and strive for entirely something else instead.

Which allows them to build a ‘Category of One’ where they are the only viable option. They never compete on CERES | a 2: because they know it’s a race to the bottom...

And they understand how to leverage this in-built ‘psychology bias’ to become the N°1 choice each and every time.

Tomorrow, you’l1l understand what makes these brands so successful...

And you'll learn how to apply some of these concepts to your own Copywriting career...

$0 you can stand out from the pack and make more money than you ever thought possible.

Make sure you turn up tomorrow.

Your friend,

## Day 6: Impactful Positioning

<!-- Source: Day 6 - Impactful Positioning.pdf; page 1 -->

I haven’t mentioned Positioning in the Cascade, and that’s for a reason.

Positioning is at the basis of the process. Without effective positioning, the Cascading effect will lose most of its power.

What’s positioning?

Said simply, it is your ‘unique way’ of doing something for your reader. But to achieve this, you must position yourself away from other methods.

See, positioning means differentiating yourself. It implies a position relative to other methods.

But it’s not about differentiating yourself for the sake of it. It’s about getting closer to your prospects, and what they want. It’s about listening to them and giving them what they want.

This position eliminates the common obstacles on your reader’s way towards their dream outcome.

The end goal of positioning is to create a category of one, an environment without any competition. You create yourself a niche where you’re the only one competing.

Here’s an example:

- Most Copywriters call themselves Copywriters. Thus, they’ re competing against each other.
- There’s only 1 Quiet Copywriter.

What’s the point of Quiet Copywriting? To differentiate between classic ‘loud’ Copywriting, and to resonate with people who want to sell ethically.

Now impactful positioning stems from shared values with your audience + evidence of effectiveness.

Why shared values? Because your ‘style’ will signal certain values.

Quiet Copywriting signals a more subtle, white-hat approach. Anyone who thinks the same will be drawn to it! Anyone who disagrees will not.


<!-- Source: Day 6 - Impactful Positioning.pdf; page 2 -->

You want people to choose between loving or hating you. Being ignored because you’re too vanilla is the worst option.

Now you will also want to prove your approach works, with concrete proof. Not having enough proof will make your approach seem weak or irrelevant.

Positioning works thanks to psychology: 100% of the time, people prefer something different over something better.

Most brands think, and say, they’re better than the competition. So, they compete on specifications. Features. And price. But that’s a never-ending, losing, battle. Because even if you win today, somebody might beat you tomorrow.

But when you’re different, when you evolve into a category of one, you can ask for any price. And people will buy. That’s why we don’t compete on ‘better.’ But on ‘different.’

But not any kind of ‘different.’ A kind of ‘different’ that’s better! In our mind, something different is something new. Something unique. A new opportunity. And it is seen as intrinsically better by the brain.

NEW is almost always better. Different is seen as better. Whereas ‘better’ is seen as unimportant.

This is a paradox. It’s not logical. But that’s why sales psychology is not conducted on logic, but on emotions. It’s irrational.

How do you find a different, unique positioning?

You can look outward and draw inspiration from life. Books, movies, art in general.

You can also look inward and identify your values. What do you believe in? With what morals, what aesthetics, do you identify the most? How did you go from whom you were before to whom you are now?

Of course this is not necessarily ‘YOU.’ It could be a client of yours. A person whom you represent.

Look at their hero's journey. What were the false beliefs they had to overcome to achieve success? Which personal way did they take?


<!-- Source: Day 6 - Impactful Positioning.pdf; page 3 -->

Your positioning will then determine the rest of the Cascade:

- The USP.
- The Unique Mechanism.
- The Big Idea.
- The Offer.

Now let’s take it from the beginning. You, or your client, are in the weight loss niche.

You have no positioning, no USP, no Big Idea and no offer. You just know you want to sell a diet. To begin, have a look at what has worked for you. In this case, you have lost weight through hypnosis. But you haven’t articulated anything yet. To do so, identify:

- A dream outcome: losing weight without taking it back. What hasn’t worked for you: keto, gym, counting calories.
- The ‘mental switch’ that you’ve had to experience to find your one true way: I realized my brain had the power to dictate my weight.
- Why this approach eliminates most of the common obstacles on the path to being (and staying) slim.

Once this is done, turn the whole process into a story. To do that, you must be a prophet, a messiah, not a pastor: Don’t preach to people, tell them a fascinating story.

People remember stories, not facts.

Ask yourself: “What stories can I tell so people will always automatically think of me when they have this issue?”

You will have the beginning of a strong positioning, and a good basis for the Cascading effect.


<!-- Source: Day 6 - Impactful Positioning.pdf; page 4 -->

### Summary

Positioning is at the beginning of the Cascading Effect.

Positioning is your ‘unique way’ of solving your reader’s problem.

The goal of positioning is to create a category of one where you have no competition.

Impactful positioning stems from:

1. The values you share with your audience.
2. Evidence of effectiveness.

Your values, whether explicitly stated or not, attract your ideal audience because people end up identifying with you.

You want people to love you or hate you - never ignore you.

You must prove your unique way of solving your reader’s problem works with concrete proof.

Never compete on specifications, features or price. It’s a losing battle.

Show your readers how ‘different’ you are to paradoxically become ‘better’ in their minds.

To find your unique positioning, take inspiration from life: books, movies, art.

And ask yourself:

- What are your values?
- What do you believe in?
- What morals do you identify with?
- How did you become the person you are today?
- What false beliefs did you overcome in the process?

Turn the answers into a story.

Show the way. Embody what you represent. Be a prophet.

Don’t tell people what to do or what to think. Don’t be a pastor.

People remember stories, not facts.


<!-- Source: Day 6 - Impactful Positioning.pdf; page 5 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Now that you know how to stand out in your market...

You're ready to tell it to your prospects in one short, impactful statement that leaves no room for doubt...

..YOU ARE THE ONLY SENSIBLE CHOICE.

And tomorrow, you’ll discover how to do just that.

You’ll see how ‘Choice Overload’ kills not only people’s ability to choose...

..But whole businesses too as they unknowingly become invisible to their prospects.

You’ll find a way to ‘insulate’ your business from the never-ending ‘Sophistication Cycles’ every market inevitably goes through...

..SO you can stand in a league of your own.

That attracts people that are able, willing and READY to buy from you...

And repels freeloaders that are just here to leech your time, energy and desire to do business.

## Day 7: The Unique Selling Proposition

<!-- Source: Day 7 - The Unique Selling Proposition.pdf; page 1 -->

800.

That’s the number of hotels available in May for a one-week vacation in Barcelona. You must choose one. Which one do you go with?

Turns out, having too many options kills one’s ability to choose. This is called ‘choice overload.’

And today, I’m going to tell you how to make sure you’re the ONLY sensible choice to your future clients, no matter your niche or your product, thanks to some lateral thinking.

First, let me tell you what happens in the real world, outside of Direct Response...

And why most brick-and-mortar brands die.

Take any ‘mass’ market you want:

- Restaurants
- Cars
- Fast food chains
- Hotels
- Supermarkets
- Etc

Every day they compete for your business. How? First by placing themselves on a segment of the population:

- Ferrari is owned by Fiat.
- Fiat produces low-end cars in volume. °o Ferrari produces high-end cars at a higher price.
- Bentley is owned by Volkswagen. © Volkswagen competes with Fiat. © Bentley competes with Ferrari.

And then by competing on price.

- Fiat looks to reduce its margins to allow more competitive prices, and thus more volume.
- Ferrari will look to increase its margins by increasing the perceived value of their cars.


<!-- Source: Day 7 - The Unique Selling Proposition.pdf; page 2 -->

As an individual, you fall into one of these segments. That’s why I can say with confidence that you most likely shop with the same businesses every day, no matter your preferences.

So when you’re running a brand, you’re either the cheapest or the most expensive. Anything in between loses because it competes with more brands just like it.

% of 25 70 5 offer

% of 20 70 10 demand

There is more demand than offer on the low-end and the high-end.

The middle end is so competitive, only a handful of brands win, because of too many factors such as marketing and advertising.

But despite this reality, most entrepreneurs will go with the middle end. Which is why a market grows by its middle.

Why? Because contrary to popular belief, entrepreneurs are risk averse. They don’t like losing money... Yes, even if they can earn more money later down the road.

And what segment of the market requires an investment before seeing profit? You’ve guessed it, the low-end and the high-end.

What if there was a way to circumvent this reality? And to be the ONLY evident choice, no matter the niche or the segment?

This is called the USP or Unique Selling Proposition.

USP v. Positioning

The USP is different from positioning, as positioning refers to the differentiated place of a business inside a market...

-Whereas the USP refers to the unique approach this business takes according to the state of said market.


<!-- Source: Day 7 - The Unique Selling Proposition.pdf; page 3 -->

- Positioning = set in stone, generally at the level of the business.
- USP = adapting to the market, generally at the level of the product and its offers.

If it’s not quite clear just yet, don’t worry. That’s because you first need to understand the concept of ‘Sophistication.’

When Markets Become Sophisticated

You may know that weight loss offers have been a thing since the 1800s. It’s been 200 years and people have seen just about everything about weight loss, including any promise possible.

Well, in Breakthrough Advertising, legendary Copywriter Eugene Schwartz talks about the different stages of sophistication a market goes through.

First, it’s immature, unsophisticated: Any product can work, with about the easiest claim possible.

Then, the more people see the same offers, the more the market goes up in sophistication. It becomes resistant.

And you need more force to counteract the inertia, i.e., people need more aggressive offers to buy...

Until they simply stop believing any promise made, no matter how mouth-watering.

If I tell you: “Lose 45 lbs. of pure belly fat in just under 12 days!”..

...Your first instinct would be to say, “BS!” Or “Show me the proof!” And you would be right. Chances are, I’m full of it. This natural skepticism comes from the higher sophistication of the weight loss market.

Now hear this: “Finally! Weird ‘Ice technique’ instantly and safely STOPS your dog from barking all the time, guaranteed.”

Unless you’re an expert on the matter of barking dogs, I’m sure you can’t know whether my promise is realistic or not. That’s because the market is rather small and immature.

With the rise of the Internet, data travels faster. Markets, once slow to develop, now rise and fall in the span of a few weeks.


<!-- Source: Day 7 - The Unique Selling Proposition.pdf; page 4 -->

What’s interesting to note is that sophistication follows a fractal pattern. Meaning that a market can go from immature to sophisticated many times, as every new generation resets it by inventing new techniques, media, or by expanding.

There are specific ways to ‘reset’ a market, to make it enter a new sophistication cycle.

Back in the 2010s, the rise of the VSL did that. Avant-garde Copywriters found a way to fuse a TSL (traditional sales letter) with a video. And just like that, all the saturated markets the TSL had exhausted lived a rebirth.

Sophistication cycles are hard to grasp because they’re entangled with several other moving parts, including the marketing medium. Every innovation in marketing opens a new sophistication cycle.

This is why you must keep an eye on your market. Consume the content your target audience consumes. Stay informed of the new trends. The new offers.

Always be in sync with its sophistication levels.

With that said, there is a simple way to get out of the endless cycles of sophistication, to enter your very own cycle...

It’s the USP.

The sole purpose of the USP is to ride the different cycles of sophistication with ease.

Meaning that you’re always a step ahead of the 1000s of other people on the same market with the same product.

What’s a USP?

Let’s begin with what it isn’t.


<!-- Source: Day 7 - The Unique Selling Proposition.pdf; page 5 -->

The USP isn’t:

- Branding.
- Slogans.
- Or even advertising.

Everything mentioned above implies telling people you’re ‘better’ than the competition.

But here I’m talking about having NO competition. That’s the sheer power of the USP, and why you need to apply its rules to your business and your clients’.

To find your USP, you start by differentiating it from your positioning. Positioning is at the level of the business. And businesses have niches.

- Copywriting...
- ..Becomes Quiet Copywriting.

Quiet Copywriting is a position, but not a USP.

Why?

Because its sole purpose is to define itself out of the norm, to target a specific type of person.

You’re signaling that you’re the perfect option for them.

But it doesn’t include a valuable statement or proposition just yet. Quiet Copy isn’t ‘valuable’, it doesn’t entice you to buy anything. It just signals that you’re different, and potentially a good fit.

The USP is one step closer to the sale.

Imagine we’re in front of a curious prospect who asks us exactly what we’re about.

So what do we do? Well, we’re going to formulate a Proposition. That’s a statement of intent that they can either accept or reject.

Your Proposition must follow at least 3 guidelines:

- Have a benefit.
- Be unique and/or exclusive.
- Be backed by proof or a guarantee.


<!-- Source: Day 7 - The Unique Selling Proposition.pdf; page 6 -->

Remember, we’ve talked about Quiet Copy as a positioning.

This means we’re going to get the people interested in ethical Copywriting curious about what we offer. That’s when we can formulate a USP for our product.

For instance, with the present training:

“You will get a full year of Copy experience in a single month, or I mentor you for free until you do.”

The USP can be at the level of the brand if you only have a single product.

A famous pizza place could for instance say: “Delivered to you in under 30 minutes, or you don’t pay.”

Because all they sell is pizza. They don’t sell TVs or phones. There we have a USP at the level of the brand.

But most of the time the USP is at the level of the product and its offers.

As a reminder:

- A product is a single object or service.
- An offer is a single opportunity to get said product.

This means there can be multiple offers for 1 product. And each one can have its own USP.

Having a unique promise backed by a guarantee at the level of the offer is the definition of an unbreakable USP.

Because now, let’s imagine a change in market sophistication in the Copywriting niche.

Now everybody suddenly knows about Copywriting, and we see hundreds of new courses on the subject.

Does this change anything? The USP doesn’t get any weaker.

The one thing that makes or breaks your USP is that...

...It needs to be true.


<!-- Source: Day 7 - The Unique Selling Proposition.pdf; page 7 -->

How do you find your USP?

To find your USP you must first look inwards:

- Who are you (or your client)?
- What makes you BETTER at delivering results?
- What do you know that the competition doesn’t?
- What’s your unfair advantage?

You then have to look outward:

- What is the competition doing wrong?
- How do you do it better?
- What can you guarantee that they can’t?

The idea is to dissect your business and the competition until you find the major difference between you and them.

You separate each element until you can say: Here’s what I can promise my reader that nobody else can.

Keep in mind that it’s a proposition, an offer. We are asking for our prospects’ business, and offering them a unique guarantee in exchange.


<!-- Source: Day 7 - The Unique Selling Proposition.pdf; page 8 -->

### Summary

Your Unique Selling Proposition allows you to be seen as the only evident choice.

It’s not the same as positioning.

Your positioning is your specific place in the market. Most of the time, it’s applied to your business as a whole.

Your USP is your unique way of doing things according to the market you evolve in. It applies to products and offers.

Every market inevitably goes through ‘sophistication cycles.’

The more products pop up, the more people are exposed to new claims, the more skeptical they get...

.And the more sophisticated the market becomes, which means the harder it is to stand out.

Market sophistication levels are hard to assess. Which is why you must stay up to date with your target market...

...And have a strong USP.

A USP is an offer. It’s a short, valuable statement of intent that your prospects can either accept or reject.

A strong USP has these 3 things:

- A benefit.
- Uniqueness or exclusivity.
- Proof or a guarantee.

To find your USP you must first look inwards:

- Who are you (or your client)?
- What makes you BETTER at delivering results?
- What do you know that the competition doesn’t?
- What’s your unfair advantage?

You then have to look outward:

- What is the competition doing wrong?
- How do you do it better?
- What can you guarantee that they can’t?


<!-- Source: Day 7 - The Unique Selling Proposition.pdf; page 9 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

As awesome as USPs are...

They’ re not enough to convince the most skeptical buyers.

But worry not, as tomorrow I’ll share with you another way you can make your dent in the most sophisticated markets.

Enter ‘Unique Mechanisms’, a.k.a. the ‘HOW’ behind your promise.

Because as much as you must appeal to the emotional side of your reader, you still need to make sense on the logical plane...

And I’ll show you how to do just that tomorrow.

You’ll discover my 4-step method to take your readers from UNAWARE to MOST AWARE in one, single sales letter...

Effectively leading them inside a ‘Logical Maze’ that makes the ‘Buy Now’ button the only sensible exit.

You’ll uncover the counterintuitive reason why explaining MORE about your product is the way to go...

And how you can immunize your Copy from the lethal tone of “trust me bro, it works!” that plagues too many sales letters...

~All without making bold claims.

As a result, you’ll be giving your readers new hope for old problems...

And make them thrilled about buying from you.

Your friend,

[Handwritten signature]

## Day 8: Unique Mechanisms

<!-- Source: Day 8 - Unique Mechanisms.pdf; page 1 -->

The more a market becomes aware and sophisticated, the more different, precise, and truthful your offer must be.

This means that you can get away with lazy Copy in a small market, but need to be different, unique, and specific in larger ones as skepticism grows.

This is why Copywriters came up with the concept of Unique Mechanisms.

To ‘get’ unique mechanisms, you must first understand its meaning.

A mechanism is:

a. A system of parts working together in a machine. b. A natural or established process by which something takes place or is brought about.

Both definitions here are important.

First because you must view the whole offer-copy process as a machine.

What does a machine do? Usually, it transforms raw materials into well-designed objects. It’s a vector of transformation.

For instance:

1. First, you have raw steel sheets with close to no value. 2.Then it goes into the machine.
3. And you get knives, which are valuable...
4. Because people can cut their food now!

Here you see that order is what brings value.

You can’t sell sand for much unless you deliver huge amounts of it. But you can heat up the sand, blow glass, and create glass pitchers.

As your market gets more sophisticated and aware, you will have to share HOW your product works. You can’t just say: “Trust me, it works.”

In fact, you will have to explain everything about it.


<!-- Source: Day 8 - Unique Mechanisms.pdf; page 2 -->

Let’s say they have back problems, and that it’s the only thing your readers know right now. But they don’t know why, nor how to fix it.

You must explain:

1. Why the usual explanations of the problem are incorrect.

“Doctors may have told you that back pain is inevitable as you age, because the muscles and discs of the back get older and weaker...”

2.Why, then, the current solutions based on this first erroneous explanation do NOT work.

“That’s why doctors recommend you take NSAIDs and painkillers. But of course, this doesn’t work! These medications only hide the pain, without treating the root cause of back pain.”

3. The REAL explanation behind their back pain.

“What nobody tells you is that the real cause of back pain is this little protein contained in this toxic food...”

4. The REAL solution to finally solve their back pain issue.

“This plant found only in the Amazon rainforest contains an enzyme capable of dissolving this protein, liberating you from years of suffering...”

By doing this you block all the potential logical exits. Your reader’s brain gets trapped into a logical maze where the only exit is the buy button.

Of course, it must be true! You can’t make things up just like I did in the above example.

The good thing, at least with health, is that most doctors have no clue about the real causes of illnesses.

So, what’s the Mechanism?

Well, that’s exactly what I’ve highlighted above.

A mechanism is the HOW behind the promise. The mechanistic and scientific explanation.


<!-- Source: Day 8 - Unique Mechanisms.pdf; page 3 -->

Here’s an example from a Clickbank VSL, where the Unique Mechanism is underlined:

“They told you that to lose weight you had to go to the GYM, restrict calories, and diet.

That’s wrong. And it’s not your fault. You were lied to. The real problem is that you don’t have enough brown fat. You have white fat.

Brown fat is a heat-generating fat: it makes you LOSE weight. Whereas white fat is the ungracious belly fat.

That’s why diet and exercise don’t work. They don’t create brown fat.

The actual solution is THIS plant that creates brown fat in your body.

It converts your white fat (the one you don’t like) into fat-burning brown fat (that key to losing weight)...”

By following this order...

1. False explanation of the problem.
2. False solutions.
3. Real mechanism of the problem.
4. Real mechanism of the solution.

You effectively take people from UNAWARE to MOST AWARE in a single sales letter.

The real work is in keeping everything legit. Which is where Research becomes crucial.

Every single sales letter can follow this logic. And when it’s solid, you don’t have to claim anything.

You have presented the proof. And blocked all other possibilities. Debunked other solutions as misleading or false...

And proven that yours is the only right way.

Giving them new hope. A new way to look at an old problem.


<!-- Source: Day 8 - Unique Mechanisms.pdf; page 4 -->

### Summary

The higher the awareness and sophistication in a market, the more specific, different and truthful your offer must be.

Having a Unique Mechanism is the best way to do that.

It transforms your product and orders it to bring out the value.

Here’s the 4-step process:

1. Give the false explanation of the problem:

Why are the usual explanations of the problem incorrect?

2.Go over the false solutions:

What are the common solutions to this problem? And why are they useless?

3. Now give your reader the real mechanism of the problem:

What are the real reasons behind the problem? And why does it work like that in reality?

4. And finish with the real mechanism of the solution:

What’s the REAL solution to finally solve this problem? How does it work?

This allows you to take your reader by the hand and guide them exactly where you want them to.

You block all potential logical exits along the way and make sure that buying your offer is the right thing for them to do.


<!-- Source: Day 8 - Unique Mechanisms.pdf; page 5 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

You’re ready to tackle the biggest mover for persuasive Copy...

Big Ideas.

Unfortunately, Big Ideas have become this nebulous, almost mystical concept that no one seems to really ‘get.’

But that changes tomorrow, because you'll be receiving the clearest definition of Big Ideas to date.

You’ll discover 7 triggers & 7 questions to help you find your next Big Idea...

.That’ll spark ‘Brain Orgasms’ in your reader’s mind and turn your product into a must-have.

You'll uncover the sad truth behind why most ‘Big Ideas’ fail..

And how to make sure yours doesn’t.

And among other things, I’ll show you how you can make your offer feel both exclusive and accessible at the same time.

If you thought that Big Ideas had an esoteric process behind them, you’re in for an awakening.

Make sure you study tomorrow’s lesson thoroughly.

Because if there’s one thing that’ll make you an A-List Copywriter, it’s your ability to find Big Ideas.

Your friend,

## Day 9: Identifying Big Ideas

<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 1 -->

What’s a Big Idea? , ‘

The Big Idea is a complex entity...

And finding one is not easy... But being good at it can propel you to the Mount Olympus of A-List Copywriters.

Because Big Ideas are the backbone of legendary Copy. The one that’s remembered years after it was published. The one that can influence a market as a whole...

~And build fabulous businesses and fortunes as a result.

So, what’s a Big Idea?

Simply, I call it “the main angle of a piece of Copy.” But there’s more to it.

First, here’s what it isn’t.

A Big Idea is not just a headline, a hook, or a benefit.

It’s definitely not a boring statement that reads like a business card turned into an ad...

A Big Idea is the concrete articulation of the Positioning, the USP, and the Mechanism. It is the end of the Cascade. It’s how you make them all intelligible to your reader.

A Big Idea is the ‘subconscious’ articulation of why your reader should buy your product.

And it’s the very reason why it connects so well with your reader...

Because this idea, this image, this ‘gut feeling...’ has been sitting in his mind for a long time... And you just happen to articulate it with words that trigger an emotional response, and relate it to your solution.

If you feel like you still don’t quite get it, stick with me because everything’s about to make sense in a minute.

But first, why should you care about having a Big Idea for your campaign?


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 2 -->

This is a good question. After all, plenty of sales letters have been successful enough without a Big Idea.

So if you’re fine with ‘good enough,’ be my guest and skip the process I'll be sharing with you today.

But if you want to hit an absolute homerun...

To rake in millions (or even billions) in sales...

And establish your own control-Copy for years to come...

Pay attention to this lesson.

And know this: no matter what you’re selling, a Big Idea will inevitably boost your sales. But it certainly is more important in the more sophisticated niches, like finance and health.

People don’t wake up and think “I really need to subscribe to another financial newsletter...”

...Hence why you need a stronger force to pull them in.

A Big Idea lets you do just that. It allows you to introduce a problem in a more urgent way, or offer an opportunity that feels 10 times better. It makes you stand out from competitors still stuck on the ‘claims bandwagon...’

Because it has an emotionally-compelling drive to it.

Take for instance the notorious “End of America” promo written by Mike Palmer that’s included in today’s swipes...

As you will see - and without spoiling you too much, the letter explains how the US dollar could lose its global status as the world reserve currency...

But what does that mean exactly?

It means that everything about a normal way of life would change. The way people travel, shop, save, take care of their family and even retire...

So it would be the “End of America” as we know it, which is something everyone can imagine in their minds in some way.


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 3 -->

It’s both widely appealing and oddly specific at the same time, because everyone has their own vision of it..

...Which makes it far more powerful and real than anything else you could say.

The reason a Big Idea is so powerful is because it solves the 5 problems every business has:

- It gets people’s attention.
- It gets them interested.
- It gets them excited.
- It gets them to take action.
- It gets them to remember you.

Most of the time, a Big Idea takes the form of a singular opportunity that’s intrinsically desirable.

It’s exciting. It makes you go “wow!” when you see it. And you want to know more about it.

And this is why you do not create Big Ideas. They already exist. They’re a real phenomenon. You discover them or make them happen in real life.

Some Copywriters say that you create or invent them. But this is a falsehood. I’ll show you how you can look for them in the second part of this training.

For now, here’s how you know you have a Big Idea and not just another ‘cool fact’ that’s ‘nice to know:’

1. Your idea is unique: It's A Big Idea is: different from what your @ unique competition is saying. It e ‘ 1 offers a new, fresh Singular perspective on your @ Exciting product. @ Desirable
- Valuable
2. Your idea is relevant: It en resonates directly with bd i your reader’s specific @ Exclusive desires, @ Uncommon pain points, or @ Time sensitive (sometimes) motnvat ions. @ Reliable/Predictable


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 4 -->

3. Your idea is simple: You can say it in a clear, concise and easy-to-grasp manner. You ‘get it’ straight away and you understand the benefit.

4. Your idea is emotionally appealing: It has an implied benefit, it creates a sense of desire, urgency or curiosity and drives your reader to take action.

5. Your idea is memorable: It sticks, it leaves a lasting impression in your reader’s mind long after they’ve read your Copy.

But here’s the thing: a Big Idea doesn't mean anything if it's not real. It must be based on reality. Otherwise, it’s a mere unproven claim. And everybody can make one up.

A Big Idea should appeal to almost everyone. It has a universal quality because it works on basic human psychology.

It saves people from reality and offers them the opportunity to experience a more ‘magical’ state of being.

“Legally Rob Banks” refers to something everyone would do if they could. Here it promises a great reward without its logical consequences. Thus, provoking a sort of ‘brain orgasm.’

You like the idea as soon as you hear it.

Now, the best way to understand Big Ideas is to study them.

Take the 4-hour Workweek from Tim Ferriss...

Here’s the premise of the book:

“Whether your dream is escaping the rat race, experiencing high-end world travel, earning a monthly five-figure income with zero management, or just living more and working less, this book is the blueprint.”

The second half of the book title summarizes it:

“Escape the 9-5, Live Anywhere And Join The New Rich.”

But the first half tells you everything you need to know:

“The 4-Hour Workweek.”


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 5 -->

You ‘get it’ because you intuitively understand that someone who works only 4 hours a week has found some sort of hack.

That number is so low that it counterintuitively makes it believable. If Ferriss offered you a 15-hour workweek, you'd think “ah, he’s just being frugal...”

But 4 hours? There has to be something that you don’t know!

Here’s why it qualifies as a Big Idea:

- It’s unique: Nobody had heard of a 4-hour workweek before 2007.

W@W It’s relevant: 99% of the population dreams of working as little as possible.

- It’s simple: You get the idea straight away in only 4 words.

- It’s emotionally appealing: The book would never have sold as many copies if it were named “The Ultimate Guide To Lifestyle Design” or “How To Outsource Everything So You Only Work 4 Hours A Week.”

{4 It’s memorable: People are still talking about it today.

Now here’s the thing. A Big Idea is not just a collection of criteria to fill. It’s more subtle than this.

Let’s see another example...

“There’s A New Railroad Across America, And It’s Making Some People Very Rich...”

In this 1999 sales letter, Porter Stansberry - a financial publishing company, uses the railroad analogy to promote its newsletter.

The letter tells you how the Internet and its new fiber optic network will change the way people communicate forever. It tells you why it’s a ‘disruptive innovation’ and why spotting this kind of trend is the key to making a fortune investing.

And how the newsletter will help you do just that.


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 6 -->

The railroad analogy is there to pull you in. It’s genius because it perfectly fits the opportunity 'visually.’ A network of cables makes you think of a railroad network...

...But that’s the icing on the cake.

Because ‘A New Railroad’ is a simple idea that carries a lot of deeper, intuitive meaning...

First of all, you know what railroads are. You see them everyday. Which gives you a subconscious feeling of ‘familiarity.’ And what’s familiar feels safe.

And because most were built decades ago, they represent long-term infrastructure investments. So there’s this subconscious feeling of ‘certainty’ that every investor loves...

Secondly, you may not know the details, but you know that railroads are historically significant. You know they’ ve played a crucial role in economic development. So you subconsciously think there’s a similar transformative potential.

Railroads have fostered the movement of people and ideas. So there’s this feeling of ‘innovation’ as well.

Thirdly, you know that railroads have made some people insanely rich. Without knowing the details, you’re certainly familiar with names like John D. Rockefeller, Andrew Carnegie, J.P. Morgan, Cornelius Vanderbilt or Meyer Guggenhein...

Some of them were first generation immigrants who became billionaires in just a few years because they were able to spot trends early on. And you have a vague idea of it, which is enough.

Lastly, you may have never heard of ‘network effects,’ but you instinctively know that railroads are only profitable when people use them. So there’s this subconscious feeling of ‘exponential return on investment’ attached to it.

Do you see how a single idea can trigger all of these preconceptions?

And how it can drive you to read the rest of the letter?

Let’s see another one before we get to the process itself..


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 7 -->

“At 60 miles an hour the loudest noise in this new Rolls-Royce comes from the electric clock.”

This one from David Ogilvy is a masterclass. It tells you so much about the car, the brand’s values and the experience of owning a Rolls-Royce in one simple, factual statement.

First, the headline makes you want to read more. It’s intriguing, specific and unexpected.

Second, this one selling point tells you 2 things about the car, without making a single claim:

1. The car is quiet: Instead of saying it, the ad shows it using a specific detail. Keep in mind engines were a lot louder in those days! 2.The car is high-end: Without stating it, the headline implies extreme luxury and quality. If the loudest noise is just a clock, the car must be incredibly well-made...

And because it reads like a technical observation, it makes it more credible than a vague, generic claim about quality.

Third, it conveys the values of the brand. Roll-Royce stands for ‘understated elegance.’ They’re not shouting about how great their car is. They’re calmly stating an impressive fact that speaks for itself.

Fourth, this is storytelling in one line. It paints a picture of what it feels like to drive a Rolls-Royce. You can imagine yourself pressing the foot pedal and not hearing a thing.

As an added bonus, it appeals to their target audience: wealthy, hard-pressed businessmen who want to shut themselves from the outside world and have some quiet time.

Lastly, the headline is memorable because it’s such an unusual comparison.

David Ogilvy had a 5-question filter for Big Ideas:

1. Did it make me gasp when I first saw it?
2. Does it fit the strategy to perfection? 3.Is it unique?
4. Do I wish I had thought of it myself?
5. Could it be used for 30 years?


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 8 -->

He’s also notorious for saying he’s only had about 20 Big Ideas throughout his career...

...Which isn’t much, but certainly enough to make him one of the most influential advertising man of the 20th century.

Now this 5-question filter is fine for assessing whether your idea is Big or not...

But how do you go about finding one?

How to Find Big Ideas

Big Ideas are the result of an alchemical process. You never create them. You find them. You identify them in time.

And there are 3 main steps to it:

- Step 1: Get it in
- Step 2: Get it out
- Step 3: Let it rest
- Step 4: Assess it

The 1st step is research. And lots of it. Because you need the fuel, the raw material, to engage with the process.

The 2nd step is to write everything down. You can do it during your research, of course. It’ll help you separate the wheat from the chaff and make everything make sense.

The 3rd is to step away from all of it and let your subconscious do the work. At this stage, your goal isn’t about finding your Big Idea...

...It’s about recognizing it in time.

And once you think you’ve done it, you can go on with the 4th and final step and assess it.

Let’s get into more detail.

- Step 1: Research

You need to be consuming as much information as possible. Some of it will seem relevant, some of it less so. And that’s ok.


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 9 -->

You may land in the most obscure places during this phase. Forgotten forums that feel like a deserted town and unassuming websites that feel like gold mines.

You must always trust in serendipity and believe that you can find a winning Big Idea.

Note that research is a big part of writing a sales letter. We’ll have a whole lesson dedicated to it so I don’t want to get ahead of what’s in store for you.

But as part of this lesson, here are some practical steps for researching your Big Idea so you can visualize the process:

- Research your reader: Desires, pain points, perceptions, motivations, aspirations, frustrations. Talk to them if possible.
- Research your competition: Watch what they say and do, and how they say and do it. Look for weaknesses, gaps and opportunities. Can you provide a unique perspective?
- Research your product: Know its core benefits by heart and how it fulfills its promise. Talk to the founders and listen closely.

The reason I recommend lots of research is simple. It gives you a baseline to look for the odd. And odd things can be good contenders for a Big Idea.

As an example, David Ogilvy got his idea for the Rolls-Royce campaign from a casual talk with one of the engineers.

And it’s only because Ogilvy knew the car so well that this odd comparison stood out to him.

- Step 2: Get it out.

Now that you’ve filled your brain to the brim with information, it’s time to put it all out.

You should be taking notes during your research but this step takes it further. It’s about ‘working’ with your notes like a sculptor plays with clay.

Study your notes, see what connects, look for what sticks out to you now that you have a broader understanding.


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 10 -->

And once you have a few candidates for Big Ideas, ‘speak’ them into existence. Say them out loud. See if they make sense, if they’ re exciting.

Tell a friend and see if they get it, if they get excited. or ideally, tell a fellow Copywriter. I highly recommend having someone to bounce your ideas off.

Work with your ‘Big Idea candidates’ for a bit.

Ask yourself lots of questions and dig deep. Questions like:

- What’s the benefit of the benefit?
- What would happen if I exaggerate the benefits?
- What would happen if my prospect doesn’t use my product?
- What’s the worst thing that can happen if he doesn’t?
- How can you creatively showcase your product? (think Blendtec’s “Will It Blend?”)

These questions are mostly there to get your creative juices flowing...

Because from my experience, it’s unlikely that you’ll find your Big Idea at this stage.

You can definitely get a positive feeling for it but you won’t be sure while you’re straining at your desk.

You need to step away for a bit.

- Step 3: Leave your work aside.

The confirmation will hit you when you least expect it. During a walk, in the shower, in bed before you fall asleep, or in the middle of a conversation...

That’s because you’re ‘detached’ from it.

Your subconscious mind does its best work in a relaxed state. You feed it all the data points so it can work on its own.

This is why geniuses from all ages were big on taking lonely walks in nature to let their minds wander uninterrupted.

But that’s not very helpful now, is it?

Worry not, as I’m not about to leave it all to chance...


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 11 -->

Like I’ve told you earlier, Big Ideas are the result of an alchemical process, where the context and the setting make them exist.

The process looks like this...

Trigger Big Idea Copywriter

The ‘Trigger’ is a random event that happens...

...That you, the Copywriter, notice. You see an opportunity.

And you use it to ‘rearrange reality’ ina way that shows the hidden benefit. This ‘arrangement’ is your Big Idea.

The Trigger is the foundation for your Big Idea. It has no distinct benefit yet. It’s just there, on its own.

You simply notice it, and you link it to the product you’re selling in a way that’s beneficial to your reader.

That’s what I mean when I say that you ‘rearrange reality.’

Your Big Idea is how you showcase this inherent (and overlooked) benefit.

A trigger can be:

- An event

EX: Analysts claim that the latest presidential decree will send green energy stocks through the roof.


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 12 -->

- A feature

EX: You skim a car’s driver manual and notice a small detail that makes all the difference.

- A discovery

EX: Spanish scientists discover a molecule that makes DNA repair itself.

- The product itself

EX: By taking advantage of a legal loophole, your method can cut anyone’s taxes by 50%.

- The offer

EX: You're a real estate agent and you offer to sell any house or you buy it yourself.

- An anecdote

EX: At your local golf course, there’s a one-legged man who can beat anyone - even the pros.

- A story

EX: You discover this story about a young child from New Delhi who’s found a way to speak 7 languages fluently and ended up teaching it to the wealthy.

- A benefit

EX: That one ‘boring’ ingredient inside your kitchen actually cures diabetes!

Now the reason I’m telling you about Triggers is simple.

An idea is only Big when the market Says so. In other words, you’ve made lots of sales for a while and no other angle beats yours.

Because you can think you have a Big Idea, you can believe in it, but the market is the sole decision-maker.

You can use David Ogilvy’s Big Idea filter. or you can use mine, which is similar, on the next page.


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 13 -->

- Is it an exciting, new opportunity?
- Is it naturally desirable?
- Is it specific and unique?
- Is it linked to an event?
- Is it true and real?
- Is it predictable?
- Is it exclusive?

If I can confidently answer ‘Yes’ to all of these questions, I know I’ve got myself a potential winner.

If not, I refine it until I do.

In any case, finding a Big Idea is never easy...

~-But when you know what to pay attention to, a.k.a. Triggers...

...When you know what a Big Idea looks like and feels like..

...When you can assess it...

...And when you practice...

You get better at it.

And with time, you too can join the ranks of A-List Copywriters who command premiums for killer sales pages.


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 14 -->

### Summary

A Big Idea is the main angle of your Copy. It connects emotionally with your reader. You find it, you don’t create it.

It articulates the positioning, USP, and mechanism.

A Big Idea is:

- Unique
- Relevant
- Simple
- Emotionally appealing
- Memorable

A Big Idea can significantly boost sales. It is crucial in more sophisticated niches.

Follow these 4 steps to find your next Big Idea:

1. Research
2. Process the information
3. Leave it aside and let your subconscious work
4. Assess your idea

Look out for these Triggers to find your next Big Idea:

- Events
- Features
- Discoveries
- Stories
- Product itself
- Offer
- Anecdotes
- Benefits

Use these questions to assess your Big Idea:

- Is it an exciting, new opportunity?
- Is it naturally desirable?
- Is it specific and unique?
- Is it linked to an event?
- Is it true and real?
- Is it predictable?
- Is it exclusive?


<!-- Source: Day 9 - Identifying Big Ideas.pdf; page 15 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Tomorrow’s lesson will show you the simplest way to make sure your prospect reads your Copy.

So simple that too many beginners overlook this yet essential component. ‘Digital writers’ love to yap about how you must be ‘concise’ and ‘clear not clever’ but when it comes to it..?

...They beat around the bush like a politician at a press conference!

It takes them forever to address the number 1 concern of their reader...

“..What’s In It For Me?”

No wonder nobody opens their emails and their sales pages have a click-through-rate below 1%..

Drill this into your head: people care about themselves first and foremost...

So unless you tell them exactly what to expect, or use what I call an ‘Ethical Bribe,’ you’ll share a similar fate.

Thankfully, you get to be part of this training and discover my most effective methods...

...Like the ‘10-Line Rule’ that lets you hook readers from the very start...

Or the ‘Bridge’ technique that transforms prospects into buyers in one single sales letter...

Or the 4 Questions I like to answer every time I write.

When you implement these methods, you’ll earn your stripes as a writer that people actually read from start to finish.

Stay tuned. And look up today’s exercise - you’ll enjoy it.

Your friend,
