## Day 10: The Big Promise

<!-- Source: Day 10 - The Big Promise.pdf; page 1 -->

Now that you have figured out all the abstract movements of the Cascade, from the Positioning and the USP, to the Unique Mechanism and the Big Idea...

...It’s time to transform them into a concrete sales letter.

And to do that, your first task is to figure out your Big Promise.

The Big Promise represents the TRANSFORMATION behind your sales page.

Imagine a bridge that takes you from one bank to the other.

On the first one, you’ve got your prospect stuck in a problem state.

You are promising them the trip from one bank to the other

**PROBLEM → SOLUTION → DREAM**

As we’ve seen, this problem state can be aware or less aware.

No matter your awareness level, you will have to promise them they’ll soon be on the other side of the river, where their dream is waiting for them.


<!-- Source: Day 10 - The Big Promise.pdf; page 2 -->

The Big Promise is the transformation through the solution. And it can have two forms:

1. A positive promise. 2.A negative promise.

The positive promise gets you somewhere you want to be:

“2 Hour Agency is a counterintuitive approach to online business, specifically the digital agency business model, that allows you to build a sustainable long term agency that generates new clients, and delivers your service to them largely without your presence or attention.”

The negative promise prevents you from going somewhere you don’t want to be:

“As of right now, the next time you leave the safety of your house you are facing serious bad news.. unless you understand the secrets of defeating sudden, extreme violence!”

The first one fulfills your dream.

The second protects you from tragedy.

Sounds simple enough?

It should. But in reality, the FIRST problem Copywriters encounter is the lack of a clear “What’s In It For Me?”

They start their Copy without stating the purpose of their intrusion.

Remember: whenever you’re sending an email, writing an ad, or a sales letter...

...You must state why you’re interrupting their day and what they get by reading you.

The best way to ensure you’ve done it correctly is to insert an ‘Ethical Bribe’ in the first lines of your Copy.

An ethical bribe is a teaser of what they are going to get by reading your letter in full.


<!-- Source: Day 10 - The Big Promise.pdf; page 3 -->

“That’s what makes what I’m about to show you so incredibly exciting. Because in all my years on the cutting edge of global energy.. I’ve literally never seen a profit opportunity like this unfold. Not even remotely.”

So remember: Start with a strong and clear promise. It must appear within the first 10 lines of your Copy.

You reader must instantly know:

- Why you’re addressing them today.
- What your proposition is (at least tease it).
- What’s in it for them.
- And why they should read this letter, or watch this video, until the end!


<!-- Source: Day 10 - The Big Promise.pdf; page 4 -->

### Summary

Your Big Promise is the transformation you're offering to your prospect, moving them from their current problem state to their dream state.

It can be framed in the positive, and get them to where they want to be...

Or it can be framed in the negative, and prevent them from ending up where they don’t want to be.

Your Big Promise:

- Serves as an ‘Ethical Bribe’ to keep reading
- Clearly states (or teases) the purpose of your Copy
- Must appear within your first 10 lines

Your reader must know immediately:

- Why you’re addressing them today
- What you’re offering (or at least a teaser)
- What’s in it for them
- Why they should continue reading (or watching)

Make sure your Big Promise is compelling and obvious.

If you’re teasing it, make sure it’s intriguing.

Not having a Big Promise in your Copy is missing your opportunity to hook your reader!


<!-- Source: Day 10 - The Big Promise.pdf; page 5 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Before writing their first sales letter, junior Copywriters inevitably make one of these two mistakes:

1. Not enough research
2. Too much research

The first half dashes into writing a sales letter too soon and completely misses the mark...

While the second half goes around in circles only to come out more confused than ever...

But now they’re out of time so they rush their writing.

No wonder their Copy reads like the ramblings of a madman.

Fortunately, tomorrow’s lesson will give you a process for ‘Preparing Your Copy’ so you don’t end up like this.

Like the ‘WHO-WHY-WHAT-HOW’ formula that turns a mediocre mess into coherent Copy that converts...

Or how you can address thousands of people and still make your reader feel like he’s ‘The Only One...’

And other techniques, like the ‘Mother Test’ that not only makes templates obsolete...

But unlocks your most genuine Copy that feels like a heartfelt conversation instead of a sales pitch.

And keep in mind we’re not even getting into the research phase yet...

We’re just Preparing The Copy...

I’ve got lots of exciting things in store for you.

Your friend,

## Day 11: Preparing the Copy

<!-- Source: Day 11 - Preparing the Copy.pdf; page 1 -->

There are 4 things you need to know before conducting your research and writing a single line of Copy.

First is the ‘WHO:’ Who are you talking to? You must understand your market.

To do that you can create a persona - an avatar of your ideal customer. Their age. Their gender. Their values, worldviews, principles.

Remember you’re writing to thousands of people, so don’t get too specific. Focus more on what they believe, rather than who they are. You must know how they see the world. Because you will then confirm their worldviews in your Copy.

It’s much more difficult to convince someone to change their perspective on things than to confirm it.

Then you must also know the author: WHO is talking?

Who signs at the end of the emails or sales letter? Are you the author, or is it someone else? You’ll need to know almost everything about this person: Their life, achievements, beliefs, etc. Amongst these, you must also discover their ethos.

The ethos is the image a person projects when they talk, or act in general. How people see them and how they want to be seen.

Here’s the example of someone who’s in the alternative medicine niche. How do they want to be seen by the reader? They probably want to be seen as compassionate, empathetic. But also, as an authority, an expert.

As someone fighting against big pharma or injustices in the medical system. Maybe they believe in free access to medicine. That's a big element of the ethos.

Then you've got the tone of voice. This is a continuation of the ethos. It is HOW they talk.

Are they more emotional? Aggressive? Are they more fact-based and logical?


<!-- Source: Day 11 - Preparing the Copy.pdf; page 2 -->

There’s a big difference between a millionaire bad boy and a natural health guru: In the lexicon, analogies, emotionality...

Then you must research the ‘WHY.’

Why are you talking to these people? This is a crucial element of your Copy.

It’s the justification for your approach.

Why do you feel the need to talk to your readers about this subject? Why do you feel the need to help them with this problem? What are your intentions?

You must have a valid reason to reach out to them. It cannot be just a purely commercial transaction.

That’s the tricky thing with sales: When you frame it as sales, or if you don't frame it at all - it doesn’t work... Because nobody likes to be sold to. When the selling feels forced or when you feel like you're being taken advantage of, you hate it. And that’s just basic psychology.

People don't want to be taken for fools. They don't want to appear gullible. When you look deep enough, you find that this is all due to shame: The selling process is shameful because it exposes us to the vulgar reality of life. Hence, you must circumvent that.

You do this by explaining the reason why you felt the need to contact them about the problem they're facing and for which you have a solution. That’s the WHY, the reasoning behind contacting them to sell them something. You will see that when you justify correctly, you will get out of the equation of ‘the seller and the prospect.’

You will find a reason that's different from: “Hey, I'm selling you a product because I desperately need money...” And you're going to find a very different explanation for it.

After that you’re going to research the ‘WHAT.’

The WHAT includes the MECHANISM and BIG IDEA: The heart of what you're selling:

- What’s the pain?
- What’s the story?
- What's the idea behind it?
- What's the opportunity?


<!-- Source: Day 11 - Preparing the Copy.pdf; page 3 -->

- What's the dream outcome?

That’s the ‘meat’ of your copy.

And finally you have the ‘HOW.’

HOW are you going to write your Copy? What kind of analogies, themes, ideas, and promises are you going to use?

This will depend heavily on the context: You don't talk the same way to different people.

That’s why templates don't work well.

For example: When you’re dealing with a serious topic like ‘cancer.’ Would you use fear tactics to sell your product? Would you make ‘loud,’ impossible, promises?

And if so, would you say that to your mother if she had cancer?

Would you say: “Hey, Mom, I found an incredible method that comes from China that will cure your cancer in 4 weeks”..?

You probably wouldn’t. In this case you would probably want to use some sort of ‘anti-selling’ tactics. Admit that your product doesn’t cure cancer. Then see which gap you can fill.

For example: When people go through chemotherapy it's very painful. Especially when they also do radiotherapy. They may have burns all over their bodies.

So maybe there is a gap here: “Follow your chemo and radiotherapy but suffer less from the consequences of your treatment.”

Finding the gap is exactly one of the things you will need to research when you prepare your Copy.

To recap:

1. THE WHO:

Whom are you talking to? And who is talking?

2. THE WHY:

Why are you even contacting me? Why are we having this conversation?


<!-- Source: Day 11 - Preparing the Copy.pdf; page 4 -->

3. THE WHAT:

What are you talking about? What’s the big opportunity, the dream outcome for your reader?

4. THE HOW:

How are you going to write your copy? How are you going to approach the sales process?


<!-- Source: Day 11 - Preparing the Copy.pdf; page 5 -->

### Summary

Before writing any copy, research and understand these 4 crucial elements:

1. THE ‘WHO:’

- Your reader: © Create a persona of your ideal customer © Focus on beliefs and worldviews rather than demographics © Understand how they see the world to confirm their perspective (don’t try to change it!)

- The author:
- Who is speaking? Is it you or someone else?
- Know their life, achievements, and beliefs © Determine their ethos (the image they’re projecting) and tone of voice

2. THE ‘WHY:’

- Have a valid reason for reaching out
- Explain why you’re addressing their problem
- Move beyond a purely commercial transaction
- Prevent resistance by framing it as ‘more than selling’

3. THE ‘WHAT:’

- Know the ‘meat of your Copy:’ Mechanism & Big Idea
- What’s the pain?
- What’s the story? © What's the idea behind it?
- What's the opportunity?
- What's the dream outcome?

4. THE ‘HOW:’
- Pick the right writing approach: © Appropriate analogies, themes, and promises © Mind the context and audience
- Find the ‘gap’ your product can fill
- Use ‘anti-selling’ tactics when appropriate


<!-- Source: Day 11 - Preparing the Copy.pdf; page 6 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Now that you’ve got a good understanding of how you should go about your sales letter...

.You’re ready to dive into the research phase.

Which is something that many Copywriters hate for good reason, because their ‘process’ looks like this...

They erratically browse the Internet like a crack addict looking for his next fix, thinking there’s some sort of ‘secret info’ out there...

And before they know it, hours have passed, they’re way behind schedule, and they hit the panic button.. A hair away from ‘rage-quitting’ Copy for good.

I’m not blaming them, because these poor fellas haven’t been taught a structured approach to research...

Which you will be receiving in its entirety tomorrow..!

Like the ‘Research Trifecta,’ that tells you exactly what to look for and how to go about it, so you never feel confused again...

Or the ‘One Word’ that’ll help you ‘Peel The Layers’ and uncover your reader’s deepest motivations and frustrations, which go way beyond ‘more money, better health, and relationships...’

And other, more subtle, techniques like the ‘Frustration Flip’ that turns tiny annoyances into must-haves.

You’ll be left with a comprehensive method that tells you exactly when you’re DONE...

SO you can finally start writing.

Your friend,

[Handwritten signature]


---

## Day 12: The Principles of Efficient Research

<!-- Source: Day 12 - The Principles of Efficient Research.pdf; page 1 -->

Let’s dive deeper into the 3 main avenues of research: the ‘WHO,’ the ‘WHAT’ and the ‘HOW.’

Let’s start with the WHO.

Know your customer

You don’t talk to a kid and a doctor in the same way. So if you don’t know who you are talking to.. You can’t communicate effectively.

First, study the metrics of your average customer:

- Gender
- Age
- Social/economic status (buying power)
- Political beliefs & values (conservative, liberal)

Your client usually knows about this data. Ask them to forward it to you.

Remember: You don’t want to influence a market with your marketing.

Don’t try to change people. Instead, identify their beliefs and confirm them. Give people what they want.

Once you have all the basic demographic information, you can:

- Establish a persona (build an avatar). To do this, answer the following questions: © Who is your ‘perfect client’? © What mistakes are your prospects making that your product or service can help prevent? © What are their greatest fears, keeping them up at night?
- What frustrations do they face without your solution? © What would be a ‘dream come true’ for them?
- What is their #1 pressing problem, pain or fear they want to solve?


<!-- Source: Day 12 - The Principles of Efficient Research.pdf; page 2 -->

- What are the top 5-10 most common objections you get about your offering?
- Are there any villains, or current news events related to your product that you can leverage? © What words do people in your market use to describe their problems? © Who are your direct and indirect competitors?
- What marketing angles have failed or succeeded in this market before?
- What do your best customers do for fun, for a living, and how much do they make? Whom are they trying to impress or get revenge on?
- What do they complain, worry about, or discuss with their spouse about your offering?

- Thanks to this info, write their autobiography (optional).
- Find out what your avatar watches, reads, consumes online. Who are their heroes? Who do they love and who do they hate?
- Gather testimonials (call them or find 3-4 star reviews on Amazon, Reddit, Quora, forums, Facebook groups...)
- Study their dream outcome, deep emotion, and main pain. - Divide pain into painful steps preventing success.
- Make an inventory of each ‘frustration’ and ‘wants.’

Now let’s break down each element.

First, the dream outcome is never money, health, status, or relationships.

This answer is too basic and will not help you. You need to go deeper, as it’s not always obvious.

One way is to ask why and peel each layer until you get to the real reason people buy.

- Why do they want more money? “Because they want peace of mind!”
- Why do they want peace of mind? “Because they want to be left alone!” m Why do they want to be left alone?
- Etc.


<!-- Source: Day 12 - The Principles of Efficient Research.pdf; page 3 -->

Some people want ABUNDANCE, some people want SECURITY. Often the former are men, the latter women.

Once you suspect a real reason or motive behind their desire, you can identify their pain.

First there’s the obvious pain. Then there’s a deeper pain.

- They want to get fit not just because it’s healthy and they would look better than now (obvious pain)...
- ..But because they fear their wives might leave them if they don’t act now! (deeper pain)

- A man’s back hurts: It’s painful (obvious pain)
- It prevents him from playing with his kid and being seen as a good father (deeper pain).

Physical pain is not enough to drive action. Most people learn to live with pain. What drives action is emotional pain.

Here’s a secret: Most people have a piece missing in their hearts and souls. Real marketing is about offering them that missing piece.

Now for ‘frustrations’ and ‘wants.’

Create 2 columns - one for frustrations and another for wants. List out the frustrations they encounter in their daily lives.

Then, for each one, find the opposite: A situation where everything goes well; these are your wants.

Your job is to find the frustrations and transform them into wants.

They will become the backbone of your copy.

Know your offer and product

Now onto the ‘WHAT.’

You must know everything about the product.

Every detail is a potential Big Idea: remember the Rolls-Royce and the Schlitz beer ads.


<!-- Source: Day 12 - The Principles of Efficient Research.pdf; page 4 -->

To do this, answer the following questions:

- How’s the product made?
- Why was it made? What’s the story behind it?
- What problem does it solve?
- What kind of ‘gap’ is our product filling on the market?
- How aware are your prospects?
- What other successful offers exist in this market?
- Why does purchasing yours make better sense?
- What is your unique selling proposition?
- Describe the product's manufacturing process, ingredients, or mechanisms if applicable.
- What untold story exists around this product that prospects should know?
- What is the biggest, most noticeable benefit they'll experience and how quickly?
- What happens if they don't buy and continue living with the current pain points?
- Why should they buy now instead of delaying the decision?
- Pricing details: Cost? Justification? Alternatives and their downsides/costs?
- Any special savings, bonuses, deadlines, shipping costs to highlight?
- Guarantee details: Returns? Refunds? Covering return shipping?

Then you have features v. benefits.

A feature is a rubber on top of your pencil. The benefit is that you can erase any mistake you make.

To get benefits out of features you simply ask: “So what?”

- You’ve got an eraser.
- So what? m You can correct any mistake.
- So what? © You look like someone who never makes mistakes.

You ask: “So what?” until you get to the deep emotion.


<!-- Source: Day 12 - The Principles of Efficient Research.pdf; page 5 -->

Your product has a foundational story. An origins story, showing why the product was made.

When John Carlton interviewed his client, a man selling a golf course.. He asked how the man (who was a doctor) got to selling it.

The man answered that he had been learning a great deal from a one legged golfer much better than many of his two-legged counterparts. The guy couldn’t see that this was a Big Idea; John Carlton did. And the rest is history!

Foundational stories are not the only good source of Big Ideas. You can also find them in:

- Real-world event (a natural calamity?)
- Discovery (news in the niche?)
- Opportunity (In the market?)
- Even the general experience of people with similar products in the niche.

Ask yourself:

- What do people feel when consuming products in my niche?
- Why didn’t the products they bought work for them in the past?
- Is there a gap in the process of getting to their dream outcome?
- What can I promise them to make them buy my product?

This ties back to everyday frustrations. What can you make better for them?

Use basic human feelings and experience: What emotions and small details can you notice, to use in your copy?

These could be:

- Elements of proof
- Results
- Anecdotes
- Case studies
- Historical facts
- Scientific articles


<!-- Source: Day 12 - The Principles of Efficient Research.pdf; page 6 -->

Remember: Whenever you make a claim, you must prove it.

Know your style

Lastly, we have the HOW.

It all boils down to the ethos of the author:

How do they want to be seen by their clients/buyers? What image are they projecting?

What kind of values do they represent?

This determines the tone of voice you will use.

There are many:

- Brotherly: “I will kick your butt until you make it.”
- Authoritative: “I’m an expert and I know what I’m talking about.”
- Horizontal: “I’m just like you, I’ve been where you are, I know what it feels like.”
- Fatherly: “I’ve been young too. Trust me, this isn’t how you should do it.”
- Friendly: “Look at this opportunity. I’m only sharing it with trusted friends.”

Some tones of voice are a mix of two: a mentor could be authoritative and fatherly, for instance.


<!-- Source: Day 12 - The Principles of Efficient Research.pdf; page 7 -->

### Summary

1. WHO: Know your customer

- Know your basic demographics: gender, age, socio-economic status, political beliefs.

- Create a persona of your ideal client:
- Identify their fears, frustrations, and dreams © Understand their pressing problems and objections
- Research their habits, interests, and influences
- Dive deep into what motivates them:

© Uncover the real reasons behind their desires © Identify obvious and deeper pains
- List frustrations and corresponding wants

Focus on emotional pain, not just physical pain, to drive action.

2. WHAT: Know your product

- Understand every detail of the product:
- How it's made, why it was created, the problems it solves © USP and market positioning

- Identify potential Big Ideas: © Origin stories, real-world events, discoveries, opportunities

- Distinguish between features and benefits: © Use the ‘So What?’ technique to uncover deep emotional benefits

- Gather proof:
- Results, case studies, scientific articles, historical facts

Every detail is a potential Big Idea. Always back your claims with proof.


<!-- Source: Day 12 - The Principles of Efficient Research.pdf; page 8 -->

3. HOW: Know your style

- Determine the ethos of the author:
- How they want to be perceived © The values they represent

- Choose the appropriate tone of voice: © Brotherly, authoritative, horizontal, fatherly, friendly © Can be a mixture of two

The tone should align with the author's ethos and resonate with the target audience.


<!-- Source: Day 12 - The Principles of Efficient Research.pdf; page 9 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Now that you’ve followed the Cascade and chosen your Positioning, USP, Unique Mechanism, Big Idea, and Big Promise...

You’ re ready to start writing your sales letter.

And to do it successfully without going insane, you must follow a clear structure...

.Which is why I’ll be sharing with you my personal and most valuable framework - the ‘APICRO’ Model - that’s PACKED with detail, logic, and specificity...

..SO you can put everything together in a cohesive package that turns lukewarm prospects into rabid buyers in one go.

You'll discover the ‘Logical Ladder’ that leverages ‘Movers’ to glue your reader’s eyes to the screen, seamlessly guiding them from problem to purchase...

You’ll get to learn my most treasured techniques learned through years of trial and error...

...Like the ‘Mechanism Triad,’ the ‘False Solution Flip,’ or the ‘Sensory Immersion’ method...

...As well as the ‘Call To Value’ that puts traditional Call To Actions to shame.

You’ll learn how to use ‘Pattern Interrupts’ the right way and how to add a ‘P.S.’ that contributes to your argument rather than detract from it...

Long-form sales letters are the most demanding form of Copy, which is why the top performers can charge so much for it.

And when you master it, everything else becomes easier...

Tomorrow’s lesson is your golden ticket to the Big League.

Your friend,

[Handwritten signature]

## Day 13: A Winning Sales Letter Structure

<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 1 -->

Now that you’ve followed the Cascading Effect and chosen your...

- Positioning
- USP
- Unique Mechanism
- Big Idea
- Big Promise

You're finally ready to start writing your sales page.

Except you don’t know where to start. What to say. Nor how to say it.

Sure you could just wing it, but this is the worst thing you could possibly do.

To achieve any kind of success you must follow a clear sales page structure, like:

- AIDA: Attention, Interest, Desire, Action...
- PAS: Problem, Agitation, Solution...
- PAISA: Problem, Agitation, Interest, Solution, Action...
- PPP: Picture, Promise, Proof, Push...
- etc.

These frameworks are great. They give you a general idea of what to do.

But they lack detail, logic, and specificity.

That’s why I’d like to share with you 3 ways of understanding a sales page that are to be used together:

1. My personal Framework. 2.9 Movers.
3. And my preferred structure.

I’ve successfully used these 3 elements for a long time. They allow me to understand Copy at a deeper level.

Disclaimer: this method isn’t plug & play. This is not a ‘fill-in-the-blanks template.’


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 2 -->

You will have to:

- Determine whether these structures fit your positioning, USP, Unique Mechanism, Big Idea, Big Promise...
- Adapt them to your own context.

Do not blindly copy and paste these structures. Instead, analyze them to understand why they work.

Also, this is going to be a dense lesson. So don’t hesitate to come back to it as much as you need to.

The Main Components of a Sales Letter

Sales letters have 5 main moments:

1. The Headline.
2. The Lead/Lede.
3. The Body copy.
4. The Offer section.
5. The P.S.

The Headline can be made of several parts.

- The Eyebrow (optional.)
- The Main headline.
- The Subhead (optional.)
- The Deck Copy (optional.)

| Component | Example | | --- | --- | | Eyebrow | WHEN MANKIND LANDED ON THE MOON IN 1969, | | Main headline | WE UNLOCKED THE KEY TO A $3.7 TRILLION BONANZA... | | Subhead | AND IF YOU THOUGHT LIFE WAS GOOD IN THE ’60S OR ’70S JUST WAIT UNTIL YOU SEE WHAT’S NEXT... | | Deck Copy | “LIFT OFF” SET FOR AUGUST 6, 2015 | | Lead | Dear Reader, You know the date by heart. July 20, 1969. |


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 3 -->

Here you’ve got an eyebrow: “When mankind landed on the moon in 1969...”

You’ve got the main headline: “We unlocked the key to a 3.7 trillion bonanza”

And the subhead: “And if you thought life was good in the ‘60s or ‘70s just wait until you see what’s next...”

Deck copy is usually a set of bullet points showing what you’ll learn by reading the letter.

For some people, deck copy also equals subhead. Here I would say the deck is “‘Lift Off’ set for August 6, 2015.”

After the Headline you enter the Lead, or Lede.

The Lead is the introduction to the Copy: It’s around 200-1000 words long.

It’s where you make your reader salivate at the idea of reading what’s next!

The lead should include:

- A Hook.
- A Pattern Interrupt (optional.)
- Your Big Idea.
- Your Big Promise.
- A Bribe, or ‘What’s In It For Me?’

The Hook is the first part of the Lead. It can include a Pattern Interrupt, though this is not mandatory.

The goal of the Hook is to grab attention and ‘hook’ readers.

A Pattern Interrupt is one way to do it. It’s a NLP (Neuro Linguistic Programming) and hypnosis term, and it consists in breaking one’s pattern.

I LOVE CHEESECAKE.

There, I just ‘broke your pattern’ by doing something unexpected. Now I’ve got your full attention.

But here’s the thing...


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 4 -->

Pattern Interrupts are only effective only when they’ re relevant. Being random like I was, is risky as it’s confusing for your reader. And you don’t want a confused reader.

| OF %) ‘i he Sine of

5 a7 2 99 Some will call this “magic + “ 9 Others will see “hope ° “ . ” Still others “dollar signs What do you see? Dear Curious Reader, If you are like me, you look at this image and you're almost hypnotized by it. What is it, you wonder? And what is it doing here, in an investment newsletter?

Please let me answer these questions with one long question!

Once you’ve grabbed their attention, you should introduce your Big Idea in a few sentences. Tell them WHY you’re talking to them today, and WHY they should read your letter in full.

You don’t have to spill the beans and reveal everything right away. See this as a teaser, but be clear about what you’re about to show.. And what’s in it for them.

Make your Big Promise and bribe them to read the full letter.

Once you’ve done this, you can transition towards your Body Copy and then your Offer Section.

We’ll cover the Offer Section thoroughly in another lesson.

My Most Valuable Framework

Now that we know what a Sales Letter looks like, let’s get more specific.

My most valuable framework is called APICRO...


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 5 -->

It stands for:

- Attention
- Problem
- Interest
- Comparison
- Resolution
- Offer

Let’s see what each one entails.

- ‘Attention’ is all about grabbing your readers by the eyeballs.

Hook them, use a Pattern Interrupt if you need to. It can be a weird fact. A weird image. A weird story.

Use urgency, novelty, or ‘bigness.’

- ‘Problem’ is where you formulate the precise problem your readers have.

And agitate the emotions keeping them up at night. And promise them you will solve it in a quick, easy, and safe way.

- ‘Interest’ means showing your reader what can happen when they find the solution.

The opportunities that could open up... Their rise in social status.. Their confidence in themselves and their abilities... Their newfound wealth and financial security.. So on and so forth.

- ‘Comparison’ means comparing the already existing solutions and showing why they do not work.

Here you show empathy to your readers and show them why it’s not their fault.

- ‘Resolution’ is the pivotal point in your copy.

This part in itself could take up half of your letter. This is when you reveal a new perspective on the problem... Explain the real mechanism behind your reader’s failures... And formulate a clear solution.


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 6 -->

- ‘Offer’ is for the Offer Section.

Here you introduce your product, zoom in on a few features and benefits, you anchor the price, add your guarantee and testimonials, you summarize your offer and end with a CTA.

You’ll learn how to craft creative Offer Sections on Day 17.

This framework lets you remember where you should be taking your readers, but as I said, we need to go deeper.

How To Use Movers

I call ‘Movers’ the most crucial moments of your sales letter.

They do the heavy lifting... And move the sale forward.

I’ve identified 9 Movers that I’ll illustrate with a real-life example...

A few years ago, I only had 2 weeks to write a long form sales letter.

We were selling a sleep formula. But there was a problem. It only contained a handful of common garden plants. Nothing crazy, unique, or surprising.

I knew straight away I couldn’t come up with a GREAT story about these everyday plants. So I went another route...

I crafted a personal story. The author’s story.

It told you how he’d wake up at 4 A.M.. Unable to get back to sleep.. And how sleep deprivation was eating him alive.

That was the first Mover: ‘Pain.’

Pain is the pitch. You’ll never go wrong by starting with it.

So I went into details about how the lack of sleep was affecting his whole life. How all of his friends knew him as ‘The Insomniac.’ How his own health deteriorated.

And how he needed to find a solution QUICK.

In the first paragraphs I teased my ‘Big Promise.’ That’s the second Mover.


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 7 -->

The Big Idea was: “How To Unlock The Sleeping Power of Common Garden Plants.”

I turned the main weakness of my ingredients into their main strength.

‘Common Garden Plants’ means you can find them in your own backyard. They’re accessible. You simply have to bend over to pick them up. They’re not exotic, which makes them less scary. I’m pretty sure you’d be comfortable with trying lavender...

The 3rd Mover was the ‘False Solutions.’ I debunked all the previous solutions, the ones the Author (and the reader) had already tried without success. I provided solid evidence explaining why they didn’t work... And teased the Mechanism (why the ‘Garden Plants’ are different) .

The 4th Mover was introducing a ‘Teacher.’

An authority figure who educates the reader. I chose the teacher to be another character of the story. I based this character on someone I knew who’s an expert on plants.

In the story he’s the ‘Wise Man.’ He makes fun of the Author for not knowing the ‘Secret power’ of plants. But ultimately accepts to reveal their secret.

Now there’s a 5th, more subtle, Mover. This one is more difficult to master because it’s all about storytelling.

It’s ‘Ambience.’ How the story feels. Where it transports your reader. I built a serene and calm Ambience.. Favorable for sleep. You build Ambience by setting the story in a specific place. Here it was in the countryside. Up on a mountain. The Mountain represents secret, almost supernatural, knowledge.

It’s all done on purpose. I made sure to include the 5 human senses: Touch, sight, hearing, smell, and taste. They all contribute to Ambience.

The Teacher would make the Author smell the plants... Touch their petals... And taste the tea he’d brew with them.

The reader can’t help but feel it for himself.

The 6th Mover is the ‘Unique Mechanism.’


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 8 -->

In the story, I added a little twist to make it spicier. See, when the author discovered that common plants would be his life-savers according to the Teacher, he became skeptical...

...Just like the reader.

The Mechanism explains the Magic with solid logic. It SHOWS why the Promise is true. And why it’s different from other solutions.

The 7th Mover is the ‘Why’ of the product: Why it exists in the first place.

Here it was because nobody had done it before. And the Author could not stand there and do nothing about it. He felt the need to provide the reader with the exact same solution that helped him.

The 8th Mover is ‘Scarcity.’ It’s all the elements that make your product unique, rare, and exceptional. Maybe the plants only grow at a certain altitude? Maybe you need 30 kgs of these plants to make 1 capsule?

Remember: A Mover moves the sale forward. So only mention the things that do.

The 9th Mover is the ‘Offer.’ You will soon know how to craft an offer. With a price anchor.. A bold guarantee... Urgency... And with elements specific to your niche.

Those are the 9 Movers I focused on.

1. Pain
2. Big Promise
3. Debunking the other solutions
4. Introducing a Teacher
5. Ambience
6. The Unique Mechanism
7. Why your product exists
8. Scarcity
9. Offer.

There may be other Movers depending on your niche and product.

Now let’s go into even more details. This time, let’s focus on the internal logic of your sales letter.


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 9 -->

A Logical Structure

Buying is at its core a direct emotional response.

Yet, people need to be able to explain their reasoning rationally, which is why you must give them a logical reason to buy as well.

That’s where we need to talk about mechanisms.

There are 3 types of mechanisms:

- The mechanism of the problem
- The mechanism of the solution
- The mechanism of the false solutions

The mechanism of the problem describes how your target audience understands its problem.

And the mechanism of the solution is how you solve that problem.

Here’s an example...

The real reason you are balding is not because of DHT like most people say...

It’s because of estrogens.

And today I’m going to tell you why DHT is not the culprit and why so many people are wrong about it.

I will then explain why you need to target your estrogen levels to stop your hair from falling out.

Here, we explain the mechanism of the problem to put forward the mechanism of our solution.

The latter hints at the emotion of ‘new.’

You give a new explanation to a problem. It gives people hope. And when you give people hope...

You make more sales.

That’s why mechanisms are so important.


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 10 -->

And because ‘better’ doesn’t mean much while ‘different’ carries a lot of meaning...

...People will usually prefer ‘different solutions’ over ‘better solutions.’

When your explanation gets ultra specific, ‘different’ turns into ‘better’ in the mind of your reader, by some sort of magic ritual in your Copy.

This is where you introduce the mechanism of the false solutions.

For instance...

Some people have told you that Minoxidil or Finasteride will stop you from balding...

...But in reality: they don’t work. And they have terrible side effects.

I’m going to explain why these products are useless when it comes to ending your hair loss for good...

And why you shouldn’t be spraying these hazardous chemicals on your scalp.

Always make sure you show why the false solutions don’t work instead of telling your reader he is dumb for using it.

Let people come to their own conclusions without telling them what to believe or how they should think.

When you’ve got your 3 mechanisms figured out, we can focus on the actual structure of your sales letter.

So you start with your Headline.

You move onto the Hook, the Big Idea, the Big Promise.

You can then introduce yourself and answer the 2 questions your reader has:

- Who are you?
- Why should I trust you?”


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 11 -->

That’s where you show your credibility and authority on the matter at hand.

It will usually be a specific result that the reader wants for himself or an achievement that supports what you’re about to say.

You then explore the problem and its mechanism. Why people feel bad about it. What they say about it and why we don’t really understand it.

That’s when you explain the false solutions and show how damaging they are.

Always prove everything you say and back up your claims with solid evidence.

You can then tell your foundational story:

- The problems you faced...
- Everything you have tried to no avail...
- And how you discovered a solution.

You can then introduce your solution and explain its mechanism.

- How does it work?
- What makes it better than what you have tried before?

Here you can get specific about it. Say clearly and explicitly how it benefits your target audience.

Make them feel and anticipate how it will change their life.

Make it exciting!

You can then bring up your offer and use anchoring to make it a no-brainer.

If your reader was to look for a solution on his own...

- How much more time would he waste?
- How expensive would that be?
- How worse will things get for him?
- What will the people around him think of him?


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 12 -->

Be creative and realistic at the same time.

Keep it ‘close to home.’ But do not shame or accuse your reader.

You want them to come to their own conclusion that passing up on your solution right now will be more painful than trying it.

You can then introduce your guarantee: Make them feel like their purchase is risk-free.

With Front-End offers, it’s worth it to have a ‘Happy Or Your Money Back’ guarantee.

When people don’t know you, they need that little extra reinsurance before they take out their credit card.

It doesn’t always have to be a ‘Happy Or Your Money Back guarantee, by the way. You can add conditions to it too.

If you’re selling a course, you could say..

“If you do not get {result} after having {proof of effort}, I will personally help you until you do”

Whatever you choose, make sure it’s strong and reassuring. Take some weight off their shoulders.

You can then bring up your testimonials. Focus on quantity here. Nobody reads all of them, maybe a couple ones at the top. People will just scroll through and be comforted to see you have plenty of proof.

That’s why you keep your best ones at the top, whether it’s from another authority figure in your niche or the most flattering ones.

You will then have one or multiple CTA. I prefer ‘Call to Value,’ which focuses on the benefit of the action, rather than the action itself.

‘Become A Six-Figure Copywriter’ will work better than ‘Buy The Course.’

You can then add a P.S., but only if it adds to your sales argument rather than detract from it.


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 13 -->

You can use some more anchoring around the price.

You can add some scarcity / exclusivity around your offer.

Or you can tell the reader that this opportunity is time-sensitive and he’ll be missing out if he doesn’t take advantage of it today.

Just keep it congruent with your sales argument.

Here’s a recap of the structure:

- Headline
- Lead
- Who you are
- Mechanism of the problem
- Mechanism of the false solutions
- Discovery story
- Mechanism of your solution
- Offer
- Guarantee
- Testimonials
- Call-To-Value
- P.Ss.

Again, this may or may not apply to your current campaign.

So don’t blindly apply it, but understand the reasoning behind it so you can make it work for you.

And use it as a general direction of where you should be taking your reader.


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 14 -->

### Summary

Core components of a sales letter:

1. Headline © Eyebrow: Optional attention-grabber © Main Headline: Primary hook for your reader
- Subhead: Supports your main headline, adds intrigue © Deck Copy: Bullet points previewing the letter
2. Lead © Hook: Grabs attention
- Pattern Interrupt: Unexpected element © Big Idea: Core concept of your pitch
- Big Promise: What the reader will gain
- Clear benefit statement: ‘What’s In It For Me?’
3. Body Copy
- Main content of your letter
4. Offer Section © Details of what you’re selling 5.P.S.
- Final reinforcement / Urgency

The APICRO Framework:

- Attention: Use urgency, novelty, or 'bigness' to grab your readers by the eyeballs
- Problem: Articulate the exact issue and agitate the related emotions
- Interest: Paint a picture of their life after finding the solution
- Comparison: Explain why other solutions fall short, show empathy
- Resolution: Reveal new perspective on the problem, explain the real mechanism behind failures, formulate clear solution
- Offer: Introduce your product, highlight features and benefits, anchor the price, add guarantee and testimonials, summarize, and end with ‘Call-To-Value’


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 15 -->

The 9 ‘Movers’ that drive the sale forward:

1. Pain: Show how the problem is affecting your reader
2. Big Promise: Tease the solution early on
3. False Solutions: Provide evidence for why they don’t work
4. Teacher / Authority: Add credibility to your solution
5. Ambience: Use storytelling, engage all senses
6. Unique Mechanism: Explain the logic behind your solution
7. ‘Why’ behind your product: Share the ‘origin story’
8. Scarcity: Highlight the uniqueness of your solution
9. Offer: Craft a compelling proposition

Pick your Movers wisely, depending on the context.

The Mechanisms in Copywriting:

1. Mechanism of the problem: How your reader understands their issue
2. Mechanism of the solution: How you solve the problem
3. Mechanism of the false solutions: Why other approaches don’t work

Focus on being ‘different’ rather than ‘better.’ Specific solutions are more compelling than generic improvements.

The logical structure of a sales letter:

- Headline
- Lead
- Who you are
- Mechanism of the problem
- Mechanism of the false solutions
- Discovery story
- Mechanism of your solution
- Offer
- Guarantee
- Testimonials
- Call-To-Value
- P.s.


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 16 -->

Here are some more general tips:

- Always back your claims with solid evidence
- Create an emotional connection while providing logical reasons to buy
- Use storytelling to transport the reader, engage all five senses.
- Make the offer a ‘no-brainer’ through effective anchoring
- Use multiple CTAs focusing on benefits rather than actions.
- Adapt the structure to your specific context, positioning, USP, and Big Idea.


<!-- Source: Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure.pdf; page 17 -->

I know it’s hard to believe, but ‘fascinations’ are so powerful that a single one of them can be enough to trigger a purchase...

## Day 14: Fascinating Bullets

<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 1 -->

This Copywriter was so talented that his employer withheld his name from the public for over 20 years... fearing that competitors would try to hire him.

I’m talking about Mel Martin who helped turn Boardroom Publishing into a $125 million cash cow with over 200,000 paid subscribers.

mi (ms Ve A

— kgs Lad ———

Mel was famous for what he called ‘fascinations,’ which are short bullet points that grab people’s attention and make them want to learn more.

Fascinations are compelling, benefit-driven bombs of curiosity that position your offer as the ONLY answer.

Take a look at some of his best ones...

- Bills it’s OK to pay late
- What never to eat on an airplane
- College aid for families with money
- Say the secret word and rental car costs less
- How to collect interest from two different banks at the same time

I could go on and on, because Mel was so prolific at coming up with them.

For instance, one of his most successful sales letters is 90% fascinations..

And nothing more.


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 2 -->

Mel would often write hundreds of them before working on a sales letter.

This would focus his mind, get his creative juices flowing and give him multiple angle ideas.

And it’s the #1 reason I recommend you become proficient at writing hard-hitting bullets.

It trains your mind to see the top benefits in an offer and tease without giving them away.

And it carries over to many other things like headlines, subject lines, subheads, openers, etc..

Simply put... If you can write great bullets, you’re a great copywriter.

Now, before we get into general good practice and proven formulas, let’s recap the purpose of bullets.

Bullet points are like mini-headlines

They draw attention. They’re easy to scan. And they break down complex ideas into bite-sized pieces without losing the core message.

Above all, they provide multiple ‘attachment points’ to hook your reader and get him to read the rest of your letter.

As such, they allow you to capture a wider audience.

Bullet points are incredibly effective at teasing your offer and building intense desire...

..But only when they trigger excitement.

Because they’re so easy to read, boring bullets are the weakest link of your sales argument and cause you to lose readership and therefore, sales.

So here are 5 rules for writing fascinating bullets:

1. Keep them short and sweet

1-2 sentences at most.


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 3 -->

Get to the point without making bold claims you cannot back up with proof.

Don’t throw superlatives around for no reason!

2. Focus on benefits

Don’t just list features, show your reader how it will benefit his life.

Use metaphors, comparisons and vivid imagery to make it come to life in your reader’s mind.

3. Make them intriguing

You want to tickle your reader’s curiosity.

It’s like telling a loved one you’ve got a surprise for them without saying what it is.

4. Get creative

Your bullets don’t have to be ‘proper’ sentences.

They have to be persuasive and if that comes at the cost of being grammatically correct, well...

Also, make sure you mix and match your bullet style. Don’t start all of them with “How to...”.

We’ll get into that in a moment.

5. Start strong

Your bullet points shouldn’t come in chronological order.

Instead, you want to drop your strongest ones from the get-go.

Now, the hardest part of writing compelling bullets is the fine line between being specific without being too specific.

Teasing without revealing too much...

And for that, there’s no better teacher than experience.

This is why I highly recommend you write tons of bullets for each of your campaigns.


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 4 -->

You won’t use all of them in your letter, but you’ll be left with plenty of ideas, angles and potential headline material.

Another thing you can do is study the works of great copywriters like Mel Martin, Ben Settle, Gary Bencivenga, Gary Halbert...

You know I’m generally not a fan of frameworks...

...But bullets are an exception.

So let’s not reinvent the wheel here and look at what the greats before us have been using successfully.

But before we get into that, here are 3 key elements of fascinating bullets:

1.A specific benefit

Tell your reader exactly how your offer will benefit him or the specific pain point that it solves.

A quick note on specificity...

You don’t want to be specific for the sake of it. You want to be specific on what’s interesting to your reader.

2.A short timeframe

Your reader wants to profit right now, not in 10 years... Put some instant gratification into your bullet point.

Look into your USP, see if you can promise a quick win and be creative.

Above all, keep it believable and do not lie.

3. Intense curiosity

You can simply leave out a piece of information. Or you can ask a question and answer it partially.

You can raise a problem and introduce your own solution (“the XYZ method” or the ‘ABC framework.’)

Or you can add an element of surprise and fun.


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 5 -->

Now in terms of structure, some copywriters say your bullets should be symmetrical.. I tend to agree, but don’t obsess over it.

- Focus on making each of them compelling by adding the 3 elements above.
- Drop your most persuasive ones first.
- And vary the format using these 22 bullet formulas I’ve personally used in my Copy...

But first, let me give credit where credit is due.

Nearly all of them are taken from the Bible of bullet point writing, the Screaming Eagle issue from September 2006: 21 Bullet Secrets That Ignite The Selling Power Of Your Copy.

Here they are.

1. The ‘How To’

This has to be the most popular bullet formula...

Because people are naturally drawn to “how to” information, especially when it’s tied to a deep feeling or desire.

You also use the negative “How not to” and follow it up with something your reader wants to avoid.

“How to approach women at the library without sounding like a dork thanks to this innocuous question”

“How to not look like a total creep when you talk to women at the gym with this body language cue used by MI-6 agents”

2. The ‘Secret’

This is similar to the “How To” but applied to legitimate secrets.

So make sure you use it for content that is genuinely less-known and don’t overuse it.. Otherwise you’ll erode trust in your offer and look scammy.

“The secret exercise used by World’s Strongest Man Bill Kazmaier to burst through overhead press plateaus and build monstrous pushing strength”


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 6 -->

3. The ‘Why’

The best way to build intrigue.

It acts as a promise and implies the answer will make a positive difference in your reader’s life.

“Why you don’t want to get a pump in the gym: it tricks you into believing you’ve had a productive workout and kills your progress.. Focus on this timeless principle instead”

“The reason why your cold DMs aren’t getting replies is not what you think... Why you don’t want to over-personalize your script”

4. The ‘What’

This one increases the perceived value of the information.

You can use it in 2 ways:

- Give your reader a specific course of action

“What you MUST do right after you send a cold DM to triple your chances of getting a reply and sign your next client”

- Offer him elusive, valuable information

“What banking analysts won’t tell you about Panama’s crackdown on gangs (buy this stock now!)”

5. The ‘What Never’

This formula is popular for good reason: it’s incredibly powerful.

You stack the fear factor on top of the promise of a benefit on top of intense curiosity...

Alternatively, you can write “Why you shouldn't..”, “The absolute worst...” or “What to avoid...”

“What never to eat with your magnesium supplement. This other micronutrient competes for absorption so you end up Pissing it all away”


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 7 -->

“The absolute worst way to launch a cold email campaign, unless you want you want to be flagged as spam and have a 0% open rate”

“What exercises to avoid for your body type and drastically reduce your risk of injury so you never have to take time away from training”

6. The ‘PLUS’

This bullet appeals to greed.

It’s usually added at the end of your list and works as the cherry on the cake.

“PLUS - My battle-tested cold DM scripts that brought me over 70% positive responses over the last year. Simply copy and paste and make it work for you too!”

7. The ‘Number’

This one is increasingly popular especially in the online courses space.

It strengthens your point by grouping together secrets, methods or reasons why.

“The 6 face self-massages used by 2015 Miss Universe Flora Coquerel.. To eliminate black circles, puffiness and sagging cheeks so you look radiant all day!”

“3 unconventional exercises for broad, strong and healthy shoulders used by Persian warriors and Indian wrestlers since millenia.”

8. The ‘Right..? Wrong!’

You can bank on your prospects' assumptions by naming it and immediately debunking it with “Wrong!”

“You have to be extroverted to work in sales, right? Wrong! Check out Module 3 to learn why we’re all introverts and how you can tap into your shyness to make people trust you and buy from you.”

9. The ‘Warning’

This formula lets you warn your reader of a danger ahead.


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 8 -->

You can target the emotion of fear or you use it in an ironic sense, where you’ll make a strong promise right after.

You can also start with “caution”, “alert” or “danger”.

“Warning: don’t look at *this* body part when talking to a woman or she’ll see you as a pervert! And I’m not talking about her breasts or her lips!”

“Caution: don’t use this 3-part cold email sequence if you cannot take any more clients in your copywriting business... Positive responses are guaranteed!”

“Danger: this “natural” ice-cream thickener wreaks havoc on your intestines! Avoid at all costs unless you want to spend the night on the toilet!”

10. The ‘Are you...?’

I like this one because it builds credibility and empathy by showing your reader you understand him.

It also builds momentum towards reading more and towards the sale.

You simply ask your reader about something you’re 99% confident he’s currently doing or thinking.

You can also use “Do you..?”, “Does your..?”, “Have you..?” or “Is your..?” and other variations.

“Do you deadlift on your back-day? Top exercise scientist Says you’re increasing risk of injury, hurting your performance and severely limiting your growth.. He says do these deadlift variations instead.”

“Are you eating fruit for dessert? This may be the cause of your bloating and gut issues. Do this to improve your digestion.”

“Does your market research take way too long? Here are the 5 things you should focus on to cut that time in half so you can take on more clients.”

11. The ‘Gimmick’

This one involves extracting an idea from your offer and assigning it an intriguing name.


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 9 -->

“This “household tool” everybody has at home prevents elbow pain, gives you a crushing grip and massive forearms... All in 2 unusual moves that take no time at all!”

“The “XYZ method” for crafting highly personalized cold DMs in minutes rather than hours and guarantee a reply on the same day.”

12. The ‘Sneaky’

Like the “Secret” bullet, this one should be used sparingly otherwise it’ll make your copy feel fake.

Not everything can be sneaky, so make sure you use it when there’s an element of conspiracy to your bullet.

“Sneaky ways the top performing key account sales reps get past gatekeepers on a cold call...”

13. The ‘Fact + Benefit’

This 2-part bullet starts with a statement of interest to your reader and follows up with how to benefit from it.

Alternatively, you can start with a worst case scenario and describe how to avoid it.

“The average number of pull-ups a woman can do is.. Zero. Follow this plan to get your first or increase your reps if you can already do one.”

“90% of copywriters give up only 2 years after starting because they focus on the wrong thing. This is how you make sure you don’t end up like them.”

14. The ‘Direct Benefit’

Here you open with a powerful claim, ideally starting with an action verb and instantly promising the benefit.

The rest of the bullet is about going deeper or adding intrigue or another benefit.

“BUILD THE V-TAPER OF AN ANIME CHARACTER with this unusual back superset used by legendary silver era bodybuilder Steve Reeves.”


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 10 -->

15. The ‘Specific Question’

Here you lead with an intriguing question that matters to your reader and you follow it up with the desired solution.

As always, make sure you add plenty of credibility, benefit, intrigue and curiosity.

“Not feeling your back when doing pull-ups..? Do this “activation exercise” at the end of your warm-up and build the “Cobra back” of legendary martial artist Bruce Lee.”

“Do you suffer from “blank page syndrome”? Discover this technique from copywriter Mel Martin to kickstart your idea muscle and come up with dozens of headlines for your sales page.”

16. The ‘Transaction’

The classic “Give me X and I’ll give you Y”. Use sparingly.

“Give me 5 minutes and I’ll show you how to bulletproof your elbows from injury. These 2 movements are the secret weapon of the greatest rock climber in the world, Adam Ondra.”

17. The ‘If... Then...’

Here you get your reader to agree to a simple requirement, like belonging to a specific group of people or ask him if he’s experiencing some kind of symptom.

You then hit him with the benefit in the second part of the bullet.

“If you have big biceps but they have no “peak”, it means you need to prioritize this little-known muscle that’s underneath... And you can do it by simply switching your grip on your biceps exercises.”

“If you only have one hour a day to learn copywriting, I’ll show you what to prioritize in order to go from total beginner to advanced in a matter of weeks! And no, it’s not hand-copying sales letters.”


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 11 -->

18. The ‘When...’

Use this one to promise a benefit at a specific time. The second part of the bullet gets into the how.

“When your prospect answers your cold DM, make sure you add this tidbit of info in your reply to guarantee he’ll book a call with you.”

“When you hit 8 pull-ups, switch to this variation to get stronger faster and build a denser, thicker back as a result.”

19. The ‘Easiest’ or ‘Quickest’

We all want the easy, fast and effortless way of doing something. It’s human nature and this bullet formula takes advantage of it.

“The quickest way to look absolutely jacked is to focus on these 4 muscles. Add this short “finisher” to your workouts twice a week and you’ll have a commanding frame that gets you respected and looked at in any room you’ll walk into.”

“The easiest way to book calls with your ideal client is not through cold email, ads or social media content... Check out module 4 where I show you how to get your existing clients to gladly send new clients your way.”

20. The ‘Truth about...’

People love controversial takes. Use this formula to challenge a commonly held belief and offer a clear solution portrayed as truth.

“THE TRUTH ABOUT MIKE MENTZER. Was he really ahead of his time with his “high intensity training” methods? Or is it all just hyped up marketing to sell you on yet another magic pill? See page 28.”

“THE REAL CAUSE BEHIND YOUR SHOULDER PAIN. The most complex joint in the human body is made to move “in 3D”... Check out module 3 to see how Indian wrestlers build resilient shoulders that never quit.”


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 12 -->

21. The ‘Better than...’

Use this when you want to position your offer as better than the commonly assumed solution.

This formula lets you bypass your reader’s BS filter, build intrigue and create momentum.

“BETTER THAN LATERAL RAISES. Discover how weightlifting world champion Dmitry Klokov built his massive shoulders that allowed him to hold over 500 lbs overhead.”

“BEYOND RUNNING FOR WEIGHT LOSS. Discover the low impact cardio alternative that burns more calories in less time, without increasing your hunger, beating your body down or shrinking your muscle mass.”

22. The ‘Single most...’

Use this formula to introduce a benefit that surpasses all others. Make sure you back it up with proof.

“The single most important movement you can do for better posture, healthier joints and increased muscle mass. And you don’t even need weights to make it work for you!”

“The single best way to always make progress in the gym is not ‘progressive overload’ or ‘training to failure...’ This counterintuitive method has been used by powerlifting legend Ed Coan, who holds 71 world records.”

There you have it: 22 proven ways to add fascinating bullets to your sales pages.

How To Use Bullets

Now let me show you an example.

But before that, here’s one last tip: don’t assume your prospect always understands your implied benefit. So ask yourself “so what?” after each ‘block’ of words in your bullets.

For instance, I could have written..:

“The exercises to avoid for your body type and drastically reduce your risk of injury...”


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 13 -->

...But what does it mean to be injured to your reader?

It means time away from the gym which, if you know a couple gym heads, is a straight ticket to depression.

This won’t be obvious to all of your readership though so I would add “..so you never have to take time away from training.”

The other implied benefit is better progress, but this one will be obvious to 100% of your readership.

Get as specific as possible with your benefits until it doesn’t make sense to get more specific.

Mind the context, know your reader and use your intuition (and common sense).

Now let’s get back to our example.

Let’s say you’re writing a sales letter for a strength training coach.

In the program, the coach shows a ‘trick’ he’s learned at a powerlifting seminar with a legend of the sport. He was shown a hip flexor stretch - called a ‘couch stretch’ - that allowed every participant to add at least 25 pounds to their deadlift personal best on that day.

Here’s how you could write a fascination around it.

First of all, you want to stay away from ‘flat’ bullets, like..

“This stretch will increase your deadlift.”

We have a vague benefit here. It’s not specific, which makes it less believable.

So that’s step 1: We add specificity.

We would write...

“This stretch will add 25 pounds to your deadlift on your next workout.”

Now that’s better!


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 14 -->

But that’s quite the claim, so we need to make it more believable.

And that’s step 2: We add credibility.

A good way to do this is to use your unique mechanism, like...

“The ‘couch stretch’ will add 25 pounds to your deadlift on your next workout!”

Not bad, but we can do better. We can add authority, in that case, but most of all, we can make it more intriguing.

Step 3 : We add curiosity.

For instance...

“Powerlifting legend Ed Coan - who holds 71 world records
- shares his unconventional ‘couch stretch’ that is guaranteed to add 25 pounds to your deadlift on your next workout.”

Now we’re talking.

Your reader has:

1. The instant benefit of increasing his deadlift on his next workout
2. The intrigue of how he can do it with an unconventional “couch stretch”
3. The credibility of Ed Coan who holds 71 world records in powerlifting

And that’s exactly how you tease without revealing too much.

Now before we wrap this up, I’ll ‘reverse-engineer’ how I crafted the bullets for today’s email, so you understand the kind of thinking that goes on behind the scenes.

- ‘Discover the 22 hard-hitting ‘Bullet Formulas’ used by million-dollar Copywriters and how to combine them for maximum impact’

This one is obvious: The easy path for writing your bullets is to look for anything that is numbered. This is the ‘meat and potatoes’ of today’s lesson, which is why it’s first in line.


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 15 -->

- ‘The 3-part blueprint that turns ordinary bullets into ‘Fascinations’ that can outperform entire sales letters’

This is one evident as well. Everything that has numbered steps is a good candidate for a bullet.

It refers to the 3 steps on pages 13 and 14.

- ‘Master the art of ‘Teasing without revealing too much’ and make your readers obsessed with uncovering the rest of your Copy’

This is peppered throughout the lesson and the bullets. A simple way to do that is to give an intriguing name to a specific approach you have.

See below.

- ‘5 golden rules to protect you from the dreaded ‘Boring Bullet Syndrome’ that costs you thousands in sales’

The 5 rules can be found on pages 2 and 3. Again, anything that is numbered is a good candidate because it creates order in your reader’s mind.

Everybody loves clearly laid-out steps, so look for it in your product when possible.

- ‘The ‘Bullet Laboratory’ technique that generates endless Big Ideas for your Copy’

The ‘Bullet Laboratory’ refers to banging out as many bullets as possible before working on a sales letter - see pages 3 and 4.

This one was reverse-engineered from the benefit of finding lots of different angles and ideas for your Copy. I gave it an intriguing name to ‘tease without revealing too much.’

- ‘How to ethically ‘weaponize’ human nature and make your offer irresistible’

This one - although not explicitly laid out in the lesson - refers to our irrepressible tendency to give in to curiosity..


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 16 -->

...Which is exactly what we’re doing by adding curiosity on top of a benefit.

- ‘The ‘Assumption Assassin’ technique that destroys objections before they form’

This one refers to bullet formula n°8 on page 7. Again, I named it in order to create some intrigue around it and give it some kind of ‘proprietary’ feel, some kind of unique mechanism.

Every time I name something, I think of metaphors to make it visual so that it ‘speaks’ to my reader.

You’ll also notice I implement - when possible - figures of speech like ‘assonances’ and ‘alliterations.’

In simple terms: I try to make the name ‘sing’ with rhymes and similar sounds. Not only does it sound and look better, it also sticks in your reader’s mind better.

There you have it: My behind-the-scenes thinking for writing today’s bullets.

Now here’s a challenge for you: Read every email from this again and spot what each bullet refers to..

How To Write Killer Bullets

You can study the greats like I mentioned on page 4. The swipes from this training are good resources too.

But most of all...

You need to write a ton of bullets to write good bullets.

And the good thing is that you can get started right now!

Check out today’s exercises and let me know what you think.


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 17 -->

### Summary

‘Fascinations’ are short, compelling statements that grab attention and build curiosity.

They break down complex ideas and tease benefits without revealing too much.

They act as ‘mini-headlines’ throughout your Copy that serve as ‘attachment points’ to hook a wider audience because they appeal to various interests.

Fascinations have 3 elements to them:

1. Specific benefit
2. Short timeframe (ideally)
3. Intense curiosity

Here are my 5 rules for writing them:

1. Keep them short and sweet
2. Focus on benefits
3. Make them intriguing
4. Get creative with structure and language
5. Start with your strongest bullets

General tips:

- Write many bullets for each campaign
- Study great Copywriters like Mel Martin, Ben Settle, Gary Bencivenga
- Use a mix of bullet formulas for variety
- Make sure each bullet is compelling and relevant to your audience
- Balance specificity with intrigue (tease without revealing too much)

How to use bullets effectively:

1. Ask yourself "so what?" after each block of words
2. Get specific with benefits (but keep context in mind)
3. Add credibility to make bold claims more believable
4. Incorporate curiosity to make bullets more intriguing


<!-- Source: Day 14 - Fascinating Bullets.pdf; page 18 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

There’s nothing like having people open your emails just because they like and trust you...

But before you reach that stage, learning how to craft a killer subject line must be your top priority.

What makes a ‘killer subject line?’

Short answer: Intrigue & specific benefit.

Long answer: Coming up in tomorrow’s lesson...

You’ll receive my simple 3-word formula that transforms boring subject lines into bombs of curiosity that get opened...

You'll discover how to stay away from ‘Poor Man’s Intrigue’ that’s plaguing too many Copywriters today...

As well as my favorite techniques, such as the ‘Million Dollar Question’ that unlocks irresistible subject lines...

...Or the ‘Congruence Code,’ that’ll make sure you always deliver on your promise.

I'll also reveal the surprising truth about open rates and why obsessing over them is killing your sales.

You don’t want to miss this one.
