## Day 15: Irresistible Subject Lines V2

<!-- Source: Day 15 - Irresistible Subject Lines V2.pdf; page 1 -->

Your email subject line is the most important part of your email... Right?

Wrong! It’s the sender.

It’s the first thing your reader will be looking at.

We are emotional beings. We see things through the lens of our emotions. And when we like somebody, we’re going to see everything they do positively.

So as long as you have a good relationship with your reader, he will open your email no matter what.

Your subject line is the second most important part of your email.

- A fair open rate is around 25%.
- A good open rate is around 40%.

Anything above is very good.

As a rule of thumb, the higher your open rate, the more you sell, because more people read your copy. But when you send emails every day, your open rates will be lower...

...And that’s ok. Because you can still make a lot of sales with lower open rates.

So don’t obsess over it.

Now, to craft a good subject line, I like to use the ABC method:

Audience - Benefit - Curiosity

You call out your target audience, offer them a benefit and draw them in with curiosity.

It is simple and it works.

Too many copywriters today use what we call ‘Poor Man’s Intrigue...’

...When they try to veil something in mystery but it ends up being too vague and boring, like...


<!-- Source: Day 15 - Irresistible Subject Lines V2.pdf; page 2 -->

“How THIS made me $100k last month”

This is very poor.

Because the word ‘this’ as a standalone doesn’t mean much. It’s overused. And it doesn’t work.

There’s a simple fix in this case. You make it more specific, while keeping it intriguing.

What exactly made you 100k last month? What’s the mechanism? What’s the game changer?

“How adding ONE WORD to my subject lines doubled my open rate.”

Here you have...

The implied Audience: Copywriters.

...The explicit Benefit: Double your email open rate.

...The Curiosity factor: ‘One word?! what word?’

It’s even stronger when you can add proof and authority to your subject line.

You can use the names of famous people when your target readers know them. You can use their status. Or the name of institutions.

For example...

“Top US Doctor warns of this TOXIC ingredient.”

There we have:

- The implied Audience: health-conscious people.
- The Benefit in reverse: avoid this ingredient, or else...
- The Curiosity factor: ‘What ingredient?’
- More proof and authority: ‘Top US Doctor’

If you wanted to make it positive instead of threatening, you could write...

“Top US Doctor says this food lowers cholesterol levels by 3 points.”


<!-- Source: Day 15 - Irresistible Subject Lines V2.pdf; page 3 -->

Another way you can increase your open rate is to add an element of surprise.

I remember a subject line from A-List Copywriter Svet Dimitrov which said..:

“How deepthroat saved my life.”

While unusual and too spicy, this works. It fits his ethos, which wouldn’t be the case with a Top US Doctor. It would be out of character.

Your job as copywriter is to keep everything congruent.

And you can make your subject line stand out without being crude.

When I was writing an email to promote a supplement that helps with digestion, I wrote...

“How an unknown fruit saved me from constipation.”

Because there was a type of Indian fruit unknown to the Western world in the list of ingredients.

Your subject line doesn’t have to be outrageous, crazy and over the top. Because it’s going to be hard for you to keep your email congruent.

You will find your best subject lines after you uncover the ‘Million Dollar Question’ your reader has in mind...

...And turn your subject line into the ‘Million Dollar Answer.’

The ‘Million Dollar Question’ is the most pressing or valuable question your reader desperately wants answered.

When you identify and address this question, you effectively tap into your reader’s core desires, concerns, or curiosities. Which makes your email instantly relevant, compelling, and likely to be opened.

There’s no shortcut to finding the ‘Million Dollar Question.’

Lots of research and frequent chats with your readers are key.

Let me give you some examples.


<!-- Source: Day 15 - Irresistible Subject Lines V2.pdf; page 4 -->

Let’s say you’re writing emails for a fitness coach.

His audience is probably wondering: “How can I lose weight without giving up my favorite foods?”

Possible ‘Million Dollar Answers’ and subject lines could be...

“How I eat 1 pint of ice cream a day and STILL lose fat”

“Lose fat while eating pizza every night”

“Eat like a King & lose fat with the ‘16/8’ method”

Another ‘Million Dollar Question’ might be: “How can I build muscle without spending all my free time in the gym?”

Here you could use...

“20min 3x a week: Forgotten muscle-building secrets from ‘HIT’ inventor”

“The last ‘biceps formula’ you’ll need (High Intensity hack)”

“The ‘lazy’ man’s guide to gaining 10 lbs of muscle in 12 weeks”

“More gym time = less gains... What to do instead”

“1 set to failure: How I build muscle in <2 hrs a week”

Now, let’s say you’re writing for a productivity coach. The ‘Million Dollar Question’ is probably something along the lines of “How can I get more done in less time?”

Possible ‘Million Dollar Answers’ could be...

“The ‘2-min rule’ stops procrastination”

“The ‘25/5’ method to getting things done”

“10 AI prompts to cram 8 hrs of work into 2”

“Counterintuitive ‘Do Less’ approach saves me 10 hrs / week”

“Forget multitasking: the ‘single-focus’ hack will double your productivity’


<!-- Source: Day 15 - Irresistible Subject Lines V2.pdf; page 5 -->

Now let’s see why these could work.

- They address the ‘Million Dollar Question’ which makes the email relevant.

- They offer significant, (sometimes) quantifiable results (‘How I eat 1 pint of ice cream a day and STILL lose fat,’ ‘stops procrastination,’ etc)

- They use specific numbers to add credibility and set clear expectations (‘gaining 10 lbs of muscle in 12 weeks,’ ‘cram 8 hrs of work into 2,’ etc)

- They’re intriguing, they hint at a solution without revealing too much (the ‘16/8’ method is an intermittent fasting blueprint, ‘HIT inventor’ refers to Mike Mentzer, the ‘25/5’ method is the Pomodoro method, etc)

- They create curiosity by suggesting counterintuitive ideas which always spike interest (less gym time for more gains, ‘doing less’ to be more productive, etc)

- They imply exclusivity or insider knowledge (‘forgotten secret,’ ‘hack,’ ‘What to do instead’)

- They use proper jargon (‘lose fat,’ ‘gains,’ ‘multitasking,’ etc)

How To Improve

Now that you have a better understanding of what makes a good subject line, the single best way to improve will be to write a lot of them.

Every time you work on an email, write at least 5 different subject lines.

- Think of your Audience...
- Provide a Benefit...
- Spark Curiosity...
- And make it surprising.

Right now, you can check out today’s exercises and get started right away.


<!-- Source: Day 15 - Irresistible Subject Lines V2.pdf; page 6 -->

Another thing you can do is to study the best.

Here are some newsletters I recommend you subscribe to:

- Jim Clair
- Ben Settle

- John Bejakovic
- Chris Orzechowski
- Svet Dimitrov
- Justin Goff
- Kyle Milligan

Study what they do to see today’s lesson in action.

Take Ben Settle, for instance. He always writes something surprising and unexpected, like...

“Gary Halbert’s second best advice”

Most people would offer the best advice. And that’s become so common that readers expect it nowadays...

But the “second best”? Not something you would expect.

So study those subject lines with a keen eye and look for the clues I’ve given today.


<!-- Source: Day 15 - Irresistible Subject Lines V2.pdf; page 7 -->

### Summary

Subject lines are the second most important part of an email.

They’re crucial for improving your open rates.

A fair open rate is around 25%. A good open rate is 40%. Anything above that is very good.

The ABC Method for crafting subject lines:

- Audience: Call out your target audience
- Benefit: Offer a clear benefit
- Curiosity: Draw readers in with intrigue

Key principles:

- Be specific and intriguing but keep them relevant
- Avoid vague ‘Poor Man's Intrigue’
- Add proof and authority when possible by using names of famous people or institutions
- Incorporate an element of surprise, try unexpected angles
- Keep subject lines congruent with your brand & ethos
- Address the "Million Dollar Question" your reader has
- Always write at least 5 subject lines for each email

Common mistakes to avoid:

- Overusing vague terms like ‘THIS’ or ‘SECRET’
- Being outrageous for the sake of it
- Being incongruent with your brand / ethos
- Focusing solely on curiosity without providing value


<!-- Source: Day 15 - Irresistible Subject Lines V2.pdf; page 8 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

As soon as someone lands on your sales page, it’ll take them around 3 seconds to decide whether they’ll read it or close the tab.

And in this very short time, you must make your reader feel...

...Like this was written just for him...

...That reading it will be valuable...

...And that he has to read it RIGHT NOW.

Quite the task, I know...

..Which is why your headline is so important! Because it can do all 3 at the same time.

And I’ll be showing you how to do just that tomorrow.

You’ll receive my 3 and 4-letters formulas that let you write headlines so magnetic your reader cannot look away...

I’ll show you how you can harness human psychology to draw your reader in without being manipulative...

And I’ll share with you some of my favorite techniques, like the ‘Selected Initiates’ method that makes readers feel special before they even engage with your letter...

...Or the ‘Quantified Outcome’ technique that makes even the boldest claims believable.

I’ll also show you how to use ‘Odd Numbers’ and ‘Power Words’ without being cringe or sounding like everyone else.

You’ll be left with a clear method for writing banger headlines that get your Copy read.

It’s just a matter of practice after that.

Your friend,

[Handwritten signature]

## Day 16: Attention-Catching Headlines

<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 1 -->

Tesla performed the best marketing stunt of 2023.

The title of the short, carefully staged video said it all:

“Cybertruck beats Porsche 911 while towing a 911”

Tesla couldn’t have thought of a better way to showcase the beastly horse power of its new truck.

Indeed, the Tesla Cybertruck can reach 100km/h in 2.6 seconds...

.Which is about the same time your reader will spend on your page before he chooses to read it or close the tab.

This is why your headline is so important.

It has one job and one job only: to hook your target reader in.

And to do that, it should address the #1 problem keeping your reader up at night.

Similar to email subject lines, I like to follow the ABC Method:

Audience - Benefit - Curiosity

You don’t have to call out your audience explicitly.

But the reader has to know straight away that this was written specifically for him.

Your headline has to provide a benefit.

This is a non-negotiable. It can be framed in the positive as something to gain, or in the negative as something to avoid.

Your reader is always asking himself “what’s in it for me?” before he engages with any piece of writing. Make him feel that reading yours will be worth his while.

Your headline will draw more people in when you can spark their curiosity.


<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 2 -->

You can draw some kind of mystery around your offer by labeling it a certain way or use words like ‘counterintuitive, unconventional, weird, forgotten, lost...’

Similarly, you can think of the 4Us when crafting your headlines:

Urgent - Useful - Unique - Ultra-specific

Adding an element of urgency to your headline will increase readership.

You can use a looming threat, an awaited solution to a painful problem or a time-sensitive opportunity that may disappear soon...

Because you want your reader to feel like he has to read it right now.

A clear promise/benefit adds usefulness.

Your headline can include your unique mechanism. The goal is to be unique so you stand out and spark curiosity.

Ultra-specific relates to many things: your target reader, the problem they face, your unique solution and the clear benefit.

Remember that people draw general conclusions from specific examples - not the other way around.

Always be as specific as possible.

Here’s A Practical Breakdown

At Last: Undercover Copy Chief Reveals Industry Secrets To Selected Initiates In Private “Speakeasy”...

Allowing Them To “Steal” Direct Response’s Most Prized Methods And Learn How To Write Winning Copy In Mere Weeks Rather Than Years

This is the headline from the sales letter to the previous version of this training.


<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 3 -->

I’m not saying it’s perfect, but it serves the purpose of what we’re doing here.

Let’s see...

- Audience: ‘Copy Chief’ indicates this will be of interest to copywriters

- Benefit: “Steal Direct Reponse’s Most Prized Methods And Learn How To Write Winning Copy In Mere Weeks Rather Than Years”

- Curiosity: the combination of ‘Undercover Copy Chief,’ ‘Industry Secrets’ and ‘Private Speakeasy’

- Urgent: ‘At Last’ makes it seem like people have been requesting such an opportunity

- Useful: “Steal Direct Reponse’s Most Prized Methods And Learn How To Write Winning Copy In Mere Weeks Rather Than Years”

- Unique: ‘Selected Initiates In Private Speakeasy’

- Ultra-specific: the whole headline is ultra-specific with its target audience, solution and promise.

, . Here’s Some More Advice

A very specific promise within a quantified outcome always works. Use odd numbers and make them believable.

As a formula, it would look like this:

How To [Achieve Desired Outcome] In [Timeframe]

“Here’s How We Made $197,327/mo In 11 Months Using The Growth Partner Model”

You can start your headline with a question your reader asks himself and follow up with a hint at the answer.

Think of the ‘Million Dollar Question’ and ‘Million Dollar Answer’ from the previous lesson.


<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 4 -->

“What Do Top Sales Reps Have In Common? America’s #1 Sales Trainer Shares Their 5 Unconventional Traits”

Introducing the #1 problem can set the stage for your solution.

“Sleeping Less Than 8 Hours A Night Kills Your Gains In The Gym.. Unless You Learn To Harness This Secret Self-Hypnosis Method Used By Navy SEALs: The Fittest Men On Earth.”

This should go without saying, but always go for an authentic headline that accurately describes your content. If you don’t, not only will your Copy feel underwhelming, you will damage your reputation.

On the same note, make sure your headline aligns with your brand voice and the tone of your content. Ensure it feels cohesive and consistent.

Don’t be afraid to A/B test your headlines. Experiment with different angles and different formulas. Only when you find a winning one can you experiment with changing a word.

How To Use ‘Power Words’

‘Power Words’ are specific words that trigger an emotional response in your reader. They can significantly increase readership and your conversion rates.

I’ll go over 8 emotional triggers, give you a handful of examples, and general guidance on when to use them.

1. Fear / Anxiety

Words like Warning, Danger, Risk, Threat, Beware, Alarming, Crisis, Catastrophe, etc.

Use them when you want to highlight the negative consequences of inaction, create urgency around a problem, or emphasize the importance of taking preventive measures.


<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 5 -->

2. Desire / Aspiration

Words like Exclusive, Premium, Elite, Luxury, VIP, Privileged, Limitless, etc.

Use them to promote high-end products, to offer exclusive access, and to appeal to your reader’s desire for status.

3. Curiosity

Words like Secret, Revealed, Counterintuitive, Discover, Uncover, Hidden, Mystery, Behind-the-scenes, Insider, etc.

Use them to tease valuable and rare information, create intrigue around your solution, or when you’re promising to reveal little-known facts or methods.

4. Anger / Frustration

Words like Revenge, Fight back, Exposed, Outrageous, Scandal, Corrupt, Unfair, Shocking, etc.

Use them when to address common frustrations or injustices, to support a cause or rally people against a common enemy, or to expose deceit.

5. Trust / Safety

Words like Guaranteed, Proven, Trusted, Authentic, Certified, Secure, Safe, Reliable, etc.

Use them to build credibility for your solution, to address concerns or objections, or to emphasize the reliability of your offer.

6. Urgency / Scarcity

Words like Limited, Now, Today, Instant, Quick, Fast, Deadline, Before it's gone, etc.

Use them to create a sense of time pressure, to emphasize limited availability, and to encourage immediate action.


<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 6 -->

7. Ease / Convenience

Words like Easy, Simple, Effortless, Quick, Hassle-free, X-Minute, Convenient, Automatic, Streamlined, etc.

Use them to highlight the simplicity of your solution, to appeal to the desire for convenience, or to address concerns about difficulty or time commitment.

8. Empowerment

Words like You, Your, Yourself, Control, Power, Freedom, Independence, Choice, etc.

Use them to put your reader at the center of the narrative, to emphasize personal agency and control, and to appeal to the desire for autonomy and self-determination.

Now, remember this: when it comes to ‘Power Words,’ moderation and authenticity are key. You want to use them mindfully, with a specific purpose in mind, after you understand what motivates your audience.

Overuse will lead to skepticism.

Also, you want to stay away from vague, overused words that don’t mean a thing anymore and stink of ChatGPT.

Things like ‘Skyrocket Your Success,’ Unlock Your Potential, or ‘Elevate Your Brand.’

How To Write Subheadlines

Subheadlines support your headline and serve as a bridge to your body copy.

Here’s what they can do for your letters:

- Maintain interest
- They’re designed to keep your reader’s attention once your headline has grabbed it © And entice your reader to continue towards the rest of your letter


<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 7 -->

- Expand on your headline © They can provide more detail about your promise or main benefit © Or clarify any ambiguity

- Highlight additional benefits
- They can introduce secondary benefits you haven’t mentioned yet © And appeal to different motivations within your reader

- Overcome objections © They can address potential concerns and skepticism © Or reinforce credibility or provide social proof

- Improve scannability
- They break up large blocks of text © And allow your reader to quickly grasp the main points of your Copy

Just like your headlines, keep your subheads concise: 1-2 sentences at most. Use clear and specific wording.

Keep your subheadline consistent with your headline, whether it’s with your promise or the tone and overall style.

Don’t restate your headline! Use it as an opportunity to provide new information or a different angle.

Remember: Your subheadline works in tandem with your headline to create a compelling intro to your Copy.

Study the daily swipes to see it in action.


<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 8 -->

### Summary

Your headlines must hook your reader within the first 3 seconds.

It should address the #1 problem keeping them up at night.

The ABC Method for crafting headlines:

- Audience: Target your specific reader (can be implicit or explicit)
- Benefit: Provide a clear benefit (either gain or pain avoidance)
- Curiosity: Spark interest to encourage further reading

The 4 U’s Method:

- Urgent: Encourage immediate action
- Useful: Offer clear value or solution
- Unique: Stand out with a distinctive angle or mechanism
- Ultra-specific: Be precise in all aspects

Key principles:

- Address your reader's primary concern
- Answer ‘What's in it for me?’ from your reader's perspective
- Use specific examples to draw general conclusions
- Quantify outcomes with believable, specific numbers
- Consider using questions that readers ask themselves
- Introduce the main problem before hinting at the solution
- Use words like ‘counterintuitive,’ ‘unconventional,’ ‘weird,’ ‘forgotten,’ or ‘lost’ to create intrigue
- Balance mystery with clarity to maintain credibility
- Ensure the headline aligns with the rest of your letter
- Use ‘Power Words’ strategically to trigger emotional responses
- Avoid overused, vague wording
- Write a consistent subheadline that supports your main headline, use it to maintain interest
- Highlight additional benefits, overcome objections


<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 9 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Every Copywriter who’s worthy of the name knows it..

..The opening of your sales letter is the most important part.

But sadly, too many take it as an excuse to rush the rest of their Copy, blindly following the same old, boring structure:

- Introduce the product with grand claims...
- Anchor the price with overused clichés...
- Reveal the bonuses, if any...
- Add a generic guarantee, if any..
- Wrap up with a short summary with no clear next steps...
- And sloppily ask for the money.

They’ll sprinkle in some weak bullets and random testimonials for good measure and call it a day.

No wonder they don’t make sales: They get their reader excited only to lead them into the ‘Valley of Disappointment’ from which there’s no coming back.

But it doesn’t have to be like that, because not only can you make your Offer Sections both compelling and captivating to your reader...

..They can be a joy to write, too.

And that’s what we’ll be covering tomorrow.

You’ll discover a counterintuitive way to brush off your reader’s strongest objection straight away...

..By turning your main weakness into your major selling point, a.k.a. the ‘Objection Flip...’

I’ll share my favorite ‘Price Anchoring’ techniques that make even the most expensive offers feel like a steal...

You’ll get a taste of an advanced NLP technique that ‘rewires’ your reader’s brain as he reads your Copy, covertly allowing you to influence their feelings...


<!-- Source: Day 16 - Attention-Catching Headlines.pdf; page 10 -->

I’ll show you how to properly use ‘Win-Win Guarantees’ that not only win over your reader’s heart...

But also shield yourself from refund requests.

You’ll see how you can motivate your prospect to buy RIGHT NOW thanks to the ‘Path Clearing’ technique...

And transform boring FAQs into compelling ‘Mini-Stories’ that people actually read.

Tomorrow’s lesson will be your definite proof that ‘Offer Sections’ can be both interesting and exciting to read...

And write.

I’m sure you’ll like it.

Your friend,

## Day 17: Interesting Offer Sections

<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 1 -->

When you write 1 to 2 long-form sales letters a month, you realize that the offer section is the most boring and repetitive part of the process.

That’s because most Copywriters follow the same structure:

- Introducing the product.
- Going over its components.
- Showing why this product is unique.
- Adding a few bullets to build up desire.
- Anchoring its price.
- Introducing the bonuses.
- Telling the price.
- Presenting the guarantee, or lack thereof.
- Sprinkling some testimonials.
- Summarizing the offer.
- Going over the next steps.
- cTA.
- P.S.

And this structure, which can be tweaked, is fine. It’s the one most sales letters follow, meaning that it works.

But what if we could make it more interesting and fun to write, so that in turn, your reader also has fun reading them?

We can, and there are a few ways to do it.

Lead your offer section with objections

“Hold on.. Isn’t it risky to point out flaws in your own product...?”

Good question! Many of my students ask me the same thing, because they believe their product should appear faultless.

But here’s the truth: your reader is already thinking about these objections!

So you’re not introducing new doubts. You’re clearing up existing ones.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 2 -->

You become a master Copywriter the day you realize that weaknesses are nothing but strengths in disguise.

By this, I mean that you will never be able to hide an issue, especially if it’s a big one. Pretending that it doesn’t exist doesn't make it disappear.

In fact, when your reader ultimately finds out, you’ll lose their trust and respect.

That’s why it’s important to follow what Joe Sugarman writes in his book Triggers: Raising big objections and addressing them.

This is why when I reveal my product, at the very beginning of the offer section, I’ll first raise the biggest objection I can find.

The one I know my reader will have.

If the product is ugly, if it sounds or feels complicated, then I’ll open with it.

But think twice before you do this. Never raise an objection that isn’t obvious.

There’s no point in undermining your product by raising an objection your reader hasn’t thought about.

Then, you turn each objection into a strength:

Feels complicated?

Yes, that’s because it’s a professional tool that has been repurposed to suit everyday users.

But it’s easier than it looks. Here, let me show you how this 9 year old child uses it blindfolded.

With this sort of objection, don’t tell them they’re wrong.

Tell them they’re 100% correct. And that’s why the product is great.

Then address the precise objection with an indisputable example.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 3 -->

As you can see, this is the ugliest product we’ve ever released.

But that’s because we hired two of the world’s best engineers to imagine it.. And we didn’t have any more budget for a designer.

It’s not pretty, but it solves your problem 100% of the time.

When addressing an objection, it’s good to use humor or wits.

It’s actually one of the rare examples when it’s OK to be self-aware and use self-deprecating humor, if and only if it serves the purpose of reinforcing the main promise: The fact that the product solves your reader’s problem.

Have fun with your offer sections. Keep the energy up, and find new ways of showcasing your product.

Here’s another way.

Tell them who it’s not for

With supplements for instance, it’s crucial to tell your reader who your product is not for.

For example, if it’s not recommended to people who take a certain type of medication, say it.

Not only because it’s the ethical thing to do, but because it can improve your results.

First, because people appreciate honesty.

Second, because it may answer a question they have.

Third, because if I say that this supplement shouldn’t be taken alongside blood pressure medication... I may disqualify people from buying it.. But I’m also qualifying all the others who had doubts about its effectiveness.

I mean, if my supplement shouldn’t be taken together with a medical treatment, that’s because it works. It’s not sugar pills.

See how we’ve yet again turned a weakness into a strength? Simply by reframing the objection.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 4 -->

There’s another counterintuitive benefit to disqualifying your buyers.

It allows you to engage your customers. Because you remind them that if they don’t engage, then there’s no point in buying:

I’ll tell you right now: if you’re planning on buying this product and never open it..

Do not buy it. I do not want your money.

This is an important thing to say when you sell a course and/or coaching...

..Because only 2% of buyers finish a <$1,000 course.

The majority of people do not even open it. Their brain associates the act of buying with solving their problem.

And if we agree that testimonials play a key role in your Copy...

.We can also agree that you’re not going to get many if your buyers don’t engage with your product.

Being honest and straightforward also makes you sound confident.

People hate needy salesmen. The desperate type. This applies to Copywriting too.

You want to sound confident, not arrogant.

The next way to make your offer section more interesting to read and write, is to use ‘Future Pacing...’

Show them a vision of their future

Future pacing is about showing them what their future will be like after they’ve bought your product.

It’s about allowing them to visualize the future in 3D. This means, through their senses: Sight, hearing, taste, smell, and touch.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 5 -->

Of course sometimes you won’t be able to use them all. But the more physical and concrete you can make their vision of the future, the better.

Now picture this...

A few years ago, a junior Copywriter was struggling with his sleep supplement sales page. He was stuck in the usual ‘better sleep, more energy’ promises.

“But these benefits are true,” he protested in my office. “Customers really do feel more energetic during the day!”

“I know,” I said. “But it won’t feel ‘real’ to your reader.”

“Then how can I make my benefits feel ‘real’?” he asked in frustration.

“Tell me about your favorite customer success story.”

He pondered for a minute or two before his eyes lit up. “Well, I remember this man in his 60s who wrote to us. He said he used to fall asleep on the couch right after lunch when his grandkids visited. But now he’s the one suggesting trips to the park, or starting pillow fights. He said his grandkids are now telling his daughter ‘Grandpa is fun now!’”

That’s when it clicked for him...

You always want to link your reader’s future to new and improved relationships. Whether it’s with their family, their friends, their children, etc.

When you sell a car for instance, don’t just future pace what driving the car will be like. Of course you can, and should, but it’s not the deepest benefit.

Being seen driving said car is much more powerful. Having people stop to watch it. Having random kids take selfies with your car, pretending it’s theirs.

Link the benefits to interesting and memorable human interactions.

The goal is to have your reader agree with this vision of their future. They need to ‘see themselves.’


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 6 -->

Just like you would want them to envision their future life in a new house they’re visiting for the first time.

While future pacing works well, you can also choose to literally rewire their present reading experience...

Rewire their vision of the present

...Meaning to influence them to feel a certain way towards your sales page.

This is more of an advanced tip, but to do this, you can use an effective neuro-linguistic programming (NLP) technique.

It’s called ‘Pace and Lead.’

- ‘Pacing’ means telling them about things they’re experiencing now, and know as true: “you’re reading this.”
- ‘Leading’ means telling them about the thing you’d like them to feel or think: “you think that this is an interesting lesson.”

In this example you would have a few paces, like 4 or 5, followed by an inconspicuous lead.

Let me show you:

So right now you’re reading this lesson (PACE), as you’re sitting comfortably in your chair or sofa. (PACE)

You’re taking your time to read it in full (PACE) because you want to make progress in your Copywriting journey. (PACE)

You might even be taking notes (PACE), as you feel this helps you better understand what’s written. (PACE)

And as you’re reading (PACE) you can feel a warm sensation deep inside your stomach (LEAD), as the meaning of this fascinating lesson feels clearer and clearer to you. (LEAD)

The keyword here is ‘inconspicuous.’

If you want this technique to work, you need to blur the line between the paces and the leads.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 7 -->

I mean, read the example again, and think whether or not you would have noticed the leads at the end if you hadn’t known what they were.

The leads should feel like paces!

When done correctly, they influence your reader’s unconscious mind without triggering its defense mechanisms.

You actually feel the warm sensation, and indeed agree that the lesson you’re reading is getting clearer and clearer.

Clear up their path ahead

In your offer section, you should also talk about the next steps your reader will take after buying the product.

Not just the technical steps, such as downloading the PDFs, or watching the videos...

...But the actual steps of their transformation. Such as the small wins along the way.

Tell them about their first day after using the product. Their first week. Their first month...

...Not just with benefits, like with future pacing, but with actual events or realizations.

Their path ahead should be clear. That’s what will reassure your reader and motivate him to take action right now.

Copy is about alleviating risk and uncertainty.

Creative Anchors

To make your price seem less than it is, you can reframe it using an ‘Anchor.’

Even if you haven’t heard this term before, I’m sure you’ve seen it before. You’ve probably heard that a product is just “the price of a cup of coffee a day!”

That’s an anchor.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 8 -->

During my time as the Copy Chief of a leading Direct Response company, one of the most common questions I got from junior Copywriters was...

“But... Won’t people ‘see through’ this technique?”

They absolutely will as long as you go for overused examples that scream ‘Tired Marketing,’ like the one above...

.._But when you use creative anchors?

Your product will seem like a bargain!

The best anchors often link back to the product you’re selling.

For instance, if you’re selling a supplement containing expensive ingredients, tell your reader how much more it would cost to buy every one of them separately.

Or how much time it would take to pick each ingredient in nature themselves.

You can also link the price to time and effort.

Imagine if you had to book 23 appointments with Dr Sinatra yourself...

How much it would cost you, and how much time it would take to take your car and drive to his Beverly Hills office...

Without even mentioning the price of gas, or the fact that Dr Sinatra is booked at least 13 months in advance.

Well, with this online course, you get 23 personal lessons for $2,34 a day.

That’s the price of the cup of coffee you’d offer him if he came to your house and personally taught you everything he knew.

The above example I just made up. But I’m sure you can agree it’s a breath of fresh air, and a cool twist on the old ‘cup of coffee’ anchor.

That’s the spirit you must have when writing anchors.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 9 -->

Your goal is to make your reader feel like your product is not only great and beneficial...

But so cheap and valuable you won’t even know you’ve paid for it in the first place. It’s all about peace of mind!

Think about:

- How much time it would take to do it alone.
- How much more expensive it would be.
- How much more painful and annoying it would feel.
- And how long they would still have to endure the pain that wakes them up at night.

Your anchor is unbeatable when you keep it true and logical. And because it speaks to our reptilian brain, it turns into an emotional decision.

Guarantees that get read

Nowadays Copywriters copy and paste guarantees. They’re all the same, which is why most readers skip them.

First, here’s a general rule:

- Front-end offer: Guarantee.
- Back-end offer: No Guarantee (most of the time).

That’s because Front-end offers rely on opportunity and abundance, whereas Back-end offers rely on results and credibility.

Offering no guarantee encourages personal responsibility and helps attract the more dedicated kind.

It makes your offer ‘serious.’

If you do have a guarantee, don’t just offer their money back...

Because it means there’s a world where your product doesn’t work.

Instead, think of a guarantee where they win no matter what happens.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 10 -->

Promise a realistic result, and if they don’t achieve it, guarantee that you’ll help them achieve it at no additional cost.

If you can’t promise results (such as with a financial offer, where it would be illegal..) you can offer extended service if the product doesn’t work as expected.

With supplements it’s harder to guarantee a result, but you can almost make returns free and unlimited, for a full refund.

Make it conditional to a specific benefit of your product.

If you’re selling a course on seduction, tell them they’ll get a certain amount of dates in the first month, or else you coach them personally... Something along those lines.

Always make it relevant to your product. Make it make sense. And never settle for a generic guarantee again.

Interesting FAQs

Most Copywriters treat FAQs as an afterthought, and it shows.

They end their sales letter with a boring list of questions and answers that nobody reads.

But the truth is, FAQs can be a powerful persuasion tool when done right. They’re your chance to address lingering doubts and reinforce benefits in a fresh and straightforward way.

Hence why I’m a big fan of inserting FAQs inside my Copy, to make them both entertaining and compelling.

Here are the 5 methods I’ve personally used.

1. The Rhetorical Question

You don’t have to wait until the end of your sales letter to mindlessly list the questions your reader has and answer them.

You can do it earlier in your Copy.

Check out the first page of this lesson to see it in action.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 11 -->

2. The Conversation FAQ

You can add questions and answers as a dialogue between two people.

This works best when characters are clearly identified in your Copy.

Do you remember the 4th Mover from Day 13 - A Winning Sales Letter Structure - the ‘Teacher?’

Having a ‘Teacher’ and a ‘Student’ within your Copy is a good way to seamlessly blend your FAQs in your sales letter. The ‘Teacher’ educates the ‘Student’ who can ask the most common questions your reader has.

Don’t worry though, you don’t have to go for this structure for your whole letter.

You can subtly add a short dialogue from a brief story and include a question and answer inside...

...Like I did at the top of page 5.

3. The Story-Based FAQ

Short stories - which can include a dialogue like my example on page 5 - are a great way to subtly address multiple questions your reader may have.

Here’s an example for a fitness program.

When Tom discovered our fitness program, he had the same questions you probably have right now.

Because he’s never trained at home before, he wondered if he was going to do the exercises correctly...

Don’t worry: Our app shows you exactly how to perform each exercise.

Tom had heard that eating well plays a key role in losing weight.

Of course it does: That’s why we include a complete meal plan into your program.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 12 -->

You can also blend FAQs and case studies. Like so:

“I thought losing weight was all about spending all my time in the gym and exhausting myself on the treadmill...”

That’s what Ben told me before joining our coaching program. But 12 weeks later, he’s gained 10 lbs of muscle, his knee pain is gone, and for the first time in his life, he can see his abs.

And he did it all without:

- Doing traditional ‘cardio’
- Giving up his favorite foods
- Training no more than twice a week with sessions lasting no more than 30 minutes

Here’s what Ben was doing wrong before working with us..

As you can see, you don’t have to list questions and answer them like a drone. You can frame them within a story.

4. The Behind-The-Scenes FAQ

You can also present your FAQs as insider insights, like I did at the top of page 8.

There are more explicit ways to do it. Here’s an example for a sleep mattress.

Here at SuperSleep Manufacturing, during our factory tours, our customers always ask us...

‘Why is your memory foam blue?’

Mark - our lead engineer - always steps up to answer.

But since he’s busy making sure our customers get the best mattresses of their life, here’s my condensed (and simplified) answer to this recurring question...

This allows you to answer questions from a position of authority.

5. The Expert Interview FAQ

Similarly, you can paste a short ‘interview’ inside your letter.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 13 -->

This is your opportunity to bring in another person in your letter for more authority. This short interview can serve a transition point to another section.

You can use it to introduce your product, a benefit, a bonus or the price.

It can be someone from the same company that complements your role, like someone with a more ‘technical’ background...

Or it can be a ‘guest.’

Let me show you an example.

Imagine you’re selling a Copywriting course and a fellow Copywriter has helped you with one of the bonuses.

Here’s how you could introduce it.

Now here’s the thing.. Being a great Copywriter means nothing if you can't find clients who'll pay premium rates for your work.

I recently discussed this with someone who cracked the client acquisition code faster than anyone I know..

.Here’s a quick chat with my friend Paul French.

Me: "Paul, you went from 0 to 6 figures in your first year as a freelance Copywriter. Most people spend years trying to get there. How did you achieve it so fast?"

Paul: "Here's what most copywriters get wrong: They obsess over writing techniques while completely ignoring the business side. When I started, I did the opposite. Instead of endlessly tweaking headlines, I built systems to attract and land high-paying clients.

I had no portfolio, no connections, and no track record. But in my first year, I made over 6 figures in revenue.

How? By treating client acquisition as seriously as I treated Copywriting itself.


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 14 -->

Paul has been kind enough to share all of his secrets in a book that we’ll be adding to your order - FOR FREE.

Here’s what you’ll find inside...”

When doing this, you want to introduce your expert as naturally as possible, and position it at a time when your reader is likely to wonder about something.

The interview should stand out visually, hence the horizontal lines in this example.

As you can see, you don’t have to save all FAQs for the end and list them like a robot.

You can - and should - sprinkle relevant questions throughout your Copy. They can serve as the perfect transition between sections.

And remember: it’s not just about answering questions...

It’s about building up your sales argument. And trust!


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 15 -->

### Summary

You don’t have to stick to the same old offer section structure everyone is using.

You can lead with your reader’s most pressing objection and turn a weakness into a strength.

You can disqualify non-ideal customers and build trust through honesty.

You should help your reader visualize their future with your solution. Engage their senses and link the benefits to improved relationships.

You can subtly influence your reader’s feelings with the ‘Pace and Lead’ technique. But make sure you do it mindfully.

Outline the next steps after the purchase AND describe the transformation process after your reader uses your solution.

Use creative price anchoring techniques:

- Reframe the price creatively
- Compare with alternatives
- Compare with DIY options
- Link to product-specific value

Add a compelling guarantee:

- Think of win-win scenarios
- Make guarantees product-specific...
- ..And results-oriented

Go for interesting FAQs:

- Integrate them into your Copy
- Use storytelling or dialogue formats
- FAQs are your opportunity to bring new characters for authority


<!-- Source: Day 17 - Interesting Offer Sections.pdf; page 16 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

A strong guarantee not only builds trust, credibility, and authority...

..But it can also be the tipping point that seals the deal.

Sadly, too many Copywriters resort to the same old recipe out of lack of creativity or pure laziness...

.And leave their readers with generic, bland, and weak guarantees that don’t mean anything anymore.

But tomorrow, I’ll show you how to make yours stand out.

You'll discover my ‘Narrative Guarantee’ technique that seamlessly blends into your sales story AND implies that nobody ever activates it...

Effectively shielding yourself from refund requests.

I’ll show you how you can leverage ‘Risk Reversal’ to make even the most skeptical readers BUY...

You’ll also learn when to use the counterintuitive ‘No Guarantee’ strategy that paradoxically boosts sales and customer loyalty.

After you read tomorrow’s lesson and practice with the exercises, you’ll have no excuse to not offer compelling and creative guarantees...

...And you’ll never settle with the old ‘Happy Or Your Money Back’ ever again.

Your friend,

[Handwritten signature]

## Day 18: Memorable Guarantees

<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 1 -->

Most Copywriters underestimate the power of a great guarantee.

And while it’s true that nowadays guarantees don’t mean much to anybody - because we see the same ones repeated everywhere...

wThat’s the exact reason why yours must stand out.

As we’ve seen, the more a market gets exposed to a certain idea, or technique... The more aware they become... And the less ‘genuine’ it feels.

When we say that Copy should NOT look like Copy, that’s what we mean. We want to preserve the ‘naturel:’ The inconspicuous nature of our discourse.

Remember: people love to buy, but they hate being sold to.

So when you offer a memorable guarantee, you shake up your reader and tell him to pay attention.

Before having a look at what makes a great guarantee, let’s first address the Million Dollar Question...

Should you even have a guarantee in your sales letter?

As usual the answer is...

...It depends.

To know whether it’s a good idea or not, ask yourself: “What do I want to achieve with a guarantee?”

On the Front-end, since the name of the game is ‘Abundance’ and ‘Opportunity,’ then we know we’re talking to people who want a quick dopamine rush.

Thus, we want to make our offer irresistible.

Does a guarantee make your offer more desirable? Most of the time the answer is ‘yes.’

Some companies are scared of offering a guarantee, but here’s what they get wrong...


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 2 -->

Their customers are not evil people trying to extract every possible dollar from them.

They’re not looking to cheat and lie to get stuff for free.

And if your product is indeed valuable and solves a real problem, then you should never be scared of offering a guarantee.

Now, there are cases where guarantees are less important, like on Back-end offers for instance. They can even be detrimental in some cases.

Because at this stage, the name of the game is ‘Certainty...’

Which means that people will pay much more to get a certain outcome.

So having a guarantee means that there’s a chance, or rather a risk, that they don’t get the outcome they want.

And that shouldn’t be the case because, how can you offer a guarantee on a result that’s already ‘guaranteed...?’

Let’s say you paid me $500 to paint the walls of your kitchen...

Would it be logical to offer you a guarantee? No, because at the end of the day, the walls are either painted or they’re not.

I can’t tell you that, in the rare case that I DON’T paint your walls, I’ll give you your money back...

The walls must be painted for you to pay me, there’s no other option.

So in these cases where the result is binary, you either get the outcome or you don’t.. So a guarantee doesn’t make sense.

But here’s an exception.

When you’re selling a high-risk opportunity, your best guarantee will be one that eliminates the risk for your reader.

But this comes at a cost...


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 3 -->

Because you’ll be taking on the risk yourself.

Painting a wall, for instance, is not a high-risk activity...

..But selling a house is.

Because there are many factors at play, from the house itself, to the market at large.

This is why real estate genius Craig Proctor came up with this great proposition... your Home Sold

Or I'll Buy It! 0 e™

You either get what you want: Craig sells your house...

Or you get what you want: Craig buys your house...

The only difference is in who buys it. You get your money either way.

This is a real guarantee, because Craig takes all the risks for you.

And if you’re thinking about selling your house, it actually feels more risky to not trust Craig with your listing, than to give it to him.

If you can implement a similar guarantee, if it makes sense for you to do it, then by all means...

Do it.

In any case, you want your reader to feel like it would be more risky to not take you up on your offer right now, than it would be to buy from you.

Now that you understand what a great guarantee can do and how it feels like, let’s see how you can make yours stand out...

But before we do, here’s a quick note on guarantees in regulated markets, such as financial products...


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 4 -->

When you’re selling a product in a regulated market, you want to keep 100% of your Copy 100% compliant...

...And that includes your guarantee.

In most European countries and in the US, guaranteeing results is

against the law.

But there’s a simple way to circumvent that without losing too

many people through refunds and chargebacks.

You simply switch from a ‘money back’ guarantee to a prolonged

service guarantee.

So instead of writing...

“If you don’t achieve X outcome, you will get your money back.”

You write...

“If you don’t achieve X outcome, I will offer you a full year of our service for free.”

How To Make Your Guarantee Stand Out

The #1 rule is to incorporate the guarantee into your overarching narrative.

You don’t have to mindlessly add a generic ‘100% Money Back Guarantee!’ section like most Copywriters do.

You can have a guarantee that correlates with the rest of your sales letter. And if possible, you want to make it logical.

For instance, let’s imagine you are selling a course where you’re helping men talk to more women without being your typical, awkward pick-up artist...

In your sales letter, you tell the story of how you’ve gone from being socially inept to being the heart of the party.


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 5 -->

Your mechanism relies on ‘Presence,’ a.k.a. body language.

And to illustrate it, you use the idea of ‘Frames.’

“Controlling your Frame is the way to achieve social adequacy and magnetic charisma.”

Here, your guarantee will have to be the next step in a logical sequence.

Meaning you can have a ‘Frame Guarantee...’

..Or use the same principles you’ve just taught them ON them.

The purpose of this is to make the guarantee seem natural enough to be accepted by your prospect...

Yet different enough so he notices it as well.

This lets you achieve the perfect balance between guaranteeing results and implying that nobody ever activates this guarantee...

Because you fulfill your promise every single time.

Here’s how you could ‘frame’ it.

I'm so confident in my system that I'll put my own Frame on the line.

Try the program for a full 60 days...

And if you don't feel a noticeable shift in how women respond to your presence...

If you don't find yourself naturally commanding attention in social situations...

Or if you haven’t transformed from the quiet guy in the corner to the man everyone wants to talk to..

I'll personally coach you for 30 days and show you in real-time how to hold frame in any situation.

Why? Because that’s what someone with true frame control would do - turn every situation into an opportunity.

See how natural this feels?


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 6 -->

How To Write A Memorable Guarantee

First of all, determine whether you need a guarantee or not.

Ask yourself: “What do I want to achieve with my guarantee?”

- Front-end: most of the time, yes, you want to convey abundance and opportunity
- Back-end: not mandatory, focus on certainty
- High-risk: you need a strong guarantee

Second, choose a type of guarantee based on your market and offer.

A standard ‘Your Money Back’ guarantee works for Front-end offers, where building trust is your priority and where your market expects it.

For regulated or sophisticated markets, you can go for a ‘results-based / service extension guarantee’ if your customer doesn’t achieve the expected outcome.

A ‘risk-reversal guarantee’ will work wonders for high-risk opportunities. Make your reader feel like not taking your offer is riskier.

Third, you want to ‘integrate’ it with your offer. Start by identifying your main mechanism or concept. Now use it in your guarantee.

Make it feel like a natural extension. Keep the same language. And make it part of the narrative.

Fourth, you want to open with confidence in your solution, which explains why you’re offering a ‘crazy’ guarantee.

You set a realistic timeframe and list the specific outcomes that your reader should be experiencing. And you finish with what you'll do if they don’t get these results.

Ideally, you tie it back to your mechanism.

Fifth and last, you add to your letter. Either after revealing or price, or you can weave it throughout your letter.

If these steps are still unclear, let me show you 2 examples...


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 7 -->

Let’s say you’re selling a fitness program for business owners.

Step 1: This is a Front-end offer on a competitive market, which means you need a guarantee to differentiate your offer.

Step 2: You can go for a results-based guarantee.

Step 3: Your client offers ‘Time Compressed Training,’ with 20-minute workouts that are more efficient than longer ones.

The keyword here is ‘time.’

Step 4: Write your guarantee...

You and I both know that time is money...

SO I won’t beat around the bush.

Give my 20-minute workouts 30 days. Log them in your app.

After those 30 days, if you don’t get better results than your previous hour-long gym sessions...

Feel more energized because you’re working out less...

And actually stick to the program because it only takes 20 minutes of your day...

..I’ll refund BOTH your money AND your time.

That is, the $250 you spent on the program PLUS $20 for every workout you completed.

Crazy? Nah.

I tell you what’s really crazy...

It’s wasting HOURS in the gym when you could have better results in a third of the time!

You could write it after you reveal the price of the program, or better yet in that case, you can weave it into your sales letter.

You can use the last part to transition into false solutions for the problem, for instance (the classic hour-long workouts).


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 8 -->

Here’s another example.

Imagine you’re selling high-ticket sales coaching on the back-end.

You don’t need a guarantee on the back-end. But the coaching is pricey and it’s a relatively high-risk opportunity so if you do want to offer one, it better be good.

You could focus on results, without promising specific numbers, and offer an extension of service.

The mechanism relies on quantifying your output (e.g. be in front of a client every day at 8am, 50 cold calls per day, etc) so as to make consistent sales.

Your client calls it the ‘Recurring Commission System.’

Here’s how a guarantee could look like...

I’m putting my own Recurring Commission System on the line.

Implement my methods for 90 days. Follow it to aT.

If after those 90 days, you don’t:

- Close deals more consistently
- Feel more confident when pitching
- Come out with a reliable system for generating revenue

You’ll receive an additional 3 months of coaching at no extra cost.

Every week for 3 months, I will personally review your sales calls until you’re hitting your target

Because - just like my Recurring Commission System teaches - consistent action creates consistent results.

Notice both examples have 5 elements to them:

1. A confident opening statement that shows conviction. If you can tie back to your mechanism or promise, all the better.


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 9 -->

2.A specific timeframe that suggests a clear testing period. It needs to be a reasonable period of time that matches your product cycle.

3. Measurable outcomes and observable results that are up to the consumer.

4. Optional but great for making it memorable: add a unique twist that goes beyond ‘Your Money Back.’ Relate to your product theme. Add a bit of humor.

For instance, a weight loss coach could say “If you don’t need to tighten your belt by at least two notches after 60 days, not only do you get a full refund, I’ll buy you a new belt in your current size.”

5. Close with the reasoning behind your guarantee. Make sure that it reinforces your mechanism and feels natural.

The next time you’re writing a guarantee, remember these principles:

- Keep it logical with your offer
- Make it naturally fit your narrative
- Don’t just add a generic section
- Make it noticeable, but not forced
- Imply that no one needs to use it

And obviously, make sure you can back it up...

Ultimately, you want to strike a balance between being natural enough to be accepted, but different enough to be noticed...

Strong enough to remove risk, but aligned enough with your story to feel genuine.

And when you do, you’ll make your reader feel like it’s riskier to NOT buy from you...


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 10 -->

### Summary

When to use a guarantee:

V Front-end offers: Use it to build trust, to make your offer irresistible and to convey abundance.

x Back-end offers: Generally less important and can be detrimental. Focus on ‘certainty’ instead.

V High-risk opportunities: Essential to eliminate risk. Risk-reversal approach works best.

The guarantee types:

- Standard ‘Money Back:’ Best for front-end offers, use it when the market expects it
- Results-based / Service extension: Ideal for regulated and / or sophisticated markets
- Risk-reversal: Perfect for high-risk opportunities

5-Step formula to write memorable guarantees:

1. Determine whether you need a guarantee or not.
2. Pick a type of guarantee based on your market and offer.
3. ‘Integrate’ your guarantee with your narrative. Make it feel like a natural extension of your mechanism or promise.
4. Be confident. Set a realistic timeframe and list specific outcomes. Say what you’ll do if your reader doesn’t achieve them. Add a unique twist if possible.
5. Weave it throughout your letter, or after you reveal the price.

Key principles:

- Make it natural yet noticeable
- Strong yet genuine
- Memorable yet logical

And most of all: Make your reader feel like it’s riskier NOT to buy than try your offer.


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 11 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Copywriting will make you a lot of money - no doubt about it.

But if you want to become rich, you need to move beyond the surface-level and peek behind the curtain, to upgrade your skill set..

And become a ‘Marketing Strategist.’

These A-players are among the highest paid in the field.

They’re the geniuses behind your favorite brands. They work in tandem with business owners to structure the company as a whole and make it more profitable.

And because they’re so valuable, they’re able to command premium fees, take their share from the revenue they help generate, and become rich in the process.

This, my friend, should be your long-term goal: Become so skilled, so valuable, that the market has no choice but to pay you handsomely.

But there’s no way around it. You need lots of hands-on experience to reach that stage. And lots of insider knowledge...

..The type of knowledge usually only available through expensive masterminds, or shared behind closed doors, at the senior level of the top DR companies...

..But that changes tomorrow. Because you’ll be receiving some of the best kept secrets of the industry..

Like the ‘3-part Business Architecture’ the biggest DR companies use to make hundreds of millions in revenue per year, almost on auto-pilot...

..And how you can use it to scale your clients’ business and take your cut!

You'll discover the 5 components of an efficient ‘Acquisition System’ that makes a profit from the very first day..

And you’ll learn the subtle art of ethically creating ‘Problem Loops’ in your products so your buyers keep coming back to you to fix them.


<!-- Source: Day 18 - Memorable Guarantees.pdf; page 12 -->

Not only that, you’ll discover more ‘hidden’ techniques, like...

The better way to make your front-end offers more profitable WHILE you keep them cheap enough to attract more customers...

...The surprising reason why ‘High-Ticket Only’ businesses struggle to scale, and how to fix it..

..Or why sending too much content to new customers is the fatal mistake that’ll kill your profit.

In the end, you’ll be left with the roadmap to quickly turn your clients’ businesses around and, in the long run, catapult your career to the top of the marketing echelon.

You definitely don’t want to miss this one.
