## Day 22: Liftnotes That Sell

<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 1 -->

Liftnotes are short emails with only ONE goal in mind...

To drive traffic to your sales page.

They never sell your product directly.

They sell the click to your sales letter or VSL.

Now, before we get into how you can craft liftnotes that prime your reader to read your sales page...

Let me tell you about where they come from.

Once you know the logic behind them, you’ll have a better understanding of how they fit into your online campaigns.

Liftnotes were invented in the golden days of direct mail marketing.

Their goal was to ‘lift’ response, hence the name. And they invariably did so: DR marketers noticed a 5 to 25% increase every time they added one to their mail package.

A liftnote is a short letter that usually looks like a folded piece of paper.

It’s slipped into the mail package, alongside the main letter, the order form and the brochure.

A liftnote stands out from the rest.

It’s short and not as daunting as the main letter. It’s easy to grab, easy to scan, and easy to read. It draws curiosity towards the main letter.

And most of the time, it’s used as an opportunity to persuade your reader with a ‘different voice.’

It can be a short letter written by another company representative, a happy customer, an industry peer or a person with authority vouching for the product...

For instance, if you’re selling a marketing seminar, an effective liftnote could be a handwritten letter from another big shot who says he’ll be attending and wants to see you there.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 2 -->

If you're selling a supplement, it could be the company CEO if the main letter is written by a doctor, or the other way around.

A liftnote adds another dimension to your sales argument.

It reduces risk, strengthens your credibility or offers another benefit.

And because this classic tool was so effective, it has naturally evolved to fit into our online campaigns.

Email liftnotes have the same purpose...

To drive traffic to your sales page.

The Anatomy of a Liftnote

It all starts with a strong subject line.

The advice from the training on subject lines - the ABC method
- applies here.

However, now that you have a better understanding of Copywriting, I’d like to get a little deeper into the topic.

So here are my 7 top rules for writing subject lines that get your emails opened:

1. Know their priorities.

Talk about your reader’s #1 problem and nothing else.

What keeps him up at night?

And as importantly, how does he talk about Tt?

You want to use his exact words.

Or like Robert Collier famously said..:

“Enter the conversation in your prospect’s head.”

That’s how you connect with your reader and get him to open your email.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 3 -->

2. Be ultra-specific.

Beginner Copywriters think that being too specific narrows down their target audience too much and repels buyers...

..But nothing could be further from the truth.

Instead, you want to be as specific as possible with your subject line.

You want to trigger a reaction within your reader’s heart or mind...

Which isn’t possible when you’re using vague wording like “take your business to the next level.”

3. ‘DNCT.’

My #1 rule for Copywriting - Do Not Confuse Them - obviously applies to subject lines.

In other words: be clear, not clever.

Don’t try to sound smart.

Don’t use words your reader wouldn’t understand straight away.

4. Be different.

Too many Copywriters use the same words and phrases...

AS a result they all sound the same, have no voice of their own and they never stand out from the crowd...

..Thereby remaining average their whole career.

You want to stay away from clichés and overused wording.

Things like “get to the next level..” “boost your immune system...” “supercharge your business...” “skyrocket your sales...” “achieve financial freedom”...

They’ ve been overused to the point they don’t mean much anymore. And they trigger your reader’s sales pitch radar.

If you want to stand out, you want to know your niche like the back of your hand.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 4 -->

Stay up to date, keep an eye on the market, and subscribe to your competitors’ newsletters.

And come up with your own way of addressing your reader’s #1 problem.

5. Keep it short.

Be concise and drop the filler words.

For instance, don’t write..:

“..This short breathing exercise will give you high energy for the whole day, every day of the week.”

Write this instead..:

“..5min breathing exercise makes you feel 20 again.”

This is short, specific and there’s an immediate benefit.

6. Make it pop.

If you want your subject line to stand out, you want to trigger something in your reader.

So you want to use words and adjectives that pack a punch and drop the weak ones.

Words can either be flat, vague and boring...

Or they can hit hard, trigger excitement, or arouse emotions.

So use action verbs, drop the adverbs, and lose the ‘very.’

Use tools like PowerThesaurus and experiment.

7. Be respectful.

“The customer is not a moron. She’s your wife!”

..David Ogilvy said about 70 years ago.

Don’t insult your reader’s intelligence. He can see through lies and exaggerated claims.

You can call out his problem in a softer, more empathetic way.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 5 -->

People are more likely to buy when you trigger positive feelings of hope instead of guilt, shame, and self-hate.

Here’s an example from a junior Copywriter I’ve had under my wing.

He wrote a liftnote for a biz opp offer with the subject line: “you’ll always be a victim.”

Would you open that email? And most importantly, how would you feel if someone said that to you...?

You obviously want your subject line to stand out, but not at the cost of damaging your likeability.

These 7 rules on top of the ABC method will drastically increase your open rates.

But getting your reader to open your email is only half the battle...

Remember: you want him to go through to your sales page.

My 14 Rules for a Powerful Liftnote

So here are my 14 rules for crafting short, intriguing liftnotes that get your reader onto the next step of your funnel.

1. Remember the Rule of One.

Only talk about one idea. Keep it simple.

Experienced writers can make 2 different ideas work in 1 email, but I recommend you don’t try it.

Instead, keep that other idea in a notebook and use it for another email later down the line.

2.Make a smooth transition from your subject line to the email body copy.

You want it to flow and make sense.

Think of the slippery slope idea from Joe Sugarman: the goal of every sentence is to make your reader dying to read the next one.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 6 -->

3. Give your reader what he wants and expects right away.

Start with a short sentence or at least a very short paragraph.

Your reader has no time for slow, boring introductions so get to the heart of the matter ASAP.

Bribe your reader into reading, make a strong promise or create captivating intrigue...

Anything that stops him in his tracks and gets him started.

It’s the reason why hookers flash their boobs at drivers on the side of the road...

They use their best assets straight away!

If telling a story, make it short, start in the middle of the action and quickly transition to the solution.

4. Make it about HIM.

Ideally, you should include a ‘you’ in the first sentence.

If that’s not possible, make sure you talk about your reader ASAP.

If telling a story about you or someone else, relate it to your reader straight away. Same for outside events, what do they have to do with your reader?

You want it to feel close to home.

5. Keep your Copy tight.

Keep it short, drop filler words, ‘kill your darlings...’

You want every word and sentence to pack meaning.

When editing, see if you can get away with wiping out your first 6 sentences.

Now read it again and see if it makes your email more punchy.

Most of the time, you’ll see it does just that.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 7 -->

6. Include fascinations to make your reader want to read more.

Those will be different from the ones you’ve included in your sales letter or VSL.

You’re not selling your solution yet, you’re not even mentioning it.

You’ re selling the click to the sales page.

But the spirit applies: You want to add ‘benefit-driven bombs of curiosity’ to your emails.

Also, they don’t have to be standard bullet points. You can include them smoothly in your body copy.

7.Write conversationally.

This is a big one that’s often misunderstood.

You don’t want to ‘write like you speak’ like some people say you should...

Otherwise your sales argument wouldn’t be structured properly.

Instead, you want to write casually.

Choose simpler words. Use contractions. And drop the formal linguo.

8. Stir your reader’s pain points..

But don’t be insulting and don’t belittle them.

Also, watch your assumptions, but cast a wide enough net at the same time.

You want to be specific but not so much that your reader reads you and thinks “no, that isn’t me at all.”

It’s a fine balance between the two and it only comes with time, experience, and knowing your reader.

9. Paint an ideal outcome in some shape or form.

You can future pace your reader and allow him to imagine his life once his problem is solved...


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 8 -->

...Or you can tell a short story that he’ll relate to and highlight a transformation.

If you’re going for the latter, be sure to make it short, about your reader and to quickly transition to your solution.

10. Back up your claims.

You want to add proof to all your claims so as to make your sales argument rock solid.

If you don’t, your liftnote will have the tone of ‘trust me bro’ and while it may still work with the people who know, like, and trust you...

You'll fail to convince the rest of your readership.

Use authority figures when possible. This applies to your subject lines as well.

Short testimonials are a great way to add credibility. You don’t have to go for the boring quote “look what our customers have to say about us...”

You can tell a short (and true) story of how one of your customers improved his life thanks to your solution and include it seamlessly into your body copy.

11. Tease your solution right.

This is key if you want high click-through rates.

You never explicitly name your solution.

You hint at it.

You talk about the hidden cause behind your reader’s problem, you hint at the answer or a ‘secret code...’

.And invite your reader to read more by clicking the link.

Your liftnote creates intrigue around your solution and makes a smooth transition to your sales page where you get into details.

Here’s an example of what I mean.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 9 -->

For one of my clients in the spirituality niche, I’ve written a long sales letter (60+ pages) that reads like a report in the first half.

And that’s exactly how I framed it in the liftnotes.

After stirring my reader’s problem or using recent events as catalysts, I continued with...

“You won’t believe what I’ve discovered in my research... click here to read my report on .”

Do you understand?

You don’t want to skip steps when you’re doing this. Your reader first needs to be curious about the problem or solution before you pitch him your offer.

Otherwise his defenses are up before he even gets to your sales page.

Here’s a caveat: You can explicitly name your solution when your reader is a past buyer.

You can also do it during the last days of your launch as your audience gets more familiar with your solution.

12. Include 2 links to the sales page.

I’ve found that emails with 2 links tend to perform better.

Include one link and your reader may miss it, add 3 and it gets too crowded.

Ideally, you want to have a link to your sales page around midway through and one at the very end.

13. Adda P.S.

This is optional and I don’t always do it.

But when I do, I don’t use it as an opportunity to summarize...

I use it to add urgency or more curiosity, and one last link.

14. Re-read your email from the POV of your reader.

And ask yourself “what is my reader thinking?” at each point.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 10 -->

This allows you to overcome objections that may prevent your reader from clicking your link or from reading the rest of your email.

Remember it has to flow and make logical sense.

This is your last and final step and it’s the most important one. It can literally make or break your liftnote.

Sometimes, us Copywriters spend so much time immersed in our niche and offer that many things seem obvious to us...

...But they’re not to our reader.

So always make sure your writing flows and your reader can see the cause and effect.

The devil is in the details and sometimes adding one sentence to connect two others can make all the difference.

Ask yourself “what does it mean?” “What's the implication?” “What’s the result?” and make the answer crystal clear.

These 14 rules on top of a bit of common sense are all you need to get your click through rates to adequate levels.

Now here’s a quick recap of what makes a liftnote that sells:

- A compelling subject line
- A short opening...
- ..That smoothly transitions to the email body
- A reason to keep reading
- A teasing of the solution
- Proof & credibility
- A strong CTA

Now that we’ve covered the core principles of crafting effective liftnotes, let’s dive into some real-world examples...

3 Liftnote Examples

Let’s get back to our public speaking coach, Sarah Angelier..

She’s had a successful launch with ‘Presentation Mastery.’ Her Welcome Sequence to her ‘Spellbound’ readers has been working very well too...


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 11 -->

..But that isn’t enough for ALL of her readers. Here are 3 liftnote examples she could send to her Non-Buyers...

‘Story-Based’ Liftnote

Subject Line: This presentation got him a promotion

Hey [First Name],

Last month, one of my ‘Presentation Mastery’ students came to me with a challenge.

A Key Account Manager was stepping down, and the CEO was looking to promote someone in-house to lead the company's biggest client account.

The catch? Each candidate would have to give a presentation on their course of action...

..And the CEO would pick someone based solely on this.

"I have to nail this," he told me. "It's a career-defining moment."

My student was up against 4 colleagues for the role. All 4 were good public speakers with years of experience.

So here’s what we did: We spent the following 2 weeks applying the ‘Emotional Anchor’ technique from the course to polish his presentation.

And when he stepped up to speak...

The CEO didn't just give him the promotion. He added a 25% raise and a seat on the leadership team.

Want to see the exact changes we made to his presentation?

>>Check out the full case study here

To your success,

Sarah

P.S. This kind of radical transformation is exactly what my students see when they apply the Presentation Mastery framework!


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 12 -->

Here’s why this liftnote would work.

Sarah shares a relatable, specific story with high stakes, that demonstrates tangible, career-changing results.

She teases the ‘Emotional Anchor’ technique and naturally leads to the case study.

The P.S. reinforces the course’s track record.

Here’s how she could leverage a timely event to sell her program...

‘Timely Event’ Liftnote

Subject Line: How Margot Robbie nailed her Golden Globes speech

Hey [First Name],

If you watched the Golden Globes last night, you probably noticed something fascinating about Margot Robbie’s acceptance speech...

She used a psychological principle that master speakers have known - and used - for centuries.

I call it the ‘Inverse Authority Pattern.’

And it’s the exact opposite of what most speakers do when they want to command attention.

The results?

- Instant credibility
- Natural authority
- Zero resistance from the audience

I’ve broken down this principle (and 7 others like it) in detail here:

>>See the principles master speakers are using

To your success,

Sarah

Here Sarah uses a current event as a hook and references a celebrity.

She creates curiosity about the technique by making it feel ‘contrarian.’


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 13 -->

There’s a short list of clear benefits and she teases additional techniques in a short, scannable format.

‘Problem-Solution’ Liftnote

Subject Line: The invisible barrier between you and TED...

Hey [First Name],

Your ‘Spellbound’ results tell me something important:

You’ve mastered the fundamentals of engaging presentations...

.And the people around you can tell you’re comfortable speaking in public.

But there’s a specific reason you’re not getting invited to speak at bigger stages yet...

.And it’s not what you think.

It has nothing to do with ‘expertise’ or speaking skills.

It goes deeper than this...

I’ve put together a detailed analysis of what’s really holding back talented speakers like you - and more importantly, how to fix it.

>>Read my analysis here

To your success,

Sarah

P.S. This is the exact thing that helped 3 of my students land TEDx talks last year.

Here Sarah starts by acknowledging her reader’s current success.

She validates their existing skills before creating intrigue about an unknown problem - all ina short, focused message with a clear next step.

The P.S. adds social proof - acting as icing on the cake.

These examples show you how you can use different angles to promote the same product...

.And how to easily have variety in your emails.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 14 -->

Here are the key principles that make these work:

1. Each email follows the ‘Rule Of One.’

2. They smoothly transition from the subject line to body copy.

3. They all tease the solution without revealing it.

4. They each create a different type of curiosity.

5. They use a conversational tone while building credibility.

6. They include an element of proof (student success, celebrity, multiple TEDx speakers.)

7. None directly sell the program - they sell the click to learn more.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 15 -->

### Summary

Liftnotes are short emails designed to drive traffic to your sales page.

They sell the click, not your product.

Key principles:

- Write a compelling headline
- Follow the ‘Rule Of One’
- Write conversationally
- Smoothly transition from the subject line to the body copy
- Start with a short, engaging opening
- Include ‘you’ early
- Make your email reader-focused
- Keep your Copy tight and get rid of filler words
- Add fascinations throughout to build curiosity
- Tease the solution
- Add proof and back up your claims

Remember: A great liftnote creates curiosity, builds credibility, and smoothly transitions the reader to your sales page - all while keeping the focus tight and the message clear.


<!-- Source: Day 22 - Liftnotes That Sell.pdf; page 16 -->

## Day 23: The Cart Abandonment Sequence

<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 1 -->

Some of your prospects will add your product to their basket and proceed to checkout...

But they won’t go through with payment.

And while around half of them are simply not ready to buy...

Others just need a gentle reminder to pull out their credit card.

And a persuasive Cart Abandonment Sequence is the most effective way to do that.

It’s a series of emails sent to a prospect who’s added your product to his shopping cart but failed to check out.

You want to remind him he’s left something behind and encourage him to complete his purchase.

A good Cart Abandonment Sequence lets you recover up to 15% of lost sales. Around half of the people who click a cart abandonment email end up making the purchase. Every sale counts and adds up to your bottomline!

But before we get into how you can craft a cohesive sequence using DR principles, you need to understand why your prospect didn’t buy.

This will help you come up with arguments to use in your cart abandonment emails but most of all...

~It will help you avoid the issue in the first place.

Here are the top 10 reasons:

1. Extra costs like shipping and taxes are too high.
2. The prospect had to create an account.
3. Delivery is too slow.
4. The prospect doesn’t trust your website with his CC info.
5. The checkout process is too long and / or complicated.
6. The prospect couldn’t see the total order cost up-front.
7. The return policy is too obscure.
8. The website was too slow, crashed or showed an error.
9. There aren’t enough payment methods.
10. Credit card was declined.


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 2 -->

You could also add personal reasons, such as their wife/husband not agreeing with the purchase, having their doorbell or their phone ring while checking out, etc.

As you can see, many things here can be prevented in the first place.

Many of those are not up to you. But you can and should advise your client on these issues when you see them. For instance...

- Make the website as professional looking as possible. Good design, like good copy, builds trust. Don’t neglect it, especially if your target audience is on the older side. Highlight testimonials, showcase guarantees and trust badges.

- Ensure every step of the purchasing process is fast, smooth, and free of technical errors. Always do a mock test before you launch.

- Make the checkout process as frictionless as possible. Don’t ask for too much info, for instance. Offer a guest checkout or accelerated checkout option. And if you sell a course, ask your client to create his account after the purchase.

- Have the price clearly visible. Make sure your buyer can anticipate it. No one likes hidden costs and extra taxes. It’s also why I like to offer free shipping when possible.

- Launch your product when you have it in stock or when you’re very close to receiving it. People are used to next-day delivery nowadays, they hate waiting. Otherwise you may do the convincing on behalf of a competitor who’s able to ship faster...

- Your return policy should be crystal clear. Highlight it, make it pop. And use words like “no questions asked” if it applies.

- Offer plenty of payment options. Credit card, shopping apps like PayPal, digital wallets like Apple Pay, “Buy now, Pay later” like Klarna.. The installment options are key for high-ticket offers. They also allow for higher average order value with products like supplements.


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 3 -->

The Perfect

Cart Abandonment Sequence

Now that you’ve done everything you can to improve the back-end, here’s how you can craft a persuasive cart abandonment sequence.

The sweet spot is 3 emails.

The first one should be sent ideally a couple hours after your prospect failed to complete the purchase.

Sales is very much prone to momentum and you want to strike while the iron is hot.

You can set the second email to arrive a day after your first one. Same thing for the 3rd and last email.

All 3 should read like gentle reminders.

There’s a bit of pressure for sure, but the tone is mindful and helpful, not needy or pushy.

Each email will be short: 70 to 150 words.

It has one goal and one goal only: get your prospect to return to his cart and complete the sale.

Here’s the logic behind the whole sequence.

Email #1

You want the lst email to be a little innocuous.

You assume the prospect simply forgot to go through or encountered a problem.

Think of it as a gentle tap on the shoulder and the tone of...

“Here mate, you dropped something. Is everything okay? Can I Pick it up for you?”

You don’t want the customer to feel like you’re only after his money. Remember you’re providing a solution to his problem, he needs you. So you want your email to reflect that.


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 4 -->

You want to offer to help him personally. Tell him he can reach you directly through email, WhatsApp or social media.

Make it feel personal and exclusive.

If you’re selling a physical product, tell your prospect you’re keeping his order aside specifically for him.

If you’re selling a digital product, try to end your email on a positive note. You want to motivate him to take action...

...So make him excited!

Email #2

The 2nd email introduces a little bit of pressure.

You still assume forgetfulness on his part while letting him know he needs to make a decision. You’re being nice but you don’t want to be taken advantage of.

This has the tone of...

“Look mate, that thing you dropped, it’s right there. Are you gonna pick it up yourself? Or do you need help? I’m not gonna stand here forever.”

You’re still being helpful by offering ways to directly contact you.

But time is ticking and a decision has to be made...

If you’re selling a physical product, you can tell your prospect that stocks are running low and you’ll still be keeping his order aside for him...

...But not for long, because you’ve got to make them available to other buyers who are ready.

For a digital product, using FOMO works well here. You can talk about how the opportunity won’t last forever, or tell him about successful clients who’ve gotten the dream results already.

You want to exude the feeling of “everything’s ready on our side, you just have to say the word.”


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 5 -->

Like you’re slightly bending your knees to pick his stuff up for him, but you cannot do it before he asks you to.

Email #3

The 3rd and last email is your bribe.

You're assuming that something’s wrong with you, this time.

So you give your prospect an extra incentive. And you let him know you cannot do better than this.

Something is still preventing your prospect from taking the plunge.

Your incentive should make it irrelevant.

This is your last and final offer so it better be a no-brainer.

Here are some suggestions:

- You can offer installments
- You can offer free shipping
- You can add a bonus as a gift
- You can offer a better guarantee
- If you’re selling a bundle of products (like a 3-months supply of vitamins) you can split it and portray it as a test

Your incentive has to make logical sense.

Which is why I’m not a fan of offering discount codes for the sake of it, like many e-commerce stores do.

Because it implies the price was too high to begin with and it downgrades your product to a commodity.

It makes you look needy. And you don’t want that.

You want the posture of “I don’t need your money, I want you to benefit from my product so I’ll make it easy for you to get started.”

I like to add an incentive that relates either to price or commitment and finish it off with a stronger guarantee in the P.S.


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 6 -->

The main incentive is your jab, the P.S. is your hook.

When you add it all up, you can craft a persuasive and cohesive cart abandonment sequence that makes sense and isn’t pushy.

Too many people resort to cheap tactics like offering a discount code straight away.

But in my experience, it’s better to use other means that are justified with a reason.

Common Mistakes To Avoid

Here’s what you want to watch out for:

- Being too aggressive: Never sound desperate or pushy. Start out friendly and helpful in your first email before gradually increasing the urgency.

- Offering a discount in the first email: Slapping on a coupon code right away devalues your product and makes you appear needy. Save your incentive - as seen above - for your final email in the sequence.

- Using guilt-inducing language: Avoid shaming your customer for not completing their purchase.

Assume positive intent and focus on making it easy for them to finish the transaction.

- Making your emails too long: Keep them concise, scannable, and laser-focused on a single CTA.

Long Copy doesn’t work with cart abandonment emails.

The Perfect Structure

To recap, here’s how to structure a persuasive cart abandonment email:

- Come up with a curiosity-driven subject line. Make sure it stands out and makes your reader want to open your email.


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 7 -->

- Remind your prospect of the product with your main benefit. Be factual and concise, one-line is enough.
- Give a good reason for reaching out. Otherwise it’ll feel like you only want the money.

With physical products, hint at limited supply and say you’re willing to keep his order aside for him.

With digital products, motivate your reader with future-pacing or use a case study. Instill FOMO by telling him about the passing opportunity and other successful students.

Don’t belittle your reader, don’t be derogative. Otherwise you draw negative feelings towards you and your brand.

- Add a visual of the product. It makes the product “real” in your reader’s mind.

For digital products, make a mock up on a computer screen, tablet and smartphone or showcase the cover of your ebook.

- Offer to personally help your reader complete the purchase.

If he’s got more questions before he commits, offer another, more private way of reaching you, whether that’s email, by phone or social media. You want it to feel personal and exclusive.

Be reassuring too, let him know you'll get back to him in a few hours tops (and make sure you do).

- Add a direct link to his shopping cart. Use a strong CTA. Make it frictionless, there’s only one step left!

Check out the next page to see how each email would look like.

Email 1 (2 hours after cart abandonment):


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 8 -->

- Friendly, helpful tone: Assume the customer simply forgot or encountered an issue
- Restate the key benefit of the product they were interested in
- Offer direct assistance to complete the purchase
- Single, clear CTA to return to the cart

Email 2 (24 hours after):

- Mild urgency, acknowledge the time that's passed
- Include a testimonial or social proof to build confidence
- Clearly state the next step they need to take
- Single, compelling CTA to complete the purchase

Email 3 (48 hours after):

¢ Open with a special incentive (e.g. installments, free shipping, bonus, better guarantee, split test)
- Double down on the guarantee in the P.S.
- Clear, compelling CTA to complete the purchase

Examples

Here’s how Sarah Angelier could write a Cart Abandonment Sequence for her ‘Presentation Mastery’ program.

Email #1

Subject Line: Quick question about your Presentation Mastery enrollment, [First Name]

[First Name],

I noticed you started the enrollment process for my ‘Presentation Mastery’ course, but didn't complete the checkout.

Do you have any last-minute questions I can help with?

If not, you can go back to your cart when you click here.

Just last month, one of my students used the ‘Emotional Anchor’ technique from this course to nail a career-defining Presentation and land a major promotion.

He was up against more experienced colleagues, but this strategy helped him command the room and seal the deal.


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 9 -->

I'd be happy to walk you through how you can apply these same Principles to your next talk.

Just hit reply and let me know what's on your mind,

To your success,

Sarah

Email #2

Subject: How ‘Presentation Mastery’ can advance your career, [First Name]

[First Name], I wanted to follow up about the ‘Presentation Mastery’ course you were interested in.

Our latest graduates have been seeing interesting results, like Emily who landed a coveted TEDx talk after applying the ‘Inverse Authority’ principle she learned.

I know how busy life can be, and this course is designed to fit seamlessly into your schedule.

You can work through the material at your own pace and revisit the lessons whenever you need a refresher.

Click here to join the program and become a more compelling speaker.

To your success,

Sarah

Email #3

Subject Line: A special arrangement for you, [First Name]

[First Name],

I've been thinking about why you haven't joined ‘Presentation Mastery’ yet...

...And I realized I might not have made it easy enough for you to get started.

So here's what I'd like to offer: Instead of the full program Payment upfront, you can test-drive the first module - ‘Command Any Room’ - for just $197.


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 10 -->

If you love it, the remaining modules will be split into 3 easy monthly payments.

This way, you can experience the exact techniques that helped Mark, one of our recent students, land a $50K client after his very first presentation using just Module 1.

Click here to start with Module 1 and see the impact for yourself.

To your success,

Sarah

P.S. I'm so confident in this first module that I’ll add this..

If you don’t get a round of applause on your next presentation after you implemented what you’ve learned in this module, I’ll offer you a free 30-min coaching session.


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 11 -->

### Summary

When a prospect doesn’t complete his purchase, it doesn’t always mean it’s a final ‘no.’

Sometimes it’s a ‘not right now’ or ‘I need more convincing.’

Here are the top 10 reasons why:

- Unexpected costs creeping up at checkout (shipping fees, taxes, handling charges)
- The dreaded account creation requirement when they just want to buy quickly
- Delivery times that don't meet their expectations
- Hesitation about sharing credit card information on an unfamiliar website
- A tedious checkout process
- Frustration from hidden costs that only appear at the last step
- Uncertainty about what happens if they need to return the product
- Technical issues that interrupt their buying momentum
- Limited payment options that don't include their preferred method
- Issues with their payment card being declined

Here’s how to make sure these don’t happen to your prospect:

- Make the website look professional
- Showcase social proof
- Ensure the checkout process runs smooth
- Give people the option to check out as a guest
- Be transparent about all costs from the start
- If possible, offer shipping that matches Amazon-level expectations
- Make the return policy crystal clear and easy to find
- Provide multiple ways to pay

The perfect 3-email sequence:

1. Email #1: The ‘gentle reminder’

Think of this as tapping someone on the shoulder who dropped their wallet. Your tone should be helpful and friendly, never pushy. Keep it short and sweet (70-150 words), focusing on:


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 12 -->

- A gentle reminder about their unfinished purchase
- An offer to help if they ran into any issues
- A reminder of the main benefit they're missing out on
- An easy way to get back to their cart

2. Email #2: The ‘friendly nudge’

Now's the time to add a touch of urgency, but keep it classy. Your approach should be:

- Acknowledge that time has passed
- Remind them why they were interested in the first place
- Include a powerful piece of social proof
- Make the next step crystal clear
- Keep the tone helpful rather than pushy

3. Email #3: The ‘final opportunity’

This is where you bring out your best offer, but remember - it's about making it easier to say yes, not devaluing your product. Include:

- A meaningful incentive that makes sense for your product
- A stronger guarantee that removes all risk
- A clear deadline to encourage action
- A logical reason for making this special offer

Here are some more guidelines:

- Keep each email focused and brief (stick to 70-150 words)
- Have one clear purpose per email
- Include visuals of what they're missing out on
- Craft subject lines that spark curiosity
- Maintain professionalism throughout
- Never use guilt trips or desperate tactics
- Save your best offers for the final email
- Make it ridiculously easy to complete the purchase

Remember: The goal isn't just to make a sale - it's to create a positive experience that makes customers glad they came back to complete their purchase.


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 13 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Here’s what most beginner Copywriters don’t realize...

When your prospect finally clicks that ‘Buy Now’ button, you’ve achieved something precious...

..Momentum.

He’s crossed the psychological barrier of spending money. He trusts you. His wallet is already open.

And this is exactly where most businesses leave massive amounts of money on the table...

Because the moment is ripe to offer him something else!

Tomorrow, we’ll be diving deep into the art of ethical upselling - where the real profit multiplication happens.

Simply put, an upsell is the ‘upper level’ of the product you’ve just sold.

It can be more of the same, or something entirely different that’s logically linked to your first product.

...Simply by understanding what your customer needs next - before he even realizes it.

We’ll go over the ‘Problem Creation’ technique that’ll make your upsells feel like a must-have instead of ‘nice to have.’

Most importantly, I’ll reveal the crucial first paragraph your upsell page needs to maintain trust and keep conversations high.

Miss this, and you risk making your customer feel cheated...

Get it right, and they’ll eagerly throw their money at you!


<!-- Source: Day 23 - The Cart Abandonment Sequence.pdf; page 14 -->

The truth is, ‘better Copy’ can only do so much for you...

But when you understand the natural flow of customer psychology and set up your funnel to match it perfectly...

-You’ll be well ahead of the curve.

If you thought cart abandonment sequences were powerful, wait until you see what happens when you add strategic upsells...

Tomorrow is definitely one of the most profitable lessons in this entire training...

...I’m sure you’ll enjoy it.

Your friend,

## Day 24: Upsells

<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 1 -->

The Upsell is a fundamental part of any sales funnel.

Upselling means to sell the “upper level” of a product. It’s a vertical sale.

It’s different from the Cross-sell, which is about selling a linked - yet different - product. It’s a horizontal sale.

The Upsell comes directly after a first checkout.

Let’s say you just bought my Front-end product. After you click on the purchase button, you would be redirected to an upsell page.

This upsell page can be long or short. It can be a video or a sales letter.

No matter the length or format, you need to follow a specific structure to make it work - which we’ll be diving into ina minute.

But first, here’s the one rule that’ll make or break your upsell...

The Golden Rule of a Working Upsell

Your upsell has to make logical sense. It’ll fail to convert if it feels out of place.

It can be anything, as long as it feels natural and logical.

It can be...

- More of the same product.

For instance, purchasing a single bottle of a supplement leads to a 6-bottle upsell.

- Better access to the product.

After purchasing a ticket to an event, you can upgrade your ticket to a VIP pass that includes private talks and sessions.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 2 -->

- Upgrading your membership.

Instead of paying $99 a month, upgrade to a yearly membership for a one-time payment of $599

- Same content, better medium.

After you buy a digital book, you can choose to upgrade to a physical copy and/or an audiobook.

It can also be a different product, but logically linked to the first one (so still vertical):

- Product A solves a problem, but creates a second problem that Upsell 1 solves in turn.

After you buy a printer, the upsell can be an ink subscription.

- Upsell 1 accelerates the results you get with Product A.

You buy a “Sell your Home” checklist. Your upsell can be a private consultation with a real estate professional.

- You turn a ‘Do It Yourself’ product into a ‘Done With You’ or ‘Done For You’ product.

You build a series of templates for small business owners so they can write a converting sales letter for their business.

Your upsell can be a course that shows them how to apply the templates, or even a Copywriting service where you write their Copy.

The idea is always to have continuity between Product A and Upsell 1.

If they do not want upsell 1, you can instead sell them a ‘Downsell:’ A lighter version of the upsell.

The downsell is NOT a discount. It’s important not to train your prospects to refuse your most expensive offers.

The downsell is simply a reduced - and less engaging - offer.

For instance instead of offering a $999 yearly subscription, you offer a $109 monthly subscription.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 3 -->

You’re not offering a discount - on the contrary...

You’ re increasing the price.

But you’re reducing the level of commitment.

With that said, your upsell can be a One-Time-Offer (OTO).

This means a one-time discount on an existing product.

For instance you could be upselling people your course for $79 instead of $149, but this offer is available only on this upsell page.

Stay true to your word if you decide to run with this.

Before we get to the writing part, here’s a quick note on the psychology of price anchoring...

When your customer purchases your front-end product at $97, something interesting happens in their mind...

That price becomes their "anchor" - their reference point for value.

This is why a $197 upsell often converts better after a $97 purchase than if you'd offered it first.

Because your customer has already:

- Committed to spending money with you
- Validated your value at a certain price point
- Developed trust in your ability to deliver

You can leverage this psychology in your upsell by:

- Comparing your upsell price to the main product: "For just $100 more...”
- Showing the relative value increase: "3X more content for only 2X the investment"
- Breaking down the incremental investment: "That's just $33 more per bottle"

Now remember: The key is to make the price jump feel natural and justified.

Just like your upsell itself, it has to make logical sense.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 4 -->

A Working Upsell in Action

Let’s see how these concepts work in the real world...

Let’s get back to our public speaking coach, Sarah Angelier.

Her front-end suite of products looks like this:

- A low-ticket offer at $6 (your entry point) © “Spellbound: The 60-Page Guide To Captivating Your Audience”

- An upsell at $97 (your premium offer)
- “Stage Ready: The Complete Video Course On How To Prepare For & Deliver A Presentation That Leaves Your Audience Asking For More”

- A downsell at $37 (for those who say no to the upsell)
- “Stage Ready Essential Video Modules: The 4 Crucial Elements Of A Captivating Presentation”

Let’s see why this works:

1. The seamless progression from written guide to video course feels natural.

After all, what’s the natural next step after reading about public speaking?

Watching someone demonstrate the techniques in action!

2. The products solve increasingly complicated problems.

The front-end product introduces fundamental concepts while the upsell brings them to life with comprehensive demonstrations and prep strategies.

The downsell makes it accessible to the more budget-conscious crowd.

3. The initial $6 investment creates an accessible entry point - especially with the 3 bonuses that are included.

The customer thinks “if I get ALL OF THIS for only $6, what do I get for $972”.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 5 -->

The upgrade from text to video justifies the price jump.

The more comprehensive upsell aligns with the more dedicated people who want to take it even further.

4. The downsell maintains the premium positioning of the full course, which wouldn’t be the case if Sarah had offered a discount.

It offers a legitimate alternative with reduced scope and allows Sarah to capture customers at a higher price point than the main product.

5. Each product stands on its own.

This is a big one. Every product has its own merit. The guide isn’t just a teaser - it’s a complete introduction to public speaking.

The upsell doesn’t just rehash the guide. It elevates the learning experience.

The downsell delivers focused value without feeling stripped down.

Each step is there to serve the customer’s growing commitment towards becoming better at speaking in public.

How to Write a Converting Upsell

An upsell is your typical sales letter or VSL, with a few key differences.

The overarching principle is consistency.

Your customers should think that they’re seeing a succession of offers on purpose.

If your upsell isn’t linked to their first purchase, then they will feel confused and let down.

So first and foremost - and this applies to a sales letter or a VSL upsell...

Congratulate them for their purchase and reassure them that they’ve made the right decision.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 6 -->

It’s all about acknowledging them, because the purchase they’ve just made feels significant to them.

If you ignore this part, they will feel cheated and undervalued.

So really take the time to say how much you appreciate them.

Then slowly transition into the next problem your product creates.

As I said, it’s a logical transition...

.Meaning that it needs an explanation.

If you’re offering them 6 bottles as an upsell, tell them the logical reason WHY they should stock up on so many bottles...

Is there a risk of shortages?

Is one of your ingredients so rare that you may encounter restocking issues?

Is there a war happening right now that makes shipments imprevisible?

It all boils down to justifying your upsell through highlighting a new problem.

Encourage the purchase of the upsell by focusing on emotions, benefits, and tangible results.

Then explain the unique mechanism, and highlight how this upsell is a real opportunity.

They need to be really on the fence, tempted to buy...

And they should finally crack and pull the trigger.

That’s where, for instance, you can offer your upsell at 50% off its public price.

Your prospect has already bought...

So they may be in the mood to spend even more money.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 7 -->

So with an upsell it’s about:

1. Solving a new problem
2. Bringing tangible results you can back up
3. Showing the unique mechanism

.All wrapped up in a great, one time offer

You can play with many different types of upsells.

For instance, if you don’t want to lower the price of your product, you can include bonuses as part of your OTO.

Now, let me warn you about the most common mistakes beginner Copywriters make with their upsells..

And how to avoid them.

The Most Common Upsell Pitfalls

Messing up your upsells doesn’t only mean leaving money on the table right now...

It means potentially damaging your relationship with your customers - which WILL hurt your business long-term.

Here are 7 pitfalls to watch out for:

1. The “Incomplete Product Trap”: NEVER make your front-end product feel incomplete without the upsell.

Your initial product must deliver everything you promise. The upsell should enhance those results...

.Not fix a broken product. Think of your upsell as a performance booster, not a patch for fundamental flaws.

2. The “Overwhelm Effect”: Resist the temptation to offer more than 2-3 upsells.

You’re building a relationship, not emptying their wallet. Your goal is to guide, not to drain.

3. The “Random Offer Mistake”: Nothing kills trust faster than an upsell that feels disconnected from the original product.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 8 -->

Each offer should feel like a natural next step - not a random money grab.

Use your upsell to tell a compelling story of progression. Show how it seamlessly extends and enhances the value of your initial product.

4. The “Psychological Gap” in upsell Copy: Many upsell attempts fail because they forget a critical emotional element...

...Making the customer feel good about their initial purchase.

Good upsell Copy does more than present a product - it starts by reinforcing the customer’s decision and shows how the additional offer will:

© Accelerate their results © Make achieving their goals a lot easier © Provide a more comprehensive solution © Eliminate additional steps or reduce complexity

Your upsell Copy should make your customers feel like they’re getting an insider secret or a professional shortcut - not like they’re being squeezed for more money.

5. The “Pressure Cooker Error”: Urgency definitely has its place when it comes to upsell Copy, but aggressive countdowns and clumsy tactics often backfire.

Remember that your customer just trusted you with a purchase... Do not abuse that trust.

Create a sense of value, not a sense of panic.

6. The “Discount Addiction Problem”: When you offer deep discounts too often or too easily, not only do you train your customers to wait for deals...

...You devalue your products.

So use One-Time Offers strategically, not as a crutch.

And focus on value, not price slashing.

7. The “No-Brainer Offer Principle”: Your upsell Copy should portray your offer as so compelling, so aligned with your customer’s desires, that saying “no” feels irrational.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 9 -->

This means:

© Demonstrating clear and tangible benefits © Showing how your upsell bridges the gap between where they are and where they want to be © Making the additional investment feel minimal compared to the potential gains

Remember: A great upsell doesn’t feel like another sale...

wIt feels like a gift, an opportunity, a shortcut to success that your customer would be foolish to pass up!

Understanding those pitfalls is half of the work...

..WBecause the real art of upselling is not about avoiding mistakes.

It’s about thinking of your upsells not as just another transaction...

But as a real solution to your customers’ problems that happens to build your business.

The Art of Ethical Upselling

Every upsell should pass this simple test...

Would you confidently sell it to your parents?

If there’s any hesitation, your offer needs work!

Because your upsell should trigger genuine excitement, not guilt about spending more.

Here’s another technique.

Think of each customer as a 10-year relationship...

Would your upsell strategy support that timeline?

To make sure it does, consider...

- How your upsell enhances their primary purchase
- Whether it moves them closer to their desired outcome
- If it strengthens their trust in your brand


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 10 -->

I’m a firm believer that you can always come up with an upsell that benefits your customer.

However, there are some rare cases where the most profitable long-term decision is to NOT present an upsell.

For instance, when your customer's success depends on mastering the front-end product first...

...It'll be wiser to refrain from upselling right now.

Because a successful upsell isn’t just about more immediate revenue.

It’s about:

- Creating genuine success stories
- Building brand advocates
- Generating referrals
- Having low refund rates
- Increasing customer lifetime value

Which will all, in turn, increase your revenue later down the line.

Long-term, the most profitable approach is often the most ethical one: Create genuine value, present it honestly, and trust that the money will follow.

This philosophy might mean leaving some short-term money on the table...

...But it builds the kind of business that thrives for years instead of months.

That’s All You Need to Start

Upselling

Your upsell could be half a page or 12-pages long...

What you say doesn’t matter so much, as long as your upsell...

1. Solves a new problem
2. Brings tangible results you can back up
3. Shows your unique mechanism


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 11 -->

The name of the game here is A/B testing: Experiment with different versions and see which one works best.

To give you an idea, you should target a 15-25% take rate on your upsell.

I can’t give you a more precise number because it depends on too many variables.

But anything less than that and you’ll be underperforming, unless you sell high ticket packages, for instance.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 12 -->

### Summary

Upselling is about vertical progression - providing an enhanced solution.

Cross-selling is about horizontal progression - providing a different, yet related solution.

Your upsell must make logical sense if you want it to work.

It should feel like a natural next step.

When it comes to upsell Copy, structure matters more than length.

Always start by congratulating and validating your customer’s initial purchase.

Present a logical transition to your upsell.

Focus on solving a new - or enhanced - problem.

Make your upsell compelling through value instead of discounts.

All your upsell should pass the “would you sell it to your parents?” test.

Think in 10-year customer relationships.

Aim for a 15-25% take rate on your upsell.

Always A/B test different versions to optimize performance.

And remember: The best upsells don’t only drive immediate revenue...

..They build lasting customer relationships, create success stories, and nourish a sustainable business that thrives for years to come.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 13 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

“Would you like fries with that?”

Six simple words that have probably made McDonald’s more money than their iconic Big Mac.

It’s also the perfect example of a “Cross-sell”...

.Which is offering a related but different product to someone who’s already buying.

While an upsell is about upgrading what customers are already buying (like supersizing your burger), a cross sell is about suggesting complementary items that make the main purchase even better (like those crunchy, perfectly salted fries).

Think about it - when was the last time you said no to that question? If you’re like most people, you find yourself nodding before you even think about it.

There’s a reason why fast food giants have perfected this seemingly casual question.

Because it’s not just about good customer service...

...It’s also about understanding the exact moment when a customer is most likely to say “yes” without hesitation.

And tomorrow, I’ll show you exactly how you can harness this same automatic “yes” response in your own campaigns.

You'll discover why the most successful cross-sells aren’t about pushing “essential” add-ons...

They’ re about making the perfect suggestion at the perfect moment.

Most Copywriters get so caught up in perfecting their main offer that they completely miss the golden window of opportunity hiding in plain sight...

.When your customer is reaching for their wallet.

At that moment, the right suggestion will always feel like a helpful reminder instead of a sale.


<!-- Source: Day 24 - Upsells.pdf; page 14 -->

We won’t stop there.

I’ll teach you the art of crafting downsells that people pick up instantly - without cheapening your brand...

As well as a simple fix to stop your cross-sells from cannibalizing your upsells.

And while other Copywriters obsess over their main product and only experience gradual bumps in revenue...

You’ll know how to take full advantage of that golden checkout phase...

...And you'll be stacking serious cash as a result.

By this time tomorrow, you’ll know exactly how to turn those natural “Would you like fries with that?” into serious revenue boosters.

Make sure you show up.

Your friend,

[Handwritten signature]

## Day 25: Cross-sells

<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 1 -->

A Cross-sell is an offer for a complementary - but different - product that enhances your customer's overall experience with their initial purchase.

The “fries with your burger” is a good example: It’s not another patty - that would be an upsell, a bigger burger - but it makes the whole meal better.

While an upsell focuses on getting customers to buy a better version of what they just purchased, a cross-sell introduces a new product that creates additional value in a different way.

Just like upsells, cross-sells come after a purchase. They usually come second or third in the post-purchase funnel, meaning after Upsell 1 and, if applicable, Upsell 2.

Your funnel could look like this:

jece ‘YES’ FSi hi cera

an UPSELE 2 ‘NO’™., plied [eco jece i Ea] paves Eis} ners PURCHASE UPSELL 1 i [eco eco H
- =O) Ege | ———. Aevsessnat

DOWNSELL wo” CROSS-SELL “a No’ =a DOWNSELL

Everything that flows from the dotted arrows with Upsell 2 is entirely optional.


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 2 -->

You can even have a second cross-sell, but I would advise against it if you already have a second upsell.

Where the upsell is vertical - meaning you get a better version of the same product, or a very close product logically linked to the first one...

...The cross-sell is a fundamentally different product.

The logical link is horizontal...

Meaning it supports the first product, but contributes indirectly to the overarching transformation.

UPSELL CROSS-SELL

- Same product line or e Different but direct improvement complementary product
- "Get more / better of e "Get something that what you just bought" works well with what you
- Usually higher price bought" point e Often lower price point
- Example: Ebook = e Example: Course + Premium video course Workbook
- Immediate logical e Indirect but valuable connection connection

For instance:

- You buy a joint health supplement and the cross-sell is a CBD cream.

It’ll help with easing the pain, but it’s not the logical conclusion of the first product. It still remains helpful and purposeful.

- You buy a car and you’re cross-sold a coupon for unlimited car washes.

The cross-sell isn’t a vital purchase. It’s a “good-to-have” and should be like the candies you see at checkout in the supermarket.


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 3 -->

- You buy a social media marketing course and the cross-sell is a content calendar template.

Here the cross-sell helps you implement what you’ve learned. You get a visual checklist you can quickly refer to at any time.

- You buy a fitness program and you’re cross-sold a recipe ebook and meal planner.

This supports your overall health and weight-loss goals.

Initially, a cross-sell was born out of a partnership between two businesses. They would simply link up their offers and share the revenue.

Similarly today, cross-sells should feel like opportunities: Really good offers that tempt you to buy, even though they're not essential.

Successful cross-sells tap into specific psychological triggers that make them hard to resist:

1. Appeal to convenience:

“Since you’re already here...” works because:

- Customers are in an active buying state
- Their payment information is already entered
- They trust you enough to have made a purchase
- The friction of making another decision is lower

Example: "Your cart is already open, and your payment info is saved. Want to grab this [complementary product] at our special customer-only price?"

2. Value bundling:

Everyone loves a good deal, so don’t hesitate to make a special offer on your cross-sell:

- Bundle pricing makes people feel like they’re saving money
- Package deals feel more complete
- Multiple products reduce the perceived per-item cost
- Bundles reduce decision fatigue


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 4 -->

Example: "Add our [complementary product] now for just $X more (regular price $Y) and save 40% with this exclusive bundle!"

3. Scarcity and exclusivity:

These triggers work because they tap into:

- Fear of missing out
- The desire to be part of a select group
- The feeling of urgency to act on limited opportunities
- Pride in accessing exclusive offers

Example: "As a new customer, you have exclusive access to [complementary product] at this time only. This exclusive offer is only open to customers like yourself who have already invested in our core product.”

4. Problem completion:

Customers don’t want only a piece of the puzzle. They want:

- Comprehensive solutions
- To avoid future problems
- To feel prepared for oncoming challenges
- To feel thorough and smart

Example: "Complete your [main product] experience with this essential companion [product] - ensure you have everything you need to succeed!"

5. Social proof:

Add social validation when possible. It will:

- Reduce purchase anxiety
- Validate the combination of products
- Show real-world usage
- Build confidence in the decision

Example: "87% of our successful customers pair these products together for optimal results!"

Quick note: The examples should NOT be used in your Copy...

They simply illustrate the spirit.


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 5 -->

A Working Cross-sell in Action

Let’s see how these concepts work in the real world...

Let’s get back to our public speaking coach, Sarah Angelier.

Her front-end suite of products looks like this:

- A low-ticket offer at $6 (your entry point) © “Spellbound: The 60-Page Guide To Captivating Your Audience”

- An upsell at $97 (your premium offer)
- “Stage Ready: The Complete Video Course On How To Prepare For & Deliver A Presentation That Leaves Your Audience Asking For More”

- A downsell at $37 (for those who say no to the upsell) © “Stage Ready Essential Video Modules: The 4 Crucial Elements Of A Captivating Presentation”

- A cross-sell at $199 (your related product) © “Voice Mastery Workshop: 1-Hour Recording”

- And a monthly downsell at $39 (a cheaper version of the cross-sell) © “Voice Mastery Foundations”

After customers buy her front-end “Spellbound” guide, they are shown the upsell “Stage Ready”. Those who say no will be shown a downsel1l...

..But whether they buy any of these two or not, they’ll be offered the cross-sell “Voice Mastery Workshop: 1-Hour Recording”.

Here’s why this cross-sell makes sense:

1. There’s a logical horizontal connection.

While "Stage Ready" teaches presentation structure and delivery, "Voice Mastery" focuses on a complementary but different skill: Vocal techniques.

It’s not “more of the same” - it addresses a distinct aspect of public speaking.


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 6 -->

2. It uses psychological triggers.

This cross-sell appeals to convenience - in the spirit of “Now that you know how to structure your talk, let’s perfect your delivery...”

It fills a gap that speakers often worry about - how they actually sound.

The value proposition is strong: A workshop recording provides concentrated expertise in a digestible format.

It doesn’t feel overwhelming - nor does it feel essential...

...Which paradoxically makes it a great addition.

3. Pricing and positioning are consistent.

While the cross-sell is more expensive than the upsell, it’s positioned as a specialized enhancement.

It suggests exclusivity and high-level expertise.

The monthly downsell at $39 is an accessible alternative.

4. The timing is right.

This cross-sell is the perfect addition for people who are already invested in improving their speaking skills.

It builds upon core speaking skills and adds a finishing touch.

5. It enhances the overall transformation.

The cross-sell contributes to the bigger picture: How to become a confident, compelling speaker.

It addresses a common fear: “I know what to say, but am I going to say it effectively?”

It provides a complete solution without being essential to the main offer.

Now, by offering the monthly downsell option, Sarah makes sure that price doesn’t become a barrier while maintaining the premium positioning of her workshop recording.


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 7 -->

How To Write a Converting Cross-sell

You don’t need to reinvent the wheel here.

You can either have a very tempting offer and a short landing page elaborating on the offer and its benefits...

This will be the case if you’re selling easy-to-consume products, or just plain good opportunities.

Or you can follow the same outline as with the upsell.

This will be for harder-to-sell offers, such as supplements or bigger investments.

In any case, you want to present the complementary product as a natural, valuable addition to your main product.

It doesn’t feel pushy.

Focus on these key elements:

1. Lead with the benefit

Say it simply. How does it enhance your customer’s experience?

2. Create a logical connection

Explain why your cross-sell makes sense. What’s the meaningful link between your main product and your cross-sell?

3. Emphasize complementary value

Don’t sell your cross-sell as a standalone product. Focus on how it enhances the overall experience.

4. Use scarcity (tastefully)

If appropriate, add a bit of urgency to your Copy. Stay away from heavy-handed tactics.

5. Provide social proof

If possible, leverage testimonials, reviews, or data that validates your cross-sell. It always builds trust and helps overcome skepticism.


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 8 -->

The Most Common Cross-sell Pitfalls

Too many beginner Copywriters get so thrilled about their cross-sells that they make stupid mistakes that they could have easily prevented.

Here are 5 pitfalls to watch out for:

1. The “Weak Link Error”: Stay away from cross-sells with weak connections to your main offer.

It doesn’t have to make obvious sense, but it has to feel right.

2. The “Overwhelm Trap”: Don’t bombard your customers with too many options.

Keep it simple and make it easy to think through.

3. The “Bad Timing Blunder”: Avoid offering your cross-sell before your customer has fully committed to their initial purchase.

Build trust with your main product first!

4. The “Price Gap Gaffe”: Don’t offer a $1,000 cross-sell after a $47 purchase...

Keep it proportional - or make sure you provide strong justification.

5. The “Hard Sell Slip-up”: Keep the tone helpful and suggestive - not pushy.

Cross-sells should feel like helpful recommendations, not pressure tactics.

Remember you can always offer a downsell to your cross-sell - meaning a more accessible offer that everybody can say yes to.

Even if it won’t revolutionize your business, it will increase your AOV.

A good cross-sell has a 10% or more conversion rate, calculated from the original buyers.

Once again, this will depend on the product you’re cross selling.


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 9 -->

That’s All You Need to Start

Cross-selling

Your cross-sell could be a quick one-pager or a more elaborate sales page...

It doesn’t matter.

What matters is that your cross-sell:

1. Makes natural sense
2. Adds genuine value
3. Feels like an opportunity

Make sure it has a logical connection to your main offer. Make it feel like a natural enhancement, that it supports the overall transformation your customer wants.

Present it as a helpful suggestion - not a pushy sale.

Create that “I’d be silly not to grab this” feeling...

...And you’ll be well positioned to reach a 10%+ conversation rate - which is what you want to aim for.

But remember: The real win is when your customers thank you for recommending that additional product.

That's when you know you've nailed your cross-sell strategy.


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 10 -->

### Summary

A cross-sell is a complementary but different product that enhances the main purchase.

It has a horizontal connection - it supports your main product indirectly.

To make your customers grab it intuitively, focus on these psychological triggers:

- Appeal to convenience
- Value bundling
- Scarcity & exclusivity
- Problem completion
- Social proof

Look out for these pitfalls:

- Weak product connections
- Customer overwhelm
- Bad timing
- Disproportionate pricing
- Aggressive selling tactics


<!-- Source: Day 25 - Cross-sells.pdf; page 11 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Do you know why most Video Sales Letters fail?

Because they’re written to be read - not spoken.

They’ re structured to look good on paper - not to sound good in your ear.

And they have about as much excitement as your standard corporate accountant reading quarterly earnings reports...

But that won’t be your fate after tomorrow’s lesson...

Because you’ll be thinking both like a writer AND a producer
- just like the very best VSL Copywriters.

You'll discover the psychology behind those crucial first words - that are worth 80% of your entire script...!

...And I’ll show you my “Emotional Rollercoaster” technique to keep your viewer’s eyes glued to their screen...

I’ll reveal how to use psychological anchoring to make even premium-priced products feel like absolute bargains...

And how to naturally guide your viewer towards buying without pressuring them.

Tomorrow’s lesson will give you the tools to write VSL scripts that convert.

Don’t miss it.
