## Day 26: A Popular VSL Structure

<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 1 -->

A VSL is not meant to be read.

It is meant to be spoken.

Which means when you’re writing your script, you need to think as a writer but also as a producer.

Imagine the person who will be reading your script.

See it unfold in your mind.

When do you want this person to pause for a moment?

When do you want to slow down the speech?

When do you want to speed it up?

What do you want to emphasize?

Add all these oral cues to your script.

VSLs are faster paced than sales letters because it takes less time for our brain to process a video than to read a long text.

When it comes to crafting a VSL, you’re looking at 3 components:

1. Research
2. Structure
3. Delivery

You start with research.

Once you have what you need, you think of the structure of your VSL.

You write your script.

And you polish it up by working on the delivery.

Now let’s go over each step in detail.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 2 -->

How To Research Your VSL

You need to find these 4 things before you start writing:

1. Your opening
2. Your intrinsic benefit
3. Your stack of social proof
4. The deep benefits and pain points

Your Opening

It’s like your title in a sales letter: it’s supposed to hook your reader in.

This is the MOST IMPORTANT part of your VSL.

Use a pattern interrupt, make it as visual and impactful as possible...

..But never over-the-top.

Grab your view by the throat.

Use Chris Haddad’s PIG formula: the Punch In the Gut!

But keep it simple and believable. Don’t go for the dramatic Hollywood-style story.

And remember that a VSL is more forgiving than a sales letter.

You don’t have to make a promise in the first 10 seconds.

You can use images and the audio to grab your viewer’s attention.

With your opening, aim for novelty that can be linked to a benefit.

The best way to find it is to ask the founder. They usually have good stories to tell. Your only job is to find the winning one.

There are 3 openings that work well with VSLs and I will illustrate them using examples:

- A bold claim or promise:

I remember this VSL that did very well. It started with a guy showing the photograph of a random-looking man to the camera and pointing his finger at it.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 3 -->

He said something along the lines of...

“This guy made a million dollars last month...

...And I’m going to explain to you why and how he did that” The whole scene works as a pattern interrupt.

The picture of this random-looking guy, the claim “he made a million dollars last month” and the promise “I’m going to explain to you why and how he did that”.

- The climax of a story:

When I was writing a VSL for a weight-loss supplement, I interviewed the offer owner.

He told me his sister had always struggled with her weight. And during a family reunion, a crazy thing happened...

It was a scorching day of summer and everyone got together for a family barbecue. And while everybody was eating, drinking and enjoying themselves, his sister was fasting...

...And her limp body collapsed in front of everyone.

The hunger and the heat were too much for her to handle. This story makes for a fantastic opening.

Here’s the line I used in the VSL:

“When I saw her collapse on the ground, my heart dropped and I didn’t know what to do.”

It was at this moment the author knew he had to help his sister lose weight, so she can stop endangering her health with extreme measures like fasting.

Your opening line is the MOST IMPORTANT line of your VSL.

It can make it, or break it. And it’s why it needs to be as powerful as possible and as visual as possible.

You need to hit your reader with a BANG.

When opening with a story, your promise can come a little later.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 4 -->

You don’t have to use it in your first line if it seems out of place.

- A stunning fact:

When I was writing a VSL about an immune boosting supplement during COVID, I used a story from the Black Plague era in the Middle Ages.

I wrote it like a history documentary. And my first line was something like this:

“When I was researching the Black Plague, I thought I knew everything about it.

But it turns out that I didn’t...

There was a group of thugs that would rob corpses and never get sick. Because they had a secret...

And I’m about to tell you everything about it.”

As you can see, this is a different kind of copy. It doesn’t rely on claims but on a captivating story.

Now you might be wondering... How do you find these stories...? You find them by living more. Experiencing more.

Go out into the world and explore. Talk to strangers. Sit ata café and watch the people around you.

Reconnect with the curious eyes of your childhood.

Ask deeper questions, read books, watch movies. Especially the ones you wouldn’t normally watch.

Expand your horizons.

Feed your brain lots of different data points and let it do its magic.

Take long walks and let your thoughts wander.

Keep a notebook to capture your ideas; they usually come out of nowhere.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 5 -->

And trust your intuition.

On a more practical level, talk to the founders / offer owners. They usually have great stories or impressive case studies you can use.

Your Intrinsic Benefit

This is the most important benefit your product will provide your prospect with.

Make sure you have it crystal clear in your mind before you start writing your VSL.

Your opening should make a smooth and logical transition to your intrinsic benefit.

And make it pop up often in your script, like breadcrumbs on a trail so your viewer never loses sight of it.

Your Stack of Social Proof

These are video testimonials from happy customers.

Keep them short and sweet.

And do not hesitate to crop the best parts to keep it punchy.

Your Deep Benefits and Pain Points

Once you know the obvious and self-evident benefits of your product, it’s time to dig deeper.

Deep benefits are not what people expect straight away.

They’ re surprising.

And they’re the ones that connect with your reader at a deeper level.

If we go back to the VSL with the bold claim opening - the random-looking man who made a million dollars last month...

What are the deeper benefits of the obvious benefit (money) ?

For some it may be to retire their parents. And see the pride and gratitude in their eyes.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 6 -->

Or buy their mom a house. And watch her wrinkles soften for the first time as the feeling of safety permeates her life.

Some people want higher status and luxury. They want to hand over the keys to their supercar to the 5-star hotel valet, before being greeted at the reception by the staff who knows them by their first name.

No matter the deep benefits, you need to make them as real as possible.

Once you’ve found them, go for the deep pain points. Similarly, materialize them.

How would your viewer feel if he doesn’t take action today? If his back pain persists?

If he stays in his dead-end job?

How would his days look like a year from now?

Don’t go overboard with the drama - keep it realistic. How To Structure Your VSL
- Start with a hook.

- Proceed with your promise.

You can also promise a gift or a surprise if they watch until the end - just make sure you really have a gift..

- If possible, tease some proof.

Add testimonials and case studies - but keep it short and sweet.

- Tell your viewer what he could achieve: The dream outcome.

Use future pacing and make it subtle so you don’t trigger their radar.

- Add social proof to your dream outcome.

Again: short and sweet.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 7 -->

- Talk about the problem.

Now you’ve got your viewer exactly where you want him: pumped up with a positive start.

Now is the time to bring him back to reality by talking about his problem.

Your copy acts as an emotional rollercoaster.

Describe his current situation, tell him why it’s not desirable...

...And bring up an impending change: “Winter is coming”.

The world is changing, the rules are different, technology is accelerating...

...And if you’re not prepared, you will suffer X, Y or Z.

- Add more social proof. Invite your viewer to react.

Give him hope. Use the tone of...

“But fortunately...

...Science has found a new way”

- Introduce yourself.

Establish your credibility. Tell your discovery story.

- Go deep into the product with its obvious and deep benefits.

Back-up all your claims with proof. You want to avoid the tone of “trust me, bro”.

- Bring up the crossroads.

Narrow down the possibilities for your viewer by making it as natural as possible.

Stay true to your tone of voice too.

If it’s brotherly, give out a “tough love” impression.

“Brother, I love you but.. You’re messing up. You need to wake up.”


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 8 -->

And you give him a gentle slap.

If it’s authoritative, adopt the posture of an expert.

“Shit is coming and it’s about to get real. Here’s what you need to do to be prepared. And here’s what’ll happen if you don’t.”

- Remind the claim and benefits.

Support them with more proof.

- Present your offer.

Make the price seem fair.

Anchor up by showing your viewer how cheap your product really is compared to something else.

“Saffron costs up to $40,000 a kilo.. If you had to buy it separately instead of getting through [your supplement], it would cost you thousands of dollars each month.”

Then anchor down by comparing it to something very cheap.

“Your subscription is only $29 a month, less than a coffee a day.”

If you’re selling a digital product or a transformation (like coaching), you can talk about the time and effort it would take to gather all the info.

- Bring up the guarantee, or lack thereof.

- Add a Call-To-Action button.

- Detail the next steps.

Tell your viewer what’ll happen after he buys. Go into details.

- Repeat your claim one last time and add another CTA button.

This structure works especially well for services, but remember it’s not set in stone.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 9 -->

How To Deliver Your VSL

When you’re writing your VSL script, you want to maintain a highly conversational tone throughout.

You want to make your viewer feel like you’re speaking directly to him, rather than reading from a formal, scripted presentation.

And to achieve this, you’ve got to balance “spoken” and “written” language.

With “spoken”, I mean natural pauses, fillers, and colloquial expressions that people use in everyday speech.

“Like...”

“You know...”

“I mean...”

“Kind of...”

Etc...

These will add an “organic flow” to your writing. Read it aloud, record yourself and listen.

And make sure it matches the speaker. Use words and sentences he would be likely to use so it doesn’t sound nor look out of place.

With “written” language, I mean staying concise. Just because your script will be read doesn’t mean it has to sound like most people do.

You know what I’m talking about: paraphrasing, long-winded sentences and endless ramblings.

You want short and punchy writing, like a sales letter. Except you add flow, rhythm and tempo to it.

When it comes to pacing, you want a brisk - but not rushed - tempo.

Generally, VSLs are faster-paced than traditional sales letters because your viewer’s attention span is shorter when watching a video.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 10 -->

However, you want to avoid speaking so quickly that the viewer feels overwhelmed or unable to digest the information.

You should also give instructions to the speaker when it comes to cadence and rhythm. Tell him when to pause, slow down, speed up, what to emphasize, etc.

Incorporate strategic pauses and moments of emphasis to keep your viewer engaged.

Pause briefly after making an important point, slow down when introducing a critical benefit or proof. This gives your viewer enough time to process the information and heightens the impact of your message.

On a different note, your speaker can speak a tiny bit faster when getting into the mechanism of your solution. This shows competence and authority - mastery over the topic. Just make sure you strike the right balance.

In terms of vocal delivery, you want your VSL to be infused with energy and enthusiasm. Add variety to the tone, volume, and inflection to keep your viewer’s attention and convey a sense of passion and urgency about your offer.

Stay away from monotonous or flat delivery!

Voice is a powerful tool to build rapport, create a sense of trust and ultimately, persuade your viewer to take action.

Add all of these natural inflections and patterns into your script to make sure it sounds genuine and unrehearsed.

I like to use a color code and highlight the words and sentences for this.

While the script and vocal delivery are critical components of a successful VSL, you also want to consider the visual elements that can support your messaging.

Don’t hesitate to add on-screen graphics, images, or short videos to reinforce your key points and provide proof or demonstrations.

Use testimonials, product demonstrations, or data visualizations like graphs to back up your claims.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 11 -->

Lastly, you want to pay attention to your speaker’s own body language and facial expressions. Encourage him to maintain eye contact with the camera and use deliberate, natural gestures to maintain a sense of confidence and authority.

Subtle changes in facial expression - such as a raised eyebrow or a slight smile - can also help to emphasize important moments in your script.

But when it comes to visual elements of your VSL, consider how they work in harmony with your script. Never add visual elements for the sake of it.

Instead, make sure they serve a clear purpose and enhance your overall message and flow of the video.

This osmosis will create a more dynamic, immersive and persuasive experience and drastically increase the effectiveness of your VSL.

Remember: Your viewer should feel as if they're having a personal conversation with you - not listening to a polished, corporate presentation.

That’s All You Need

to Craft Killer VSLs

Focus on hooking your viewer with a powerful opening, establishing credibility and delivering a compelling offer.

But remember: It’s not just about your script. It’s also about HOW it’s presented.

Maintain a natural, conversational tone.

Pay attention to pacing, vocal delivery, and the strategic use of visual elements.

And always remember to make it feel like a personal conversation between your speaker and your viewer.

Test different angles and styles...

...And your VSL will be printing cash.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 12 -->

### Summary

A successful VSL has 3 components:

1. Research: Find a powerful opening, your intrinsic benefits, deep pain points, and social proof

2. Structure: Organize your script in a way that takes your viewer on an emotional rollercoaster, leading them to your offer and CTA

3. Delivery: Balance “written” and “spoken” language to achieve a natural, conversational tone. Pay attention to pacing, vocal delivery, and visual elements.

Infuse your script with natural pauses, fillers, and colloquial expressions to make your viewer feel like they're having a personal conversation.

Maintain a brisk but not rushed tempo, use strategic pauses and emphasis to keep your viewer engaged.

Deliver the presentation with energy and enthusiasm that feels genuine.

Add on-screen images, videos, graphs to support your message and back up your claims.

Pay attention to your speaker’s body language and facial expressions to enhance the overall impact of your VSL.


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 13 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

Tomorrow we’ll dive deep into one of the most overlooked - yet lucrative - opportunities in Copywriting...

.Advertorials.

While other Copywriters are fighting over the same email, sales letter and VSL projects, you’ll discover a hidden goldmine that very few know how to tap into.

Imagine being able to write Copy that doesn’t feel like Copy...

...Content that flies under your reader’s radav..

While STILL delivering a powerful sales message.

That’s the magic of advertorials, and tomorrow, I’ll be pulling back the curtain on exactly how it’s done.

You'll discover how to blend the persuasive power of sales letters and the eye-catching nature of ads with the engaging essence of blog posts...

...TO create hybrid content that converts like crazy.

I’ll show you 5 proven advertorials models and tell you which one to pick for your offer...

...As well as 12 battle-tested hooks that never fail to draw your reader in.

I’ll share with you my “Assembly Approach” to crafting advertorials so that you never stare at a blank page ever again.

You’ll also discover my “Breaking News” formula that creates such intense FOMO, your reader won’t resist taking action.

And among many other things, I’ll reveal why sometimes the “ugliest” photos can outperform professional stock images...

Or how to turn a simple list into a persuasive selling tool - without seeming “salesy”.

But the best part...?


<!-- Source: Day 26 - A Popular VSL Structure.pdf; page 14 -->

While everyone else is zigzagging between emails, traditional sales letters and social media posts...

...You'll have a unique skill that sets you apart and opens doors to high-paying clients who are desperately looking for Copywriters who understand the art of advertorials.

It may very well change the way you think about writing Copy, too.

.But I still have plenty of surprises left for you.

## Day 27: A Successful Advertorial Structure

<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 1 -->

An advertorial is a mix between an ad, a blog post, and a sales letter.

It usually acts as a transition between a clickable ad - most of the time a native ad - and a sales letter.

The goal is to inform and build up desire without triggering your reader’s sales pitch radar.

This means the advertorial should not directly sell.

Or at least do it in a toned down way.

Their main purpose is to educate and entertain.

With that in mind, you can focus on crafting highly valuable, informative - but also thrilling - advertorials.

Just remember: Try to keep the obvious hype to a minimum.

Working Advertorial Models

In your journey you will find several types of advertorials:

1. The “Personal Story” Advertorial

In this type of advertorial you tell your own story.

You usually go over a problem you had, and how you solved it thanks to a specific solution.

You aim to engage your reader on an emotional level and build trust through relatable experiences.

You want to position yourself as a relatable figure who has “been there” and can guide the reader to a similar successful outcome.

2. The “Breaking News” Advertorial

This type of advertorial takes on the form of a news article, or a sensational story.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 2 -->

You want to present information in a sensational, news-reporting style to create a sense of urgency or excitement around your solution.

It’s supposed to surprise readers and make them want to buy the product through fear of missing out or mimetism - the desire to imitate others.

3. The “Listicle” Advertorial

They’ re usually structured like a “List” article. They cover multiple reasons why your reader should choose your solution.

The reasons are presented in a concise, easy-to-scan format.

As great Copywriter and advertorial specialist Brandon Ham writes:

“Usually they cover 5-17 different reasons why someone would want a product. The reasons can be as short as 2 sentences to as long as 2+ paragraphs.”

4. The “Top 5” or Comparison Advertorial

These advertorials mimic “Top 5” videos or blog posts. THey’re similar to the Listicle.

They’ re about positioning your solution as the superior choice by directly comparing it to alternatives.

5. The “Customer Review” Advertorial

This is a personal review of the product, written from perspective of a happy customer.

It’s not about the story: It’s a subjective review of the real-world features and benefits of the product through an authentic, first-hand account.

Regardless of the specific model you’ll go for, you’ve got to remember that the goal of your advertorial is to educate, entertain, and build desire without overtly pushing a sales pitch.

The right approach will depend on your market, product, and target audience.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 3 -->

Popular Advertorial Hooks

You don’t create an advertorial from scratch...

You assemble it using a combination of winning angles.

The only way to know about them is to study winning advertorials and build your own swipe file.

You then analyze the advertorials and find one that’s closest to the one you’d like to build.

After that, follow the same structure and tweak it accordingly with your research.

The following hooks are carefully designed to pique curiosity, elicit an emotional response, and set the stage for your product or solution.

They’re all proven to work - but they’re not set in stone.

Feel free to adjust as needed, depending on your situation.

- This Weird [PRODUCT] Helps [TARGET] [BENEFIT]?

This hook plays on your reader's curiosity by highlighting an unexpected or unconventional product that offers a specific benefit to their target demographic.

The use of "weird" grabs attention and makes the reader want to learn more.

- Former [AUTHORITY FIGURE] Says 90% of Americans are Completely Unprepared for [SIGNIFICANT EVENT]

Here you’re trying to create a feeling of scarcity or importance your solution.

You leverage the credibility of a relevant authority figure and evoke a feeling of urgency around a pressing issue - that your product prevents, alleviates, or solves.

- The [EASY] Way To Banish [PROBLEM]... For Good!

You tap into your reader’s desire for convenience and “quick fixes” and promise an easy solution to a common problem.

“For good” suggest a permanent fix.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 4 -->

- [AUTHORITY FIGURE] Discover [NEW] [SOLUTION] That Helps You Finally [BENEFIT]

You combine the endorsement of a relevant authority figure with the promise of a novel solution that delivers a specific benefit.

The authority figure can be an institution (e.g. Harvard University), a celebrity, or an accredited person (e.g. Top US Cardiologist).

- Finally! A [EASY] [SOLUTION] That Provides [BENEFIT] Those [PROBLEMATIC EVENTS]

This hook emphasizes the ease of the solution while directly addressing your reader's problematic experiences and the specific benefits your solution can provide.

- [X] Things To Look For In A Good [SOLUTION]

Presented as a list, this hook positions your product as the best option by highlighting the key criteria your reader should consider when evaluating their options.

- Goodbye [PROBLEM]! Discover The World’s First [BIG] [SOLUTION] With Patented [MECHANISM] to [BENEFIT]!

This hook bids farewell to your reader's problem and introduces a groundbreaking, patent-protected solution that delivers a significant benefit.

- Who Else Wants A [BENEFICIAL AND EASY] [SOLUTION] That [BENEFIT] ?

The inclusive "who else" language taps into your reader's desire to be part of a group benefiting from your solution.

- [EVERYDAY PERSON] Turned [INITIAL INVESTMENT] Into [BIG NUMBER] In Under X Days - Here’s How...

The success story of a relatable, "everyday” person hooks your reader and sparks their curiosity about the featured solution.

- [DISEASE] improves [PHENOMENON]? - Top doctor shares his [FIELD] secret (Do This)

Combining a surprising medical claim with an authority figure's endorsement creates intrigue.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 5 -->

It presses your reader to uncover the secret.

- [AUTHORITY FIGURE] Exposes [FIELD] Loophole That Deposits [AMOUNT] Checks Directly Into Your Bank Account, Every Day...

This hook leverages an authority figure's credibility to reveal a little-known loophole that can generate significant, recurring income for the reader.

There are other popular and effective advertorial hooks out there and this is by no means an exhaustive list.

The best thing you can do next is to build your own swipefile, study them, and in time come up with your own successful hooks.

The Body Copy

Once you’ve found your main angle, you will need to finda solid body structure.

They will vary according to the angle you choose, but here’s the main frame that most advertorials follow:

- Angle
- Story
- Problem with other solutions
- Product introduction
- Benefits
- How it works / Mechanism
- Social Proof
- CTA (either price justification and link to order page, or link to the sales page / VSL to learn more)

Again, this structure is typical, but it doesn’t work 100% of the time.

This is a starting point.

Let’s go over each part in a little more detail.

- Angle

Start by clearly establishing the main angle that you introduced in your headline.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 6 -->

Whether it’s a personal story, a news-style narrative, ora comparative analysis...

Make it clear for your reader.

- Story

Continue with your angle here, depending on the framework you’ve chosen.

- Problem with other solutions

Expand on the problem your target audience is facing.

Acknowledge the existing solutions they may have tried, and gently highlight their shortcomings or limitations.

- Product introduction

Here you formally introduce your solution. Provide a clear, concise description.

- Benefits

Focus on your most important benefits that make your solution different from the rest.

- How it works / Mechanism

Diver deeper into how your solution works. The underlying mechanisms, technologies, or approchaes that set it apart.

Make it engaging and easy to understand.

- Social Proof

Add testimonials, case studies, or endorsements from happy users, industry experts, or authorities.

This builds trust and credibility like nothing else.

- cTA

Here you can either encourage your reader to buy your product right now - in which case you’d start by justifying and anchoring the price...

..Or you can invite them to learn more and click a button to reach your sales page or VSL.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 7 -->

Whether you follow this structure to a T or tweak it here and there, remember to make your body copy flow naturally from one section to the next.

Create a cohesive narrative that educates, entertains, and ultimately, persuades your reader.

Mistakes to Avoid

As much as it may seem like you simply need to follow these guidelines to make your advertorial work...

..There’s still plenty of opportunity to mess up.

Here are 3 pitfalls to watch out for:

1. Not testing enough.

You want to be A/B testing multiple versions of the same advertorial at once. This will let you gather data and optimize your results.

The most important things to test are Headlines and Leads / Angles - although you could be testing the whole structure too.

2. Being too vague.

Advertorials MUST be clear.

Your reader must know what it is about, and have a clear understanding of your solution - and a clear CTA.

You can lead with stories and entertainment for sure, but keep a clear direction and a single point of focus.

3. Not using images.

While text will always be the backbone of your advertorial, the proper use of visual elements never fails to engage your reader.

Well-formatted advertorials with images are easier to scan and make for a more comfortable read - which ultimately means more sales.

This is not opinion: This is the result of rigorous testing.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 8 -->

Now here’s a caveat.

In my experience, it’s always better to go with “uglier”, more realistic photos instead of pristine stock photos that scream “fake” and “corporate”.

The latter feels inauthentic and salesy.

I recommend you go with more natural and relatable images that complement your narrative.

Now It’s Your Turn

We've covered the essential elements of crafting effective, high-performing advertorials.

Remember the following key points:

- Understand the different working models of advertorials and choose the one best suited to your product and audience

- Draw inspiration from popular hook formulas to capture attention and build desire - but customize them to your situation

- Structure your body copy to guide your reader through a logical flow, from the initial hook to the final CTA

- Avoid common mistakes like not enough testing, vague messaging, and poor use of visuals

Now that you have a solid foundation, it’s time to study real-life examples and build your swipefile.

Pay attention to what works and don’t be afraid to test and iterate.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 9 -->

### Summary

Successful advertorials blend the informative nature of an article with the persuasive power of an advertisement and sales page.

The key is to educate and entertain your audience, while subtly building desire for your solution.

The most common advertorial structure are:

- The “Personal Story”
- The “Breaking News”
- The “Listicle”
- The “Top 5”
- The “Customer Review”

Take inspiration from proven hooks and experiment with them.

Draft different ones, modify them, or combine them as needed.

For your body copy, establish a clear angle, tell a compelling story, highlight the problem your solution solves, and showcase the benefits. Use social proof and a strong CTA.

Remember to test different versions, avoid vagueness, and leverage relevant visuals.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 10 -->

### Coming Up Tomorrow...

What if I told you that a Copywriter’s most potent persuasion tool has been wired into the human brain since the dawn of our species?

That this primal, instinctive form of communication can hijack your reader’s neurochemistry...

.And shape their desires, fears, and decisions in ways the common man would hardly believe?

I am - of course - talking about “Storytelling”...

wA.k.a. the one skill that separates “good” Copywriters from A-Listers.

And tomorrow, we’re not just peeking behind the curtain...

...We're tearing it down!

I’ll start by disclosing the unexpected reason stories are so powerful...

...Which has nothing to do with “what happens” - unlike what most people think...

You'll discover the “Dopamine-Pumping” techniques that keep your reader hooked and hungry for more...

..The “Bang-Your-Head-Against-The-Wall” method that transports them into the desperate experiences of your characters...

.And the sneaky way to make them feel like they've lived through your stories themselves - leaving an indelible mark on their psyche.

I’ll share a counterintuitive truth about “flaws” - the surprising reason why you want to embrace them and forget about “perfection”...

...As well as the subtle art of “Deliberately Leaving Things Out”...

SO you can write captivating - and relatable - stories.


<!-- Source: Day 27 - A Successful Advertorial Structure.pdf; page 11 -->

Forget cold, clinical facts.

The most persuasive Copy is built on a foundation of human experiences, emotions, and drama...

And nothing does it better than telling a good story.

Stories don’t just inform - they mesmerize.

If you want to wield that hypnotic power, you can’t afford to miss tomorrow’s lesson.

Consider yourself warned.

Your friend,

[Handwritten signature]

## Day 28: How To Tell Captivating Stories

<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 1 -->

[Portrait of Harriet Beecher Stowe]

This is American author Harriet Beecher Stowe.

When President Abraham Lincoln met her in 1862 at the start of the Civil War, he said...

“So this is the little lady who wrote the book that started this great watr..”

Her novel - Uncle Tom’s Cabin - was published in 1852 and was an instant hit, selling over 300,000 copies in the US and becoming the second best-selling book of the century - following the Bible.

The story is about Uncle Tom, a black slave seeking freedom while suffering cruelty and abuse from his owners.

Through vivid descriptions of hopelessness, shattered families, and crude violence, the book portrayed the brutal reality of slavery.

Most importantly, Stowe created complex characters with deep feelings, allowing readers to relate to their suffering.

So much so that the shifted public opinion about slavery...

...Contributing to the growing tensions between North and South that led to the Civil War and, ultimately, the abolishment of slavery.

That's how powerful a story can be.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 2 -->

Why Stories Work

Stories aren't just entertainment.

They're how humans are wired to learn, think, and make decisions - which makes them the most powerful tool for communication.

When someone reads or hears a story, something remarkable happens: Different areas in their brain start to light up and mirror the storyteller’s brain patterns.

This is called “brain coupling” or “neural synchronization”.

It means that when you read about someone running, your motor cortex activates. When you read about delicious food, your sensory cortex lights up. When you read about someone’s struggles, you engage your empathy center.

Your brain processes these imagined experiences like real events.

This is why you flinch when watching someone fall, or why your heart races during a car chase, or why you feel like an absolute boss when James Bond saves the world (or gets the girl) - even though you’re comfortably sitting on the couch.

Incredible things are happening at the molecular level: Your body starts to secrete a powerful blend of neurotransmitters that influence a whole lot of processes inside your body.

Dopamine, for instance, creates anticipation and keeps you engaged.

Oxytocin builds trust and empathy with the characters.

Endorphins reward you during victories and moments of triumph.

Adrenaline makes you temporarily immune to pain and fatigue.

And then there’s cortisol, which makes you alert and engaged.

All this means one thing: When you tell a story, you’re not just sharing information...

You're quite literally taking control of your reader’s brain.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 3 -->

There are simple ways to trigger these neurochemicals strategically, but it’s beyond the scope of today’s lesson.

For now, let’s build a solid foundation of what makes a good story - and why they work.

- Stories are your “natural operating system”.

Call them mental models, cognitive maps, schemas, metaphors, narratives or scripts...

They’ re how you make meaning of life. They help you make and justify decisions. They let you understand your place in the world in relation to others.

They’ re the most effective way of processing emotions and complex information.

- Stories are your “comfort zone”.

Their structure feels intuitively familiar: You don’t need to know about storytelling structures and techniques to appreciate them.

Writers haven’t “invented” storytelling frameworks...

..They’ve simply noticed the patterns and formalized them.

- Stories are your “bridge to others”.

They help you step into different perspectives.

They allow you to see the world differently and build a mutual understanding of it.

They build your identity and allow you to share it with others— influencing them in turn.

Stories have amazing constructive and destructive powers— fueling us to build civilizations and conquer nations...

...And this is why they’ve been humanity’s favorite method for sharing knowledge, values, and inspiring action for millennia.

Needless to say, marketers have noticed its powers...

...And realized early on that they aren't just for entertainment.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 4 -->

Stories In Copywriting

Stories are the best persuasion tool, plain and simple.

Here’s why:

- Stories make information memorable

A 1969 study tested Stanford students on their ability to memorize a list of 12 words.

Half the group studied the list for 2 minutes while the other half were asked to create a story containing all the words during the same time.

And when the students were tested on their recall...

..93% of those who crafted a story remembered all the words...

While only 13% of those who tried to memorize the list managed the same feat.

You can certainly see it for yourself: When was the last time you remembered a cold, hard fact...?

..compared with all the tiny, seemingly insignificant, stories you remember daily and are able to recall for no reason?

This happens for a very simple reason: Stories provide context that helps information stick.

The narrative structure of stories mirrors how our brains store information.

We don’t process data in isolation - we connect it and make it make sense in relation to others.

- Stories bypass resistance

When people are absorbed in a story, their mental barriers lower.

They become less likely to argue or counter-argue with your message.

Instead of staying on guard, they're drawn into your narrative, and become more open to new ideas and perspectives.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 5 -->

- Stories create emotional investment

Most importantly, stories create emotional investment.

They transform abstract concepts into concrete experiences, which helps people connect with brands and products.

And since “people buy with emotion”, this is exactly what you want to foster.

The 4 Pillars Of Stories

Every compelling story - whether it’s a novel or a sales letter - rests on 4 fundamental pillars that work together.

I’ll show you 2 models: The 4 P’s and the 4 C’s.

While the P’s focus on the key elements of stories, the 4 C’s focus on the transformational aspects. I find both to be right and complement each other nicely.

We’ll quickly go over each so that you have a better understanding of what makes a good story.

The 4 P’s are:

1. People
2. Place
3. Plot
4. Purpose

“People” are the heart of your stories: They are the characters your audience will connect with, root for, and see themselves in.

In your Copy, they might be customers sharing their experiences, experts offering guidance, or the founders and employees of the brand you’re writing about.

What matters is that they feel real and relatable, with genuine desires, fears, and dreams that your audience shares.

“Place” provides the context and environment where your story unfolds. It’s not just about physical location - it's about the situation, the circumstances, and the “world” your characters inhabit.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 6 -->

In your Copy, "Place" might be the frustrating situation your customer finds themselves in, the landscape your business navigates, or the future world your solution helps create.

A well-thought-out “Place” helps your reader understand the stakes and relate to the challenges, as there will be many subconscious elements to it.

“Plot” is the sequence of events that moves your story forward. It's not just WHAT happens, but HOW one event leads to another, creating a chain of cause and effect that feels both inevitable and surprising.

In your Copy, your “Plot” will often be the journey from problem to solution, the series of discoveries that led to how your solution came to be, or the transformation your customers experience.

“Purpose” is what gives your story meaning beyond mere entertainment. It's the deeper truth, the lesson, or the insight that makes your story worth telling and remembering.

In your Copy, your “Purpose” might be to show why your solution works when others fail, to demonstrate its real-world impact, or to inspire your prospects to solve their problem.

Now for the 4 C’s:

1. Character
2. Conflict
3. Change
4. Connection

“Character” is just like “People”: All stories revolve around them.

“Conflict” is the heart of any story. This is the problem or challenge that drives the narrative forward. It’s what stands in the way of your character, creating tension and raising the stakes.

There’s no reason for your reader to keep engaging with your story without meaningful conflict - it wouldn’t be exciting.

In your Copy, the “Conflict” might be the specific pain points, frustrations, or obstacles your reader faces — the reasons they need your solution in the first place.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 7 -->

“Change” is the journey of transformation that makes your story meaningful. This is more than “what happens” - it’s about the lessons learned along the way.

It’s the bridge between who your character was at the beginning and who they are becoming.

In your Copy, this could be the step-by-step process of how your characters overcame their challenges and reached their goals thanks to your solution.

“Connection” is the universal truth that resonates with your reader. This is where your story transcends the plot to touch on shared human experiences and emotions.

It's what makes your story not just interesting, but meaningful.

In your Copy, this could be tapping into common desires, fears, or aspirations that your reader immediately recognizes and relates to.

It doesn’t matter which “model” you use, as long as your stories include:

- Relatable characters or situations that your reader recognizes and relates to

- A clear and compelling progression of events

- Meaningful stakes that create emotional investment

- A satisfying resolution that delivers on your story's promise

For our purposes, it doesn’t have to be more complicated than this.

And remember: While each “P” or “C” is essential, they work together to create something greater than the sum of their parts.

A good story weaves them all together so seamlessly that your reader doesn’t see the structure - they just feel the impact.

Let’s use an example to illustrate.

Do you remember the “story-based email” from Sarah Angelier in “Day 22: Liftnotes That Sell”?


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 8 -->

Last month, one of my ‘Presentation Mastery’ students came to me with a challenge.

A Key Account Manager was stepping down, and the CEO was looking to promote someone in-house to lead the company's biggest client account.

The catch? Each candidate would have to give a presentation on their course of action...

..And the CEO would pick someone based solely on this.

"I have to nail this," he told me. "It's a career-defining moment."

My student was up against 4 colleagues for the role. All 4 were good public speakers with years of experience.

So here’s what we did: We spent the following 2 weeks applying the ‘Emotional Anchor’ technique from the course to polish his presentation.

And when he stepped up to speak...

The CEO didn't just give him the promotion. He added a 25% raise and a seat on the leadership team.

This story features a “Presentation Mastery student” (People / Character) who will be giving a presentation at his company (Place).

The former Key Account Manager is stepping down and the CEO offers 5 employees the chance to replace him. He will choose based solely on one presentation (Plot).

The student is worried about being up against 4 colleagues who are good speakers with years of experience (Conflict).

The Purpose shows how Sarah helped the student get the promotion by applying a specific technique from her public speaking course.

Not only did the student get the promotion, he got a 25% raise and a seat on the leadership team (Change) .

The Connection? Well, this story shows that anybody can overcome anything - no matter the odds - as long as they seek the right support with the right knowledge...


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 9 -->

...In that case, Sarah’s product and / or coaching!

I used this example to make a point: Your stories don’t have to be long to be impactful.

Because here’s what a lot of beginner Copywriters get wrong: They believe their story has to cover it all and they end up turning it into a novel that’s way too long...

...And misses the mark.

Next, I’ll show you how stories operate on 3 distinct levels - with each serving a different purpose and creating a different kind of impact.

The 3 Levels Of Stories

1. The 1st level is a direct report of experience.

This is the simplest form of storytelling: A straightforward account of what happened.

Think of it as the "just the facts" approach, like saying...

“Sarah Williams, a 34-year-old mother of two, struggled with weight loss for years.

She lost 18 kilos in 6 months through a combination of our supplement and moderate exercise.

Her blood pressure normalized, and she now maintains a healthy weight of 59 kilos.”

This approach works best when you need to establish credibility quickly, when your audience is analytical or already aware of the problem, or when the facts alone are compelling enough to drive your point home.

2. The 2nd level of stories is allegorical.

Allegorical storytelling uses metaphors and symbols to convey deeper meaning.

Think of it as “myths and legends” - except you don’t have to make it that epic. Simple metaphors do the job nicely.

Instead of just stating facts, you might say...


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 10 -->

Imagine your body as a house with a broken thermostat.

You can keep adjusting the temperature manually - dieting
- but as soon as you stop, it reverts to its faulty setting.

You can insulate the walls - restrict calories - or add radiators - exercise more - but these are just temporary fixes.

What you really need is to repair the thermostat itself - to fix the system that regulates your body's energy use...

..Which is exactly what our supplement does.

Instead of forcing temporary changes, it repairs your metabolic "thermostat", allowing your body to naturally maintain its ideal weight - just like a properly regulated house maintains its ideal temperature.”

This works well when you want to explain complex concepts or create an emotional connection while keeping it professional.

3. The 3rd level is emotional storytelling.

This is where we bring in rich detail and raw feeling.

“The dressing room mirror wasn't just reflecting my image anymore - it was screaming at me.

I stood there, surrounded by a pile of ‘medium-sized’ dresses that were all too small - fighting back tears while my daughter waited outside.

‘Mommy, can I see how pretty you look?’

Pretty?! I hadn't felt pretty since before the kids. Before the late-night stress eating. Before I started avoiding every camera and mirror in sight...”

This kind of story works best when you want a deep emotional connection, when your reader needs to feel understood, and when you have enough space to develop the full story.

Now here’s what plenty of beginner Copywriters get wrong...

...They always go for level-3 stories and end up writing a novel that nobody reads.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 11 -->

Not everyone wants to know about how Michelle feels since she’s put on those unwanted kilos - it’s not always appropriate.

In some cases, a plain and simple transformation - going from state A to state B - will be a better fit.

Just like sometimes, a brief metaphor will drive your point home better than a full blown story. Just like it can be a great addition to your sales letter.

You can - and should - use level 1 and level 2 stories in your Copy. In fact, the best Copy weaves all 3 levels of stories together.

You might start with an emotional hook (Level 3), support it with data (Level 1), and wrap it up with a memorable metaphor (Level 2).

This is why stories are the most potent form of persuasion.

They let you connect with your reader on multiple levels while delivering your message in a way that sticks.

Now, I’ll show you how to build a narrative arc that naturally compels your reader to read more - and buy your solution.

An Effective Story Structure

Every powerful story follows a natural arc that resonates with how humans process experiences and change.

Think of it as a journey that takes both your character AND your reader from one place - and state of being - to another.

NORMAL TRANSFORMATION WORLD aN ia DISRUPTION DISCOVERY = JOURNEY A


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 12 -->

You start in the “Normal World”, where you establish the starting situation. But you don’t just set the scene...

You show what’s at stake and make your reader relate to it.

You want them to recognize this world, whether it’s the frustration of trying to lose weight or the opportunity to stand out in the workplace and advance their career.

Then comes the “Disruption”...

...That moment when the status quo becomes unbearable or when an opportunity becomes too important to ignore.

This is where you introduce the problem or challenge in a way that matters.

Maybe it's the moment someone sees themselves in the mirror and doesn't recognize who they've become, or when an employee realizes public speaking is the one skill that can get them a promotion.

The “Journey” follows - and this is where good writing truly shines. Show the struggle, the failed attempts, the moments of doubt.

Don't be afraid to make your character fail - these setbacks build anticipation and make your solution more powerful.

Each moment of self-doubt, each attempt, and each failure make the need for a solution more urgent and meaningful.

Next comes the “Discovery” - the moment when everything changes.

This is where you present your solution - but it's crucial to show WHY it works rather than just stating that it does.

Help your reader understand the mechanism behind the success - whether it's a revolutionary approach to weight loss or a little-known presentation technique.

Finally, we reach the “Transformation”.

This final stage isn’t just about showing results - it’s about demonstrating meaningful change AND inspiring action.

Your reader should see not just what changed...


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 13 -->

..But how it felt, what it meant, and what became possible as a result.

That’s how you make the “Transformation” part meaningful - and impactful.

As you can see, your story structure doesn’t have to be more complicated than this.

Again - this is where most beginner Copywriters go wrong...

They desperately try to cram all 17 steps of Campbell’s “Hero’s Journey” into their stories...

...And end up surprised when they see that nobody reads them.

My brother in Christ: We’re selling stuff with our stories here - we’re not going for a Pulitzer prize.

Here’s how you can adapt this framework to your Copy, so that it maintains its appeal while being more practical...

ORDINARY TRANSFORMATION WORLD aN fe CALL TO CHANGE GUIDE XS RESISTANCE ay

Your story starts in the “Ordinary World” - but you’re not just describing it...

You’ re reflecting your reader’s current situation in a way they intuitively recognize.

Show the daily frustrations, the recurring problems, the deeper cost of staying stuck.

This isn’t so much about dramatic pain points as it is about the quiet - and often intimate - desperation of knowing something needs to change.

The “Call To Change” comes next...


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 14 -->

But it doesn’t have to be as dramatic as a pipe-smoking wizard knocking on your door asking you to go on an adventure...

Most of the time, it’s going to be a small moment that carries a lot of meaning in retrospect. See it as the final straw that makes the status quo unbearable.

Things like not fitting into your favorite pair of jeans, or flopping a presentation at work and feeling judged by your colleagues.

The key is to make it feel like things need to change NOW.

Then comes the “Resistance” - which is where too many beginner Copywriters fall short, because they entirely skip over their reader’s doubts and fears.

You want to do the opposite: Acknowledge them. Respect them.

Show that you understand why they might hesitate, why they've put off making changes, why they might not trust that this time will be different.

You’ll build trust and credibility like nothing else when you do it right.

Once you’ve done so, you introduce the “Guide” - which will sometimes be you, sometimes another person, sometimes your solution...

Now, this is key: You’re not the hero...

Your reader is!

You're the wise mentor who helps the hero succeed - which implies that you’re not boasting...

..You’ re providing understanding and insight. Show that you’ve been there too and how you’ve helped others through the same issues.

You finish with the “Transformation” - where you paint a picture of success that goes beyond simple results.

Again, show your reader what becomes possible. Help them see themselves in this new reality. Make them feel the confidence and enjoy the benefits once they’re living the life they want.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 15 -->

How To Craft Likable Characters

It’s no coincidence if the first P and C of the 4 Pillars are “People” and “Character”...

..Because storytelling is all about the human experience.

Don’t believe me?

Let’s see...

Take The Lord Of The Rings, for instance. The quest to Mordor is thrilling for sure - but it’s Sam’s loyalty and Frodo’s internal struggles with temptation that really drive the story forward.

In the movie Titanic, while the sinking is spectacular, the story resonates because Rose is breaking free from societal constraints and learning to live life on her own terms.

And beyond the animal kingdom drama in The Lion King, the movie really is about Simba overcoming guilt and self-doubt to accept his responsibilities and embrace his true identity.

“What happens” in stories is often interesting...

The setting, the atmosphere, are certainly immersive...

But the real reason these stories are successful is because of how the characters react to these events - and who they become as a result.

That’s the secret to powerful storytelling: Characters that feel real and relatable.

This is true whether you're writing a novel or a sales letter, because at the heart of every buying decision is a human being with hopes, fears, and dreams.

When it comes to your characters, focus on 3 things:

1. Make them human
2. Make them specific
3. Make them universal

When I say make them human, I mean make them like you and I...

..Flawed.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 16 -->

Show their weaknesses, their struggles, and allow them to be vulnerable.

Perfect characters are neither believable nor interesting.

It's the imperfections that make them real and relatable - which is why Deadpool is more popular than Superman.

When you’re writing customer stories, don't be afraid to show their initial skepticism, their past failures, and their moments of doubt.

Next, you want to make them specific. What I mean by that is you want to add concrete details that bring them to life.

Instead of saying someone "wanted to lose weight”, show how they dreaded seeing themselves in the mirror.

Give them clear motivations that go beyond the surface level.

They're not trying to lose weight for the sake of it - they're trying to make their children proud, or trying to regain the confidence they've lost.

Lastly, you want to make your characters universal - and this is what many beginner Copywriters miss.

While your characters should be specific, their core desires and struggles should connect to universal human experiences.

We all know what it feels like to want to be better, to fear failure, to hope for change.

When you tap into these universal emotions through specific characters that go through key moments, you create stories that resonate deeply with a wide audience.

Now, for clarification: You want the dreams and fears to be universal, but you want the ways you show them to be specific.

For instance, Sarah Angelier wouldn’t tell you about how she’s helped her 6 year-old son talk in front of the class without blushing...

Just like you wouldn’t use the example of the woman in the dressing-room when your reader is a 40 year-old man trying to get rid of his dad bod.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 17 -->

Different stories need different types of characters, and understanding common archetypes can help you choose the right approach for your message.

There are many, but I’ll give you the 3 most common ones you'll be using in your Copy.

1. The “Everyday Hero”

He’s the most versatile archetype in Copywriting. This is your regular person facing common challenges, someone your reader can easily relate to.

They're not superhuman - they're just like your reader, but they've found a way to overcome the same challenges your reader faces.

Their transformation feels achievable because they started from a similar place.

2. The “Expert Guide”

This character plays a different but equally important role.

He brings credibility and authority to your story, as long as you make him approachable and trustworthy.

The best Expert Guides don't just share their knowledge - they show genuine understanding and empathy for those they help.

They're not perfect - they've often learned through their own struggles and mistakes.

3. The “Success Story”

This character serves as proof of possibility and inspiration.

But be careful - if your Success Story feels too perfect, it’ll feel questionable and you’ll lose your reader’s trust.

The most effective Success Stories are still connected to the struggles and doubts they overcame. They remember what it felt like to be where your reader is now, and they share not just their triumph but their journey.

Now, crafting likable characters is not the only way to create emotional engagement...


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 18 -->

How To Engage The Emotions

You’ve probably heard of the saying “Show, don’t tell”...

..But what does it mean and why does every writer and their mother yap about it?

When you’re telling someone that "she was nervous", you’re giving them a piece of information.

But when you’re writing...

"Her hands trembled as she opened the envelope, the paper crinkling beneath her fingers."

You’ re creating an experience.

Similarly, instead of writing “our product is effective”, show it:

"Within three days, Henry was sleeping through the night for the first time in 4 years."

Or like Sarah Angelier did in her Cart Abandonment Sequence:

“Just last month, one of my students used the ‘Emotional Anchor’ technique from this course to nail a career-defining presentation and land a major promotion.

He was up against more experienced colleagues, but this strategy helped him command the room and seal the deal.”

Or...

“Our latest graduates have been seeing interesting results, like Emily who landed a coveted TEDx talk after applying the ‘Inverse Authority’ principle she learned.”

It can be as simple as this - because emotion in storytelling isn't about being dramatic or sentimental...

It's about creating authentic experiences that resonate with your reader.

And it all starts with sensory details.

Every time you describe how something looks, feels, sounds, smells, or tastes, you're not just adding details..


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 19 -->

You're activating different areas of your reader's brain, making the experience more vivid and memorable.

So don’t hesitate to bring in the senses every time you describe something - and go beyond mere depiction.

"As Daniel walked up to the front of the conference room, his heart was pounding and his breath was shallow. His audience was watching him so closely he could feel the weight of their expectant gaze.

A faint aroma of fresh ink and crisp paper hit him, as he realized his hands were clenching the freshly printed programs he was supposed to be handing out...”

Ain’t that more powerful than simply writing “Daniel was stressed out during his last presentation”?

Beyond the senses, tension is another element you can play on.

This doesn't always mean conflict or drama - it’s about building anticipation, raising the stakes, and delaying the resolution.

And it can be as simple as raising questions in your reader's mind that need to be answered:

"The moment had finally arrived. After weeks of painstaking preparation, endless rehearsals, and countless sleepless nights, you were about to pitch your start-up in front of investors. This was your chance to finally raise enough money to get it off the ground - and bring your product to life.

But what if you froze? What if you forgot your lines? What if they didn't connect with your story the way you hoped?"

When you carefully control the release of information, you keep your reader engaged and invested in your story.

But the words you use are not the only thing that engages the emotions of your reader...

The pace of your writing matters too.

Short, punchy sentences create urgency. Longer, flowing sentences can build anticipation or create a sense of peace:


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 20 -->

“You begin slowly, your voice a calm, measured cadence that immediately commands your audience's attention.

Pause.

Breathe.

Then, a sudden shift in tempo.

Your words tumble forth with a sense of urgency that jolts them to the edge of their seats.

You know your topic.

They can tell.

And just when they start to get used to it, you transition again - this time to a more conversational rhythm, weaving personal anecdotes and relatable examples before you drive home your central message.”

By varying your sentence length and structure, you create a rhythm that pulls readers along and reinforces the emotional tone of your story.

Now that you understand the backbone of good stories, I’ll show you the different types of stories you’ll be using in your Copy.

Stories In Copywriting

You tell 3 kinds of stories in Copy:

1. Your personal story
2. The story of someone else 3.A fictional story

As long as it relates to our offer and as long as it feels close to your reader, all 3 are effective.

Your personal story carries a lot of weight - because you’ve lived it. You know exactly how it felt to face that challenge, experience that breakthrough, or discover that solution.

This firsthand knowledge allows you to speak with both authority and vulnerability. It lets you paint vivid pictures with authentic details that only someone who's been there could know.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 21 -->

Here’s how Sarah Angelier’s own story could look like:

“I remember standing in that hotel bathroom, gripping the sink so hard my knuckles turned white. In 10 minutes, I was supposed to deliver a keynote speech to 500 industry leaders. My heart was racing, my mouth was dry, and I was fighting the urge to run back to my room.

I'd been here before— dozens of times. Each speaking engagement was the same story: sleepless nights, panic attacks, and a performance that was merely ‘good enough’ because my fear held me back.

The worst part? I was as legit as they come. As a senior marketing exec with 16 years of experience, I knew the ins-and-outs of the trade - AND the industry...

..But when it came to speaking in public, I was the shadow of my former self - all because I was too scared to let my authentic ‘me’ shine through.

That day in the bathroom, I made a decision. I couldn't live like this anymore. I needed to understand why, despite my expertise, I was overwhelmed with fear.

This led me to study not just presentation techniques, but the psychology behind anxiety. Over the next year, I developed a system that transformed my relationship with public speaking.

Today, I help others break free from the same fears that once held me back. When I step on stage now, I feel energy - not panic. And nothing brings me more joy than helping others discover this same freedom.”

When you tell how you found your way through, your reader isn't just reading a story - he’s seeing a path forward for himself.

These stories work brilliantly in welcome sequences and sales letters - and “About Me” sections.

Stories about other people work the same way. It just takes a little more research on your part.

You can use the origin story of the founder, client success stories, or testimonials.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 22 -->

They all serve the same purpose: Show your audience what’s possible through a different lens.

Here’s another example Sarah Angelier could use...

"’T'm not a natural speaker.’ That's what Daniel - a brilliant software engineer - told me in our first session. His company had given him 3 months to improve his presentation skills or he'd lose his shot at leading the new AI division - his dream role.

Daniel could explain complex algorithms beautifully one-on-one, but put him in front of a group, and he'd freeze. His words would tangle, his hands would shake, and all that expertise seemed to evaporate.

We started with what I call ‘micro-moments’— 30-second explanations to tiny, friendly audiences. Daniel thought it was too basic at first, but he trusted the process. We gradually expanded these moments, building his confidence through deliberate practice and mental preparation techniques.

Six weeks later, Daniel presented his AI proposal to the board. ‘For the first time,’ he told me afterwards, ‘I wasn't thinking about my fear. I was focused on my message.’ He got the position, and more importantly, he discovered a confidence he never knew he had.”

These stories work because they add new perspectives through which your reader can connect with your message.

They work great in sales letters and emails - whether a launch sequence or a liftnote - as well as social media posts.

Now in some cases, the most powerful story isn't one that actually happened, but one that shows a deeper truth.

Fictional stories give you the freedom to perfectly illustrate your point.

You can combine elements from multiple real experiences, address sensitive topics with a gentle touch, or simplify complex concepts into digestible stories.

Check out the next page to see how.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 23 -->

“Picture 2 entrepreneurs preparing for the most important pitch of their careers.

Louise spends weeks memorizing every word of her presentation. She rehearses in front of her mirror until she can recite it perfectly. On pitch day, she delivers every word exactly as planned - until an investor asks an unexpected question...

Her rhythm breaks as her memorized script suddenly becomes useless. The rest of the presentation turns into a blur of anxiety and improvised responses.

Meanwhile, Antoine takes a different approach. Instead of memorizing a script, he masters his core message. He practices sharing his ideas in various ways, embracing the natural flow of conversation. He turns his presentation into a story - his story.

During his pitch, he connects with his audience, adapts to their responses, and handles questions with genuine excitement. His presentation isn't ‘picture perfect’, but it feels authentic - and investors loved it.

The difference isn't natural talent. It's understanding that great presentations aren't about perfection - they’re about connecting with your audience...”

Maybe Louise doesn’t exist. But her character is heavily inspired from Claire - who gave a heavily-rehearsed pitch that felt robotic at one event Sarah attended...

The same goes for Antoine. You may have been inspired by another speaker at another event, who was very laid back...

.And you transmuted them into 2 fictional characters that you can compare side-by-side to make your point.

I’m not telling you to lie, because your stories must be rooted in reality.. But you can definitely “bend” the truth.

Your stories must feel believable - which is why you show your characters’ flaws and don’t go overly dramatic with the events.

Fictional stories work well in all kinds of educational content - sales pages, emails, even social media posts.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 24 -->

In any case, remember that all your stories must show a clear transformation, include specific, relatable details, and naturally support your solution.

There are other ways to think of your stories:

1. Origin stories
2. Transformation stories
3. Vision stories

Origin stories can be incredibly powerful— when done well.

The key is in finding the deeper emotional truth at the heart of your origin, whether it’s a personal struggle, a moment of inspiration, or the desire to help a loved one.

Don’t just say what happened— focus on why it mattered and how it connects to your reader’s needs and desires.

Transformation stories are the bread-and-butter of stories in Copy. They show the journey from problem to solution - from the “Normal World” to a “New World” where change has happened.

The best transformation stories don't just focus on the end result - they dive deep into the turning point, when change becomes possible.

They’ re great at showing how your solution helps your reader.

Last, you’ve got “vision stories”, where you paint a picture of what could be.

You start by acknowledging your reader’s current reality before you build a bridge to a better future. You want to make this future both aspirational AND achievable, so that your reader feels inspired but not overwhelmed.

Now, great storytelling isn’t just about how you tell your stories...

...It’s also about WHEN you tell them.

You might use a story as your hook to draw your reader in with an intriguing situation they recognize from their own lives...

.And make them think “That’s exactly how I feel!” or “That’s exactly what I want!”


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 25 -->

Or it might be the foundation of your solution - explaining the hidden “why” behind what you’re offering.

Or it might simply serve as supporting evidence - backing up your claims through examples that are relatable.

As long as you make your reader the hero of your story, it’ll feel like more than words on a page.

Whether you're sharing your story, someone else’s, or a fictional one, your goal is to illuminate the path forward and invite your reader to take the first step.

And as always, remember that your stories are there to persuade your reader to buy your solution - not to distract from it.

Before we wrap this lesson up, I’d like to leave you with a handful of more practical tips on how to add stories to your Copy.

How Stories Fit In Your Copy

It all depends on the context - your niche, your target audience, your customer’s journey - and the format.

In sales letters, stories have multiple uses.

You can use one in your hook to capture attention - and go on with a “story lead” - through a situation your reader will intuitively recognize.

Stories can nicely illustrate the mechanism of the problem, false solutions, and your solution.

And of course, you can bring a boring testimonial section to life through entertaining success stories.

Emails are great opportunities to tell great stories.

Origin and vision stories are a good fit for welcome sequences, for instance. Presale sequences can build anticipation through a narrative arc that spans multiple emails. And liftnotes can convert like crazy with relevant stories.

It’s a little different for social media.


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 26 -->

You can tell your story in one single post, or spread it across multiple ones. The key is in finding the core idea of your story and telling it concisely - but powerfully.

In general, your reader will pay less attention to your content because the opportunities for distraction are almost endless - so you want to make it as impactful as possible.

With long-form content, you have more room to develop full story arcs with rich detail and lots of emotion. Take your time to build connection, create tension, and show why the transformation matters.

With short-form content, you want to focus on one key moment and one emotion - like a single significant scene. Only keep the details that carry a lot of weight.

And remember that the best stories are all about the human experience - whether you’re writing a long story for your sales letter or a “micro-story” for an ad.

Focus on the why behind the transformation and the emotional journey.

Telling great stories is an art form that takes years to master...

..But when you apply the key elements I’ve shared with you today, you’ll be well on your way to tell captivating stories that are not just read, but felt and remembered...

...And that sell!


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 27 -->

### Summary

The 4 Pillars of Stories:

- People - Relatable, complex characters
- Place - The context and environment
- Plot - The sequence of events
- Purpose - The deeper meaning and insight

Or:

- Characters - Make them human and flawed
- Conflict - Challenges that create tension
- Change - Transformative journeys
- Connection - Universal human emotions

3 Levels of Storytelling:

1. Direct - Straightforward facts / report of experiences
2. Allegorical - Metaphors and symbolism
3. Emotional - Vivid sensory details

Engaging Storytelling Techniques:

- "Show, don't tell" - Use specific descriptions, engage the senses
- Build tension and anticipation
- Leverage writing rhythm

Story Types for Copywriting:

- Personal stories
- Stories of others
- Fictional stories

Integrating stories effectively:

- Use as hooks, explanations, or evidence
- Adapt length / level of detail based on format
- Focus on human experience and emotion


<!-- Source: Day 28 - How To Tell Captivating Stories.pdf; page 28 -->

Yourself.

The intangible things that make the difference between starving artists and successful entrepreneurs.

How to leverage impostor syndrome to drive you towards mastery...

The “Conqueror Mindset” that’ll make you irresistible to your clients...

How to project the “Casual Nonchalance” that screams authority
